How to use this course

  1. One day per sitting. Read the lesson top to bottom. Each one is self-contained but builds on the day before.
  2. Play with every widget. The widgets are small, real computations running in your browser, not pictures. Drag the sliders until the idea clicks.
  3. Take the quiz, then mark the day done. Progress is saved on this device only. Nothing is sent anywhere.
  4. Open the sources. The small chips after sentences (like this one Vaswani 2017) link to the original paper, blog, or documentation. When you finish the course, read the originals in the Paper Bible.

Week 1 · Day 1

What is a language model?

After today you can explain, with a real example, what a language model computes, why it generates one token at a time, and how a next-word guesser ended up knowing facts and following instructions.

About 90 min · No prerequisites

Today's big idea

A language model is a function that takes a sequence of text and returns a probability for every possible next piece of text. Everything else, from chatbots to code assistants, is built by calling that function over and over. Getting those probabilities right on the whole internet turns out to require grammar, facts, and a surprising amount of reasoning.

1. A machine that guesses the next word

Finish this sentence: "The cat sat on the ___." You probably thought "mat". Maybe "floor" or "sofa". You almost certainly did not think "photosynthesis". Your head just did what a language model does: it took the words so far and produced a ranked list of what could come next.

A language model is a program that assigns a probability to the next piece of text given the text so far. The "pieces" are called tokens: chunks of text, usually a word or part of a word, that the model treats as its atoms (Day 2 is entirely about them). So the model's job, stated precisely, is to compute

p(next token | all the tokens so far)

for every token in its vocabulary at once. If the vocabulary has 50,000 tokens, the model outputs 50,000 numbers that are all between 0 and 1 and add up to 1. That list of numbers is a probability distribution: a complete statement of how likely each candidate is. For "The cat sat on the", a good model might say "mat" 0.31, "floor" 0.12, "couch" 0.08, and tiny values for the other 49,997 tokens.

Notice what the model is not: a database of sentences, or a rulebook of grammar. It is a scoring function. You can score any sentence with it by multiplying the probability of each token given the ones before it. "The cat sat on the mat" gets a high score; "Mat the on sat cat the" gets a score close to zero. That multiplication is the chain rule of probability, and it is the reason a next-token predictor is also a judge of whole texts. Jurafsky & Martin 2025

Analogy

Think of your phone keyboard's autocomplete. It shows three suggested words above the keys, ranked by how likely each one is to come next. A large language model is autocomplete on steroids: the same idea, but it considers thousands of words of context instead of two, it ranks tens of thousands of candidates instead of three, and it has read far more text than any human could. The difference in scale is so large that it becomes a difference in kind.

2. Autoregressive generation: one token at a time

A model that only predicts one next token seems useless. How does it write a whole paragraph? By looping. The model predicts a distribution for the next token, one token is chosen from that distribution, it is glued onto the end of the text, and the longer text is fed back in. Then the model predicts again. This loop is called autoregressive generation: "auto" because the model's own output becomes its next input, "regressive" because it is predicting from the past.

text = "The cat sat on the"
for step in range(20):
    probs = model(text)          # one number per vocabulary token
    next_token = sample(probs)   # pick one; see Day 7 for how
    text = text + next_token
print(text)

Two consequences follow. First, the model never plans the whole answer in advance: every token is committed before the next is considered, which is why a model that starts a sentence badly often cannot recover. Second, how you choose from the distribution matters. Always picking the single most likely token (called greedy decoding) produces bland, repetitive text. Picking at random in proportion to the probabilities (sampling) gives livelier text and also lets the model say different things each time you ask. Day 7 covers temperature and the other knobs that control this choice.

3. Build one yourself: the n-gram model

The simplest language model that actually works is the n-gram model: it predicts the next word by counting what followed the previous n−1 words in a training text. A bigram model (n=2) looks at one previous word. If, in the training text, "sat" was followed by "on" 40 times and by "down" 10 times, the bigram model says p(on | sat) = 0.8 and p(down | sat) = 0.2. That is the whole model: a table of counts turned into fractions. Jurafsky & Martin 2025

Try it: the widget below trains an n-gram model in your browser. Pick n, press generate, and watch the probability table it consults at each step.

Notice three things. With n=1 the output is word salad. With n=2 or 3 it starts to sound like the training text. With a large n it copies long passages verbatim, because most 5-word contexts occurred exactly once, so there is only one "choice". Every language model faces this tension: short context is too dumb, long context is too sparse.

N-gram models powered speech recognition and machine translation for three decades, from Frederick Jelinek's IBM group in the 1970s and 1980s through the 2000s, and they introduced perplexity, the standard measure of how surprised a model is by test text, which we still use today. Wikipedia 2026 Their fatal weakness is that counting cannot generalise. If the training text contains "the cat sat" but never "the dog sat", a bigram model thinks "dog sat" is impossible, even though cats and dogs are obviously similar. Fixing that weakness is the story of the next forty years.

Going deeper (optional): perplexity

Perplexity is the exponential of the average negative log-probability the model assigns to each real token in a test text. A perplexity of 20 means that, on average, the model was as uncertain as if it were choosing uniformly among 20 tokens. Lower is better. Shannon's 1951 experiment can be read as measuring the perplexity of humans: his subjects' guesses imply that English carries roughly 0.6 to 1.3 bits of information per letter, so a perfect letter predictor would be choosing among only about 2 equally likely letters on average. Wikipedia 2026 Day 3 connects this number to the loss function used to train every modern model.

4. Why guessing words forces real knowledge: the compression view

Here is the question that makes the whole field make sense. If an LLM is "just" predicting the next token, why can it answer questions, translate, and write working code?

Because predicting well is hard. Consider the text "The capital of France is". To put high probability on "Paris", the model must, in some form, store that fact. Consider "17 × 23 =". To predict "391" instead of a random number, it must do the multiplication, or memorise the table, or learn a procedure that gets it right. Consider a detective novel whose last page reads "and so the murderer was". To predict the name, the model has to track the clues across the whole book. Each of these is a next-token prediction, and each one can only be done well by a system that has learned the underlying structure: grammar, facts, arithmetic, and reasoning. The training objective never mentions any of those things. They are forced into the model because they are the cheapest way to lower the prediction error on a huge, varied corpus.

The clean way to say this is through compression. Claude Shannon showed in 1948 that the best possible compression rate of a source equals its entropy, which depends on how predictable it is, and that a good predictor is therefore a good compressor. Shannon 1948 A language model gives you exactly such a predictor: using it with an arithmetic coder, a text can be stored in about −log₂ p(text) bits, so the better the model's probabilities, the smaller the file. DeepMind researchers made this literal in 2023, using Chinchilla 70B as a compressor: trained only on text, it beat gzip on text and even compressed ImageNet image patches to 43.4% of their raw size (PNG: 58.5%) and LibriSpeech audio to 16.4% (FLAC: 30.3%), although the "compressor" itself is enormous. Delétang 2023 Compression and understanding are two views of the same thing: to compress a physics textbook well you must, in some sense, know physics.

One caution. "In some form" is doing a lot of work above: nobody fully knows how facts and procedures are represented inside the network, and "learned because it helps prediction" does not mean reliable. It only has to be good enough to lower the average loss, and section 7 shows the failure modes.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a game: I read you a book one word at a time, and before each word you guess what it will be. At first you guess badly. After a million books you get good, because you learned how sentences work. But to guess well in a book about dinosaurs, you also had to learn dinosaur facts. To guess the ending of a maths problem, you had to learn maths. A language model is a computer playing this exact game on nearly everything ever written. It was never told to learn facts or maths. It learned them because that is the only way to win the guessing game.

5. A short history, with dates

Try it: the timeline below runs from 1948 to today. Tap a milestone to see what changed. The paragraphs after it give the story in order.

Notice the gaps. Statistical language modelling was a mature field for decades; the recent explosion came from three things landing together: a better architecture (2017), a recipe for scaling it (2018–2020), and a way to make it conversational (2022).

1948 and 1951: Shannon. Claude Shannon founded information theory and, in the same paper, built the first language models: letter and word n-grams of English, which he used to generate increasingly English-looking gibberish. Shannon 1948 In 1951 he ran the guessing game on people: subjects predicted the next letter of a text, and from their guesses he estimated how much information each English letter carries. Shannon 1951 This is the origin of the idea that predicting text measures understanding of it.

1970s–2000s: n-grams. Jelinek's group at IBM turned word counting into working speech recognisers; n-grams with clever smoothing stayed state of the art for decades. Wikipedia 2026

2003: the first neural language model. Yoshua Bengio and colleagues replaced the count table with a small neural network that first maps each word to a learned vector (the origin of embeddings, which Day 2 explains) and then predicts the next word from the vectors of the previous ones. Because similar words get similar vectors, "the dog sat" borrows strength from "the cat sat". Bengio 2003 In 2010 Tomáš Mikolov showed that a recurrent neural network (RNN), which reads text one word at a time while carrying a memory vector, beat n-grams clearly. Mikolov 2010

2013: word2vec. Mikolov's team at Google released a fast way to train word vectors on billions of words; the famous result was that vector arithmetic captured meaning, as in king − man + woman ≈ queen. Mikolov 2013

2014–2015: sequence to sequence and attention. Using LSTM networks, a kind of RNN invented in 1997 that keeps a memory over long spans, Wikipedia 2026 Sutskever, Vinyals and Le showed that one network could read an English sentence into a vector and a second could write the French translation out of it. Sutskever 2014 Bahdanau, Cho and Bengio then let the decoder look back at every input word while writing each output word, choosing where to look by a learned attention weight. Bahdanau 2014 That trick is the seed of everything after.

2017: the Transformer. Vaswani and colleagues at Google removed the recurrence entirely and built a network out of attention alone, which made training parallel across all positions and therefore much faster on GPUs. Vaswani 2017 Day 4 and Day 5 build this architecture piece by piece; see also the Transformer paper review.

2018–2020: GPT-1, GPT-2, GPT-3. OpenAI applied the Transformer to plain next-token prediction and scaled it. GPT-1 (June 2018) showed that pretraining on books and then fine-tuning beat task-specific models; OpenAI 2018 at 117M parameters it is the smallest row in the GPT-2 paper's size table. Radford 2019 GPT-2 (February 2019, 1.5B parameters, trained on 8 million web pages) could do tasks like summarisation and translation with no fine-tuning at all, just by being prompted. OpenAI 2019 GPT-3 (May 2020, 175B parameters) made in-context learning the headline: give it a few examples in the prompt and it performs the task, with no change to its weights. Brown 2020 The GPT-1, GPT-2 and GPT-3 reviews cover each in depth.

November 2022: ChatGPT. OpenAI wrapped a GPT-3.5 model, tuned to follow instructions and hold a dialogue, in a free chat interface. OpenAI 2022 The underlying model was not a leap; the packaging was, and it brought language models to the general public.

2023–2026 in one paragraph. GPT-4 (March 2023) added image input and much stronger reasoning. OpenAI 2023 Meta's LLaMA (February 2023) started a wave of open-weights models that anyone can download and run. Touvron 2023 OpenAI's o1 (September 2024) introduced reasoning models that write a long hidden chain of thought before answering, trained with reinforcement learning, OpenAI 2024 and DeepSeek-R1 (January 2025) showed the same recipe working in an open model. DeepSeek 2025 Since then the frontier has been about longer contexts, tool use and agents, multimodality, and cheaper inference. Day 14 tells this part of the story properly, with the 2025–2026 models.

6. What "large" means: parameters, data, compute

Three numbers describe the size of a language model.

Parameters are the learned numbers inside the network, the weights that Day 3 explains how to train. More parameters means more capacity to store patterns. Training data is measured in tokens; a token is roughly three quarters of an English word. Compute is the total number of arithmetic operations used in training, measured in floating-point operations (FLOPs). A useful rule of thumb is that training costs about 6 FLOPs per parameter per token, so compute ≈ 6 × parameters × tokens. Kaplan 2020 Day 6 turns this into a calculator.

ModelYearParametersTraining tokensTraining compute
GPT-2 (largest)20191.5B8M web pages, about 40 GB of textnot reported
GPT-32020175B300B3.14 × 10²³ FLOPs
Llama 3 405B2024405B15.6T3.8 × 10²⁵ FLOPs, on up to 16,000 H100 GPUs

Sources: GPT-2 from OpenAI's paper, Radford 2019 GPT-3 from its paper's appendix, Brown 2020 Llama 3 from Meta's report. Meta 2024 Check the rule of thumb on Llama 3: 6 × 405 × 10⁹ × 15.6 × 10¹² ≈ 3.8 × 10²⁵. It matches.

To make the sizes concrete: each parameter is usually stored as a 16-bit number, which is 2 bytes. So a 7B-parameter model takes 14 GB of memory just to load, a 70B model takes 140 GB, and a 405B model takes 810 GB, which is why big models are split across many GPUs and why Day 10 is about shrinking them. "Large" is not a fixed threshold; GPT-2 was called large in 2019 and would be called tiny today. As of October 2026 the biggest frontier models are believed to be in the trillion-parameter range, but labs no longer publish exact sizes, so treat any such figure as an estimate.

7. What an LLM can and cannot do

Because the model is a next-token predictor trained on a snapshot of text, its strengths and weaknesses follow a pattern. Learn the pattern and most surprising behaviours stop being surprising.

What it is good at. Anything that looks like continuing a document in a style it has seen a lot of: drafting and editing prose, translating between common languages, writing and explaining code, summarising, answering questions whose answers appear in many places in the training data, and performing a task after seeing a few examples in the prompt. Brown 2020 With long chains of thought, modern models also solve multi-step maths and reasoning problems that older models could not; Day 9 covers how and how far.

What it is bad at, and why.

  • Making things up. A model fills in the most plausible next tokens whether or not it has the underlying fact, so a confident-sounding wrong citation or a wrong date is a natural output, not a glitch. This is called hallucination. OpenAI's 2025 analysis argues that it is reinforced by training and evaluation that rewards guessing over saying "I don't know". OpenAI 2025 Day 12 shows how retrieval reduces it.
  • Anything after its training cutoff. The weights are frozen after training. Without a search tool, the model does not know this morning's news, and it may not know that it does not know.
  • Counting letters, precise arithmetic, spelling backwards. The model sees tokens, not letters, so "how many r's in strawberry" is a question about something it cannot directly see. Day 2 explains this one fully.
  • Acting on the world. On its own, the model only emits text. Running code, browsing, sending email and checking its own work require tools wrapped around it, which is the subject of Day 12.
  • Being consistent. Sampling means two runs of the same prompt can disagree, and a small change in wording can change the answer.

Common misunderstandings

  • "It just looks up sentences it has seen." It cannot: a 70B-parameter model is a few hundred gigabytes, far smaller than its training text, and it produces sentences that never appeared anywhere. It stores compressed patterns, not a copy of the data. Pieces of memorised text do exist, but they are the exception.
  • "It understands nothing, it is only statistics." The compression argument cuts the other way: the only way to get these statistics right is to model the structure that generated the text. Whether that counts as understanding is a real debate, but "only statistics" is not a description of what the model has learned.
  • "It knows when it is wrong." The model's probabilities describe how plausible text is, not how true it is. Confidence in tone and confidence in fact are different things.
  • "A bigger model is always better." Size helps, but data, training recipe and post-training matter as much; Day 6 makes this quantitative.

8. Base model versus assistant

The thing that comes out of next-token training on raw internet text is called a base model (or pretrained model). Ask it a question and it will continue the document in whatever way the internet usually does: it might answer, but it might also produce five more questions in the same style, because a list of questions is a common kind of web page. An assistant model such as ChatGPT is a base model that has been further trained, with examples written by people and with feedback on its answers, so that it treats your text as an instruction and responds helpfully. OpenAI's 2022 InstructGPT paper describes this recipe, and it showed that a 1.3B-parameter model tuned this way was preferred by human raters over the 175B base GPT-3. Ouyang 2022 The whole of Day 8 and the InstructGPT review are about this second stage; for today, just remember that "predicts the next token" describes both models, and that the assistant has simply been taught that the most likely next tokens after a question are a good answer.

Check yourself

Q1. What does a language model output when given the text "The cat sat on the"?

Answer 3. The model returns a full probability distribution over the vocabulary; a single next word is only obtained afterwards by sampling or taking the maximum. It does not retrieve training sentences and it does not produce a parse.

Q2. Why is generation called "autoregressive"?

Answer 2. The model's output becomes its next input, one token at a time. It does not plan the full answer in advance and it does not revise earlier tokens.

Q3. A bigram model trained on a text where "the dog" never appears but "the cat" appears often will assign to "dog" after "the"…

Answer 4. An n-gram model is a count table; unseen pairs get zero unless smoothing is added. Knowing that cats and dogs are similar requires learned word vectors, which is what Bengio's 2003 neural model introduced.

Q4. Which statement best captures the "compression view" of language modelling?

Answer 1. Prediction and compression are mathematically linked (Shannon), and Delétang et al. showed a 70B language model literally compressing text better than gzip. The model does not store a zipped copy of the data.

Q5. Llama 3 405B was trained on about 15.6 trillion tokens. Using the 6 × parameters × tokens rule, its training compute is roughly…

Answer 3. 6 × 4.05 × 10¹¹ × 1.56 × 10¹³ ≈ 3.8 × 10²⁵, which is exactly the figure Meta reports. The other options are off by factors of at least 100,000.

Q6. What is the main difference between a base model and an assistant model?

Answer 2. Instruction tuning and feedback change what the model considers the likely continuation of a question; the mechanism is unchanged. InstructGPT showed a 1.3B tuned model beating a 175B base model in human preference, so size is not the difference.

Recap: five things to remember

  1. A language model computes p(next token | context) for every token in its vocabulary; that distribution is its entire output.
  2. Text is generated autoregressively: predict, pick one token, append, repeat. How you pick (greedy or sampling) changes the result.
  3. N-gram models are count tables; they cannot generalise to unseen word combinations, which is why neural models with word vectors replaced them.
  4. Predicting diverse text well forces the model to learn grammar, facts and procedures, because that is the cheapest way to reduce prediction error. Prediction and compression are the same problem.
  5. "Large" means parameters, tokens and FLOPs together (GPT-2 1.5B, GPT-3 175B on 300B tokens, Llama 3 405B on 15.6T tokens); a base model becomes an assistant through extra training, not through size.

References

  1. Speech and Language Processing, Chapter 3: N-gram Language Models — Jurafsky & Martin, Stanford, 3rd edition draft, 2025. The clearest written introduction to n-grams, smoothing and perplexity; read sections 3.1–3.3 and 3.7.
  2. A Mathematical Theory of Communication — Shannon, Bell System Technical Journal, 1948. Section 3 shows the first n-gram text generators; the rest founded information theory.
  3. Prediction and Entropy of Printed English — Shannon, Bell System Technical Journal, 1951. The human letter-guessing experiment; the first measurement of how predictable English is.
  4. A Neural Probabilistic Language Model — Bengio, Ducharme, Vincent & Jauvin, JMLR, 2003. The first neural language model and the origin of learned word embeddings.
  5. Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space — Mikolov et al., Google, 2013. The word2vec paper; short and readable.
  6. Neural Machine Translation by Jointly Learning to Align and Translate — Bahdanau, Cho & Bengio, ICLR 2015. Where attention was born; Figure 3's alignment maps are worth the visit alone.
  7. Attention Is All You Need — Vaswani et al., Google, NeurIPS 2017. The Transformer. You will read it properly on Day 4.
  8. Language Models are Few-Shot Learners — Brown et al., OpenAI, NeurIPS 2020. GPT-3; Table 2.1 and Appendix D have the size and compute numbers quoted above.
  9. The Llama 3 Herd of Models — Meta AI, 2024. The most detailed public account of how a frontier-scale model was trained; sections 1 and 3 give the numbers in today's table.
  10. Language Modeling Is Compression — Delétang et al., Google DeepMind, 2023. Makes the compression view literal with experiments on text, images and audio.
  11. Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback — Ouyang et al., OpenAI, 2022. The InstructGPT paper: how a base model becomes an assistant.
  12. Why language models hallucinate — OpenAI, 2025. A short, honest explanation of why models guess instead of abstaining, with numbers.
  13. Deep Dive into LLMs like ChatGPT — Andrej Karpathy, YouTube, 2025. A 3.5-hour tour of the whole pipeline for a general audience; the first hour pairs well with today.
  14. Language model — Wikipedia. A compact history from Jelinek's n-grams to today, with pointers to the primary papers.

Next: Day 2 — Tokens and embeddings

Week 1 · Day 2

Tokens and embeddings

After today you can explain how text becomes numbers a network can use, run the byte-pair-encoding algorithm by hand, and predict which strange LLM failures are really tokenizer failures.

About 90 min · Builds on Day 1

Today's big idea

A model never sees letters or words. A tokenizer chops text into pieces from a fixed vocabulary, each piece gets an integer id, and each id selects one row of a big table of learned vectors. Those vectors, the embeddings, are the only thing the network ever reads. Almost everything odd about how models count, spell and price text comes from this one design choice.

1. Characters, words, or something in between?

A model predicts the next token; the first question is what a token should be. Three natural answers, each with a cost.

Characters. Treat every letter, digit and punctuation mark as a token. The vocabulary is tiny and nothing is ever "unknown". But "The cat sat on the mat" becomes 22 tokens, each carrying almost no meaning, and sequences get long, which is expensive: Day 4 shows that attention cost grows with the square of the length.

Words. Split on spaces and punctuation. Each token is meaningful and sequences are short. But the vocabulary explodes: "love", "loves", "loved", "loving" are separate entries that share nothing, and any word outside the list (a new name, a typo, "unbelievableness") becomes a single unknown token with all its meaning lost.

Subwords. Keep common words whole and break rare words into familiar pieces. "unbelievableness" might become "un", "believ", "able", "ness": four tokens that each occur in thousands of other words. Nothing is ever unknown and sequences stay short. Every large language model since 2018 uses some version of this. Hugging Face 2026

UnitVocabulary sizeTokens in "The cat sat on the mat"Unknown words?Problem
Charactersabout 100–30022neverlong sequences, no meaning per token
Wordshundreds of thousands+6oftenhuge table, no sharing between word forms
Subwords30,000–250,0006 (common words stay whole)neverodd splits of numbers, names, non-English text

Analogy

A subword vocabulary is like a box of LEGO. A few big, specially shaped bricks are there because you use them constantly (a whole wheel, a door); everything else you build from small standard bricks. The box holds a fixed number of brick types, chosen so that the things people build most often need the fewest bricks. Byte-pair encoding decides which special bricks earn a place in the box.

2. Byte-pair encoding, step by step

Byte-pair encoding (BPE) is the algorithm most modern tokenizers use to choose their vocabulary. Philip Gage proposed it in 1994 as a compression trick: repeatedly find the most common pair of adjacent bytes and replace it with a new single byte. Wikipedia 2026 Sennrich, Haddow and Birch adapted it for translation in 2016: run the same merging on the characters of a training text, stop after a chosen number of merges, and the resulting pieces are your vocabulary. Their motivation was rare words, which earlier systems handled with a dictionary; subwords improved translation into German and Russian by over a BLEU point. Sennrich 2016

The training algorithm is short enough to state in full:

vocab = all single characters in the training text
split every word into its characters
repeat K times:
    count every adjacent pair of symbols across the text
    (a, b) = the most frequent pair
    add the new symbol "ab" to vocab
    replace every occurrence of a followed by b with "ab"
# vocab now has (characters + K) entries; the K merge rules are saved in order

Here is a worked example on a toy text containing the words low ×5, lower ×2, newest ×6, widest ×3 and yes ×2. Start by splitting into characters: l o w, l o w e r, n e w e s t, w i d e s t, y e s.

MergeMost frequent pairCountRunner-upText after the merge
1e + s → es11 (6 + 3 + 2)s + t (9)l o w · l o w e r · n e w es t · w i d es t · y es
2es + t → est9l + o (7)l o w · l o w e r · n e w est · w i d est · y es
3l + o → lo7o + w (7, a tie)lo w · lo w e r · n e w est · w i d est · y es
4lo + w → low7n + e (6)low · low e r · n e w est · w i d est · y es

After four merges the vocabulary is the 11 original characters plus es, est, lo, low. The suffix "est" was discovered before any whole word, purely because it was frequent. Merge 3 was a tie; implementations break ties by a fixed rule, which is one reason two tokenizers trained on the same data are rarely identical. To tokenize new text you do not recount anything: split into characters and apply the saved merges in order. "lowest" becomes low est; the never-seen "slowest" becomes s low est.

Try it: the widget below runs this algorithm on a sample text, one merge per click. Then type your own sentence and see how a trained tokenizer splits it.

Notice that early merges are common letter pairs and suffixes, later merges are whole common words, and a rare or misspelled word falls apart into several pieces. Try a long number and a word in another language.

3. The tokenizer family

BPE has several relatives. They all produce subwords; they differ in how they pick them and in what they treat as the starting alphabet.

WordPiece is Google's variant, used by BERT. It merges the pair whose combined frequency is highest relative to the frequencies of its parts, so it prefers pairs that occur together more often than chance. Schuster 2012 Devlin 2018 You can recognise its output by the ## prefix on pieces that continue a word, as in play ##ing.

Unigram goes the other way: start with a huge candidate vocabulary and repeatedly remove the pieces whose loss hurts a probabilistic model of the text the least, until the target size is reached. Because several segmentations of a word remain possible, it can sample different splits during training, which acts as a regulariser. Kudo 2018 SentencePiece is a library that runs either BPE or Unigram directly on raw text, treating the space as an ordinary symbol (shown as ▁), which makes it work for languages such as Japanese and Chinese that do not use spaces between words. Kudo & Richardson 2018 T5 used it with Unigram; the first Llama models used it with BPE. Touvron 2023

Byte-level BPE solves the last "unknown" problem. If your base alphabet is characters, a text containing a character you never saw (an emoji, a rare Chinese character) still breaks the tokenizer. GPT-2 instead starts from the 256 possible byte values of UTF-8 text, so every string in every language can be encoded, and merges build up from bytes. Radford 2019 Almost all current models use this approach. tiktoken is OpenAI's open-source, fast implementation of byte-level BPE; its vocabularies are what GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 use, and Meta built Llama 3's tokenizer on top of one of them. OpenAI 2022

4. Vocabulary sizes, and why they are what they are

GPT-2's vocabulary has 50,257 entries: 256 byte tokens, 50,000 learned merges, and one special end-of-text token. Hugging Face 2026 GPT-4's has about 100,000. Wikipedia 2026 Llama 3's has 128K: 100K tokens from tiktoken plus 28K chosen to serve non-English languages better. The payoff is measured in characters per token: on English text Llama 2's tokenizer averaged 3.17 and Llama 3's averages 3.94, so the same document costs about 20% fewer tokens. Meta 2024

Why not a million entries, then, so every word is one token? Three reasons. The embedding table grows with the vocabulary (section 7 has the arithmetic), and so does the output layer that scores every token at every step. Rare tokens are seen rarely during training, so their vectors are poorly learned; a word that appears 50 times is better represented by two pieces that each appear a million times. And a token that is too specific cannot share: "unhappiness" as one token learns nothing from "happiness". The sweet spot has drifted upward as models and datasets grew, from about 30,000 in BERT's era to 100,000–250,000 today; it is a trade-off, not a law.

5. Tokens are the unit of cost and of memory

Once you know that models think in tokens, several practical facts fall into place.

Price. Commercial APIs charge per million tokens, separately for input and output, because compute is proportional to token count. For English prose, a token is 3–4 characters or about three quarters of a word, so a 1,000-word document is about 1,300 tokens; code and numbers cost more.

Context window. The maximum amount of text a model can consider at once is also counted in tokens. GPT-2 could see 1,024 tokens, about 750 words. Hugging Face 2026 Llama 3 models support 128K tokens, roughly a 300-page book. Meta 2024 Day 7 explains why a long context costs memory at inference time.

Language inequality. Because vocabularies are learned mostly from English text, the same sentence in another language usually splits into more tokens. Petrov and colleagues found the same content costing up to 15 times as many tokens in some languages as in English: higher prices, less room in the context window and slower responses for those users. Petrov 2023 This is exactly why Llama 3 spent 28K of its vocabulary on non-English pieces.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine you have to send messages using only stickers from a sticker book, and you pay one coin per sticker. The book has a sticker for "the", one for "cat", one for "ing", and single-letter stickers for emergencies. Common English words cost one coin. A rare word, a long number or a word in another language must be spelled out with lots of little stickers, so it costs more. The computer never sees your letters, only which stickers you used, which is why it can be bad at counting letters: it would have to remember what is printed on each sticker.

6. When the tokenizer bites: strawberries, arithmetic, and spelling

Many famous LLM blunders are not reasoning failures. They are the model being asked about something it literally cannot see.

Counting letters. In 2024 "how many r's are in strawberry?" became a standard test that many models failed, answering two instead of three. The word is one or two tokens, so the model never receives an "r"; it must recall from training how that token is spelled, and spelling knowledge is only indirectly present in web text. Karpathy's tokenizer lecture walks through this and the related failures (reversing a string, counting words). Karpathy 2024 A model allowed to spell the word out first usually gets it right, because then each letter is its own token.

Arithmetic. Numbers are split by whatever the merge table happened to learn, so "1234567" might become "123", "456", "7" while "7654321" becomes "765", "43", "21", and adding numbers whose digit groups do not line up is harder than it should be. Singh and Strouse showed that inserting commas so that digits are grouped from the right raised GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 arithmetic accuracy markedly, with no change to the models. Singh 2024 Several newer tokenizers split numbers into single digits or groups of three for this reason.

Typos and formats. A misspelled word falls apart into unusual pieces the model has rarely seen together, so a single typo can derail an easy question; larger models suffer less, and training with randomised segmentations helps. Chai 2024 Case matters too: "Hello", "hello" and " hello" (with a leading space) are three different tokens. The space is part of the token, which is why a prompt ending in a trailing space often produces worse output: the model expected the space glued to the next word.

The lesson: when a task depends on characters or digits, let the model spell things out or hand the task to a tool (Day 12); and before blaming the model's intelligence, check what the tokenizer did.

7. Embeddings: a token becomes a vector

The tokenizer's output is a list of integers, for example [464, 3797, 3332] for "The cat sat" in GPT-2's vocabulary. Integers like these are labels, not quantities; token 3797 is not "more" than token 464. A neural network needs numbers it can add and multiply, so the first layer of every language model converts each id into a vector of real numbers called its embedding.

The mechanism is a lookup table. The embedding matrix has one row per vocabulary entry and one column per dimension of the vector; looking up token id 3797 means taking row 3797. GPT-2 small has 50,257 rows of 768 numbers each, Hugging Face 2026 which is 50,257 × 768 ≈ 38.6 million parameters, almost a third of the model's 124 million. Llama 3 405B has 128K rows of 16,384 numbers, Meta 2024 about 2.1 billion parameters, which is only half a percent of the model, which shows how the balance shifts as models grow.

Nobody writes these numbers down. They start random and are learned like everything else (Day 3). Because the only pressure on them is to help predict the next token, tokens that appear in similar contexts end up with similar vectors: the "dog sat / cat sat" sharing that n-grams could not do.

8. Measuring similarity: the dot product

Once words are vectors, "similar" has a precise meaning. The dot product of two vectors multiplies them element by element and adds the results. For a = (2, 1, 0) and b = (1, 2, 0), a·b = 2×1 + 1×2 + 0×0 = 4. The dot product is large when the vectors point in the same direction and have large entries, zero when they are perpendicular, and negative when they point in opposite directions. Cosine similarity removes the effect of length by dividing by both vectors' lengths: cos(a, b) = a·b / (|a| |b|). Here |a| = |b| = √5, so cos = 4/5 = 0.8; for c = (0, 0, 3), cos(a, c) = 0. Cosine runs from 1 (same direction) through 0 (unrelated) to −1 (opposite). Jurafsky & Martin 2025

Keep the dot product in mind: it is the most important operation in this course. Attention (Day 4) is a dot product between a query and a key, and section 11 shows that the model's final prediction is one too.

9. word2vec and the analogy trick, with its limits

In 2013 Mikolov and colleagues trained simple word vectors on billions of words and found that directions in the space corresponded to relations between words. The most quoted example: take the vector for "king", subtract "man", add "woman", and the nearest word is "queen". The same works for "Paris − France + Italy ≈ Rome" and for grammar, as in "walking − walk + swim ≈ swimming". Mikolov 2013 Mikolov 2013 Nobody programmed this; it emerged from predicting nearby words. Stanford's GloVe (2014) obtained similar vectors from global co-occurrence counts. Pennington 2014

Try it: the map below shows word vectors squashed into two dimensions. Hover or tap a word to see its cosine similarity to the others, and build an analogy of the king − man + woman kind.

Notice that related words cluster, that the "male → female" offset is roughly parallel for several pairs, and that some analogies fail. This is a 2-D picture of a space with hundreds of dimensions, so on-screen distances are approximate.

The demo is often oversold. The standard evaluation quietly excludes the three input words from the answer; allow them, and the nearest vector to king − man + woman is usually "king" itself. Linzen 2016 Nissim and colleagues showed that several widely quoted "biased" analogies, such as man : doctor :: woman : nurse, are artefacts of that rule: with the honest query the answer is "doctor". Nissim 2020 Word vectors do encode real regularities, and real biases from their training text, but one arithmetic trick is not proof of either.

10. Static versus contextual embeddings

word2vec gives one vector per word, forever. That is a problem for "bank": the river bank and the savings bank share one blurry average vector. A static embedding cannot know which sense is meant. In 2018 ELMo showed that vectors taken from inside a language model that has read the whole sentence give every occurrence of "bank" a different vector reflecting its context, and feeding those contextual embeddings to other systems improved the state of the art on all six tasks tried. Peters 2018 BERT made the same point later that year and became the standard way to get such vectors. Devlin 2018

In a Transformer both kinds coexist. The embedding matrix of section 7 is static: row 3797 is the same numbers whatever the sentence. Each layer then mixes information between positions (Day 4), so by the top of the network the vector at "bank" has absorbed "river" or "savings" from its neighbours. "The embedding of a sentence" used for search (Day 12) means such a contextual vector, not a row of the lookup table.

11. The other end: the unembedding, and weight tying

Day 1 said the model outputs a probability for every vocabulary token. Where do 50,257 numbers come from, when the network's last layer produces one vector of 768? From another matrix, the unembedding or LM head, with one row per vocabulary token. The final vector is dot-producted with every row, giving 50,257 scores called logits; a softmax turns them into probabilities by exponentiating each and dividing by the total, so everything sums to 1. The model's prediction is literally "which vocabulary vector points most in the same direction as my current state?"

The unembedding has the same shape as the embedding matrix, so why not use one matrix for both? That is weight tying. Press and Wolf showed in 2016 that tying the input and output embeddings lowers perplexity and, for translation models, cuts the parameter count by more than half. Press 2016 Inan 2016 The original Transformer shares one matrix between its embedding layers and the pre-softmax projection, citing Press and Wolf, Vaswani 2017 and GPT-2 does the same, which is why its 124M parameters hold one 38.6M table rather than two. Very large models often untie them again, since the table is a tiny fraction of their size; Day 5 counts the whole block's parameters.

Going deeper (optional)

Vocabulary size also shapes compute at the output: scoring 128K tokens against a 16,384-dimensional state costs about 2 billion multiply-adds per generated token, before the softmax, which for small models is a large share of the per-token cost. Pre-tokenization matters too: GPT-2 first splits text with a regular expression so that merges never cross a space or join letters to punctuation, and the exact rules (are digits split, how many spaces may group together) are part of why code and numbers tokenize differently across models.

Common misunderstandings

  • "Tokens are words." Common English words are usually one token, but rare words, names, numbers, code and most non-English text are several, and a leading space or a capital letter changes the token. Count tokens with a tokenizer, not with a word counter.
  • "The model reads letters, so it can spell." It reads token ids. Spelling, letter counting and reversing strings are hard because the letters are hidden inside the tokens.
  • "Embeddings are chosen by linguists." They are learned from scratch from the prediction objective; no one assigns meanings to dimensions.
  • "king − man + woman = queen proves the model understands gender." It shows a consistent direction exists in the space, under an evaluation rule that excludes the input words. Evidence of regularity, not understanding.
  • "A bigger vocabulary is always better." It shortens sequences but enlarges the tables and starves rare tokens of training examples; it is a trade-off.

Check yourself

Q1. In BPE training, what is merged at each step?

Answer 2. BPE is pure frequency counting over adjacent pairs; it knows nothing about dictionaries or embeddings, which is why it happily learns suffixes like "est" before whole words.

Q2. GPT-2's vocabulary size is 50,257. Where does that number come from?

Answer 4. Byte-level BPE starts from the 256 possible byte values, adds the chosen number of merges, and reserves a special token. The count has nothing to do with the number of words in the data.

Q3. Why do many models miscount the r's in "strawberry"?

Answer 1. It is a tokenization problem: letters are hidden inside tokens. Spelling the word out letter by letter, so that each letter is its own token, usually fixes it, which rules out the other explanations.

Q4. Vectors a = (1, 0) and b = (3, 0). Their cosine similarity is…

Answer 3. Cosine divides the dot product (3) by the product of the lengths (1 × 3), giving 1. The dot product alone would be 3; cosine deliberately ignores length.

Q5. What is the difference between a static and a contextual embedding?

Answer 2. The lookup-table row is static; the vectors inside a Transformer after attention has mixed in the neighbours are contextual (ELMo, BERT). Both are learned, and dimensionality is not what distinguishes them.

Q6. What does weight tying mean in a language model?

Answer 4. The two matrices have the same shape (vocabulary × dimension), so they can be shared; Press and Wolf showed it helps perplexity and saves parameters. Sharing across layers is a different idea.

Recap: five things to remember

  1. Subword tokenization keeps common words whole and splits rare ones; BPE builds the vocabulary by repeatedly merging the most frequent adjacent pair, and byte-level BPE guarantees nothing is ever unknown.
  2. Vocabulary sizes (GPT-2 50,257; GPT-4 about 100K; Llama 3 128K) are a trade-off between shorter sequences and bigger, sparser tables.
  3. Tokens are the unit of price, speed and context length; non-English text can cost many times more tokens for the same content.
  4. Letter counting, digit arithmetic and typo sensitivity are mostly tokenizer effects: the model sees ids, not characters.
  5. An embedding is a learned row of a lookup table; similarity is a dot product or cosine; contextual vectors from inside the network beat static ones; the output layer is the same lookup run backwards, often with the very same matrix.

References

  1. Neural Machine Translation of Rare Words with Subword Units — Sennrich, Haddow & Birch, ACL 2016. The paper that brought BPE to NLP; it includes a short Python implementation of the merge loop.
  2. Summary of the tokenizers — Hugging Face Transformers documentation. BPE, WordPiece, Unigram and SentencePiece compared on one toy corpus, with the GPT-2 vocabulary arithmetic.
  3. Byte-Pair Encoding tokenization — Hugging Face LLM Course, chapter 6. Train a BPE tokenizer yourself in a few dozen lines of Python.
  4. Let's build the GPT Tokenizer — Andrej Karpathy, YouTube, 2024. Builds the GPT-2 tokenizer from scratch and explains every weird LLM behaviour that traces back to tokenization.
  5. SentencePiece: A simple and language independent subword tokenizer and detokenizer — Kudo & Richardson, EMNLP 2018. Why treating the space as a symbol matters for languages without spaces.
  6. Subword Regularization — Kudo, ACL 2018. The Unigram tokenizer and the idea of sampling segmentations during training.
  7. tiktoken — OpenAI, GitHub. The fast byte-level BPE library behind OpenAI's models; the README shows how to count tokens in three lines.
  8. Language Model Tokenizers Introduce Unfairness Between Languages — Petrov, La Malfa, Torr & Bibi, 2023. Measures how many more tokens the same text costs in different languages.
  9. Tokenization counts: the impact of tokenization on arithmetic in frontier LLMs — Singh & Strouse, 2024. Shows that how digits are grouped into tokens changes arithmetic accuracy.
  10. Speech and Language Processing, Chapter 6: Vector Semantics and Embeddings — Jurafsky & Martin, Stanford, 2025. Dot products, cosine, word2vec and GloVe explained carefully with small examples.
  11. Linguistic Regularities in Continuous Space Word Representations — Mikolov, Yih & Zweig, NAACL 2013. The short paper where king − man + woman ≈ queen first appeared.
  12. Issues in evaluating semantic spaces using word analogies — Linzen, 2016. A short paper explaining why the analogy test is weaker than it looks.
  13. Deep contextualized word representations — Peters et al., NAACL 2018. ELMo: the case for contextual embeddings.
  14. Using the Output Embedding to Improve Language Models — Press & Wolf, EACL 2017. The weight-tying paper, cited by the Transformer.
