Next: Day 3 — Neural networks in one day

Week 1 · Day 3

Neural networks in one day

After today you can read a training loop line by line, compute a cross-entropy loss by hand, and explain what "the model learned" means in terms of weights, gradients and a learning rate.

About 90 min · Builds on Day 2

Today's big idea

A neural network is a big pile of multiplications and additions with a few bends in it, controlled by millions of adjustable numbers called parameters. Training means: measure how wrong the output is with a loss, compute which direction each parameter should move to reduce that loss (the gradient), and nudge every parameter a tiny step that way. Repeat a few million times. That is the entire learning algorithm behind every LLM.

1. One neuron: weighted sum, bias, bend

A neuron in a neural network is a tiny function. It takes some input numbers, multiplies each by its own weight, adds the products up, adds a constant called the bias, and then passes the result through a simple non-linear function called the activation.

output = f(w₁x₁ + w₂x₂ + … + wₙxₙ + b)

A worked example. Let x = (2, −1), weights w = (0.5, 1.0) and bias b = 0.3. The weighted sum is 0.5×2 + 1.0×(−1) + 0.3 = 0.3; with the ReLU activation (next section) the output is 0.3. Change the bias to −0.5 and the sum becomes −0.5, which ReLU turns into 0: the neuron is "off". The weights decide which inputs the neuron cares about and in which direction; the bias sets how easily it switches on. Training is choosing these numbers automatically.

Try it: the widget below is one neuron with sliders for its two weights and its bias. Drag them and watch the output under ReLU and GELU.

Notice that with ReLU the output is exactly zero across a whole region of inputs, and that GELU is a smooth version of the same shape that dips slightly below zero before rising. Notice also that no setting of the sliders can make the output curve wiggle twice: one neuron is a very simple function. Power comes from stacking many of them.

2. Activations: why the bend is not optional

Suppose we left the activation out, so each neuron just computed a weighted sum. Stack ten such layers and you get… a weighted sum. Composing linear functions gives a linear function, so a hundred linear layers can only draw straight lines (or flat planes) through the data; they could never learn that "cat" and "dog" are near each other but far from "the", or anything with an "if". The non-linearity is what lets stacked layers represent curves, corners and switches, and with enough neurons a single hidden layer with a non-linear activation can approximate any continuous function to any precision, a result known as the universal approximation theorem. Wikipedia 2026 The theorem says such a network exists, not that training will find it, which is why the rest of today is about training.

Which bend? Early networks used S-shaped functions (sigmoid, tanh), which squash everything into a fixed range and whose slope vanishes for large inputs, making deep networks hard to train. The modern defaults are all variations on "pass positives, kill negatives":

ActivationFormulaf(0.3)f(−0.5)Used in
ReLUmax(0, x)0.3000original Transformer, most vision networks
GELUx · Φ(x), Φ = Gaussian CDF0.185−0.154BERT, GPT-2, GPT-3
SiLU (Swish)x · sigmoid(x)0.172−0.189Llama (inside SwiGLU), many 2023+ models

ReLU (rectified linear unit) is max(0, x): cheap, with slope 1 for positive inputs so gradients pass through unchanged, and the activation that made deep networks practical around 2010–2012. Nair & Hinton 2010 Glorot 2011 GELU multiplies x by the probability that a standard normal variable is below x: a smooth curve that acts like ReLU for large |x| but lets small negatives through a little; it is used in BERT and the GPT family. Hendrycks 2016 SiLU (Swish) is x times the sigmoid of x, found by automated search and nearly identical in shape to GELU. Ramachandran 2017 SwiGLU is a layer design rather than an activation: the input goes through two linear maps, one passes through SiLU, and the results are multiplied, so one path "gates" the other. Shazeer showed in 2020 that this improved Transformer quality over plain ReLU or GELU layers, Shazeer 2020 and Llama and most open models since use it. Meta 2024 The differences between these are small; the difference between having one and having none is everything.

3. Layers, MLPs and parameters

A layer is a row of neurons that all read the same inputs. If a layer has 3,072 neurons each reading a 768-number vector, it has a 768 × 3,072 weight matrix plus 3,072 biases, and computing it is one matrix multiplication followed by the activation. Stacking two such layers, one that expands and one that shrinks back, gives the MLP (multi-layer perceptron), the workhorse of every Transformer block. GPT-2 small's MLP goes 768 → 3,072 → 768: 768×3,072 + 3,072 + 3,072×768 + 768 = 4,722,432 parameters, about 4.7 million, and there are 12 of them in the model, roughly 57 million of its 124 million parameters. Hugging Face 2026 Day 5 accounts for the rest.

The parameters of a model are all its weights and biases together (and, from Day 2, the embedding table). "GPT-3 has 175 billion parameters" means 175 billion adjustable numbers, every one set by the procedure in the next four sections. Nothing else is learned; the architecture, activations and tokenizer are fixed by the designers before training starts.

Analogy

Think of a studio mixing desk with a million sliders. Nobody can set them by hand, so you play a reference track, measure how far the output is from it (the loss), and a machine tells you, for every slider at once, "nudge up" or "nudge down" and by how much (the gradient). You move every slider a tiny bit (the learning rate), play the next bit of the track, and repeat. After millions of nudges the desk reproduces the reference. Training a language model is this, with the reference track being "the next token of the text".

4. The loss: how wrong were we?

To improve, the model needs a single number that says how bad its current output is. For next-token prediction that number is the cross-entropy loss, also called negative log-likelihood: look up the probability the model gave to the token that actually came next, take its logarithm, and flip the sign. PyTorch 2026

loss = −log p(correct next token)

A worked example. Suppose the vocabulary has four tokens and after "The cat sat on the" the model outputs probabilities mat 0.60, floor 0.20, car 0.15, the 0.05. The real next token was "mat", so the loss is −ln 0.60 = 0.51. If the real token had been "floor", the loss would be −ln 0.20 = 1.61; if "the", −ln 0.05 = 3.00. The loss is 0 only for a probability of 1, and it climbs steeply as the probability of the truth falls, so putting 0.001 on the right answer costs 6.9 while putting 0.5 costs 0.69. For a whole text, average the losses over every position. Training a language model means driving this average down on trillions of tokens. The perplexity of Day 1 is simply e raised to the average loss: a loss of 0.51 on every token would be a perplexity of 1.67.

Why the logarithm rather than "1 minus the probability"? Products become sums: the probability of a text is the product of per-token probabilities (Day 1), and the log turns that into an average that does not shrink to zero for long texts. And the log punishes confident mistakes severely, which pushes the model to spread probability over everything plausible instead of betting on one option. It is also Shannon's quantity: the loss in bits (log base 2) is the number of bits needed to encode the correct token under the model, so minimising cross-entropy is literally maximising compression. Delétang 2023

5. Gradient descent and the learning rate

Now the key move. The loss is a function of all the parameters. For any single parameter, we can ask: if I increase it slightly, does the loss go up or down, and how steeply? That rate of change is the parameter's gradient. The gradient of the whole model is the list of these rates for every parameter, and it points in the direction of steepest increase of the loss. So to reduce the loss, move every parameter a small step the opposite way:

w ← w − η · ∂loss/∂w

The step-size multiplier η is the learning rate, and it is the most important number a practitioner sets by hand. A tiny one-parameter example shows why. Let the loss be (w − 3)², a bowl with its bottom at w = 3, so the gradient is 2(w − 3). Start at w = 0.

Learning rate ηw after step 1step 2step 3Behaviour
0.10.601.081.46creeps towards 3; converges, slowly
0.53.003.003.00jumps to the bottom in one step (a lucky special case)
1.06.000.006.00oscillates forever, never settles
1.16.60−1.328.18diverges; each step overshoots further

Try it: the widget below runs gradient descent on a bowl-shaped loss. Move the learning-rate slider and watch the ball settle, oscillate, or fly off.

Notice that too small is slow but safe, too large explodes, and the useful range between is narrow. Real runs warm up with small steps, hold a peak rate, then decay towards zero so the final parameters settle; GPT-3 175B used a peak learning rate of 0.6 × 10⁻⁴ and Llama 3 405B used 8 × 10⁻⁵, tiny numbers because each step is averaged over millions of tokens. Brown 2020 Meta 2024

Real losses are not clean bowls: with billions of parameters the surface has valleys, plateaus and saddle points, and we only see a noisy estimate of it from a small batch. Gradient descent still works, far better than anyone expected before 2012, and exactly why is still a research question.

6. Backpropagation: passing blame backwards

Gradient descent needs the gradient of the loss for every one of 175 billion parameters. Nudging each one and re-running the model would take 175 billion forward passes per step. Backpropagation computes all of them for about the cost of two forward passes, using one rule from first-year calculus: the chain rule, which says that if y depends on u and u depends on w, the effect of w on y is the effect of w on u times the effect of u on y.

Think of it as assigning blame. The last layer can see directly how much each of its outputs contributed to the loss. It tells the layer before it: "your output number 7 made my loss worse by this much per unit"; that layer multiplies by its own local slopes to work out how much blame goes to each of its weights and each of its inputs, and passes the input blame further back. Layer by layer, blame flows from the loss to the first weights, and every weight ends up with a number saying how it should change. Because every operation (multiply, add, ReLU, softmax) has a simple local derivative, the whole thing is mechanical, and libraries like PyTorch do it automatically by recording the forward computation and replaying it backwards. Wikipedia 2026

A two-step example. A neuron with one input computes y = w·x, and the loss is (y − t)² where t is the target. Let x = 2, w = 1, t = 5: then y = 2 and loss = 9. The chain rule gives ∂loss/∂w = ∂loss/∂y × ∂y/∂w = 2(y − t) × x = 2(−3) × 2 = −12. The negative sign means increasing w lowers the loss. One gradient step with η = 0.1 gives w = 1 − 0.1×(−12) = 2.2, so y = 4.4 and the loss falls from 9 to 0.36. A network with a million layers does the same thing a million times over.

Rumelhart, Hinton and Williams published the modern form for neural networks in 1986, after earlier discoveries in other fields, and every deep learning system still uses it. Rumelhart 1986 Karpathy's micrograd implements a complete backpropagation engine in about 100 lines of Python; to see the chain rule become code, start there. Karpathy 2020

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a long line of kids passing a bucket of water from a pond to a fire. The fire does not go out: that is the loss, the problem. Who is to blame? The last kid sees how little water reached the fire and tells the kid before: "you gave me too little." That kid works out how much was their own fault and how much the kid before them, and passes the complaint down the line. In the end every kid knows how much more (or less) to pass next time, and nobody needed to see the whole line. That is backpropagation. Then everyone adjusts a tiny bit and you try again. That is gradient descent.

7. Minibatches, SGD, and Adam

Computing the exact gradient over all 15 trillion training tokens before every step would be absurd. Instead each step uses a minibatch, a random sample of the data, and taking steps on the batch's noisy gradient estimate is stochastic gradient descent (SGD). The noise is mostly a feature: it is cheap and helps escape flat regions. GPT-3 175B used batches of 3.2 million tokens; Brown 2020 Llama 3 405B started at 4 million tokens per batch and grew to 16 million. Meta 2024 Bigger batches give cleaner gradients and spread across more GPUs, but past a point they stop helping.

Plain SGD uses the same learning rate for every parameter, but in a language model some parameters receive large, frequent gradients (common tokens' embeddings) and others tiny, rare ones. Adam keeps, for every parameter, a running average of its recent gradients (momentum, which smooths the noise) and of their squares (how big that parameter's gradients usually are), then divides one by the square root of the other, so each parameter effectively gets its own learning rate. Kingma 2014 AdamW is Adam with weight decay applied correctly: every step, all weights are also shrunk slightly towards zero, which discourages any single weight from growing huge and improves generalisation. Loshchilov and Hutter showed that the way earlier Adam implementations mixed weight decay into the gradient was subtly wrong, and that decoupling it makes the decay setting independent of the learning rate. Loshchilov 2017 AdamW is the optimiser behind essentially every LLM trained since; PyTorch's defaults are a learning rate of 0.001, averaging constants of 0.9 and 0.999, and a weight decay of 0.01, though LLM runs usually override all of them. PyTorch 2026

Adam's price is memory: two extra numbers per parameter, so the optimiser state for a 7B model in 32-bit precision is 56 GB on top of weights and gradients, a cost Day 10 returns to when explaining why fine-tuning big models is hard.

8. Overfitting: the train/validation gap

A model with billions of parameters can memorise its training data instead of learning the patterns in it. The symptom is a gap: the loss on training batches keeps falling while the loss on held-out text, the validation loss, stops falling and then rises. That rise is overfitting, and since the goal is to predict text the model has never seen, validation loss is the number that matters. Everyone in machine learning watches two curves and changes something when they separate.

The classic cures are more data, a smaller model, dropout (randomly switching off neurons during training so none can be relied on alone), Srivastava 2014 weight decay, and early stopping at the best checkpoint. Large language models mostly sidestep the problem with the first cure: with trillions of tokens they see most of their data only once, so there is little to memorise. The danger returns when data runs short: Muennighoff and colleagues found that repeating the dataset up to about four times costs almost nothing, but beyond that extra epochs stop helping and eventually hurt. Muennighoff 2023 "Running out of fresh data" is the modern form of overfitting, a theme Day 6 returns to.

9. Why GPUs

Look back at section 3: a layer is a matrix multiplication. So is attention (Day 4), so is the output layer. Nearly all the arithmetic in a language model, forward and backward, is multiplying large matrices, which is thousands of independent multiply-adds that can all happen at once. A CPU has a handful of fast cores built for doing different things in sequence. A GPU (graphics processing unit) has thousands of slower cores built for doing the same thing to many numbers at once, which is exactly what matrix multiplication needs; shading millions of pixels per frame, the job GPUs were built for, is the same kind of arithmetic.

The turning point was 2012, when AlexNet, trained on two consumer GPUs, won the ImageNet contest by a wide margin and made GPU training the default. Wikipedia 2026 Today's chips are built around matrix multiplication: NVIDIA lists 1,979 trillion 16-bit operations per second for the H100 SXM's tensor cores (assuming a sparse matrix; about half that for dense ones), with 80 GB of memory read at 3.35 TB/s. NVIDIA 2026 Llama 3 405B was trained on up to 16,000 of them at once. Meta 2024 The bandwidth number matters as much as the compute: when generating one token at a time, the bottleneck is usually moving weights from memory to the cores, not arithmetic, the key fact behind Day 7.

10. The whole thing in ten lines

Here is the training loop that every language model, from a toy to a frontier model, actually runs. Everything else in this course is detail about one of these lines.

model = Transformer(params)                 # random weights (Days 4–5)
opt = AdamW(model.parameters(), lr=3e-4)    # the optimiser (section 7)
for step in range(num_steps):
    tokens = next_batch(data)               # ids from the tokenizer (Day 2)
    logits = model(tokens[:, :-1])          # forward pass: scores for each next token
    loss = cross_entropy(logits, tokens[:, 1:])   # section 4
    loss.backward()                         # backpropagation fills in every gradient (section 6)
    opt.step()                              # move every parameter against its gradient (section 5)
    opt.zero_grad()                         # clear gradients for the next batch
    if step % 1000 == 0: print(step, loss.item(), validation_loss(model))

Two details are worth seeing. The input is the text minus its last token and the target is the text minus its first, so one pass over a 4,096-token sequence makes 4,095 predictions at once and scores them all; that is where the efficiency of Transformers over one-word-at-a-time RNNs comes from. And nothing in the loop knows about grammar, facts or chat: the loss is the only teacher, which is Day 1's point about compression. Karpathy reproduces GPT-2 with exactly this loop, fleshed out, in a four-hour video. Karpathy 2024

Going deeper (optional)

Loss curves of language models are strikingly regular. Kaplan and colleagues found in 2020 that validation loss falls as a smooth power law in model size, data and compute over many orders of magnitude, with no overfitting kink as long as data grows with the model. Kaplan 2020 That regularity lets labs predict a run's final loss before paying for it; see Day 6 and the scaling-laws paper review. Things a real loop adds that today skipped: gradient clipping (capping the gradient's length so one bad batch cannot blow up the weights), mixed precision (16-bit arithmetic with a 32-bit master copy of the weights), and splitting the model across GPUs.

Common misunderstandings

  • "The network is programmed with rules." Only the architecture is designed. Every number that determines behaviour is found by gradient descent on the loss; no one writes rules into the weights.
  • "Backpropagation is how the model learns." Backpropagation only computes gradients. Learning is the update step; backprop is the bookkeeping that makes the update cheap.
  • "A lower training loss always means a better model." Only validation loss on unseen text measures generalisation; a falling training loss with a rising validation loss means memorisation.
  • "GPUs are used because they are faster at everything." They are slower at sequential work. They win because the workload is almost entirely matrix multiplication, which is embarrassingly parallel.

Check yourself

Q1. A neuron has inputs (1, 2), weights (3, −1), bias 0.5 and ReLU activation. Its output is…

Answer 3. Weighted sum: 3×1 + (−1)×2 + 0.5 = 1.5, which is positive, so ReLU leaves it unchanged. Forgetting the bias gives 1; forgetting ReLU on a negative sum would give a negative output, which ReLU never does.

Q2. Why can a stack of layers without activation functions not learn complex patterns?

Answer 1. A weighted sum of weighted sums is still a weighted sum. The non-linearity is what makes depth meaningful and is required by the universal approximation theorem.

Q3. The model gives the correct next token a probability of 0.05. Its cross-entropy loss (natural log) for that token is about…

Answer 4. −ln(0.05) ≈ 3.0. The loss is not "1 minus probability" (0.95) and not the probability itself; 1.30 would be the base-10 log, which is not the convention.

Q4. With loss (w − 3)² and w starting at 0, a learning rate of 1.1 makes gradient descent…

Answer 2. Each step multiplies the distance to the minimum by |1 − 2η| = 1.2, so w goes 0 → 6.6 → −1.32 → 8.18 and keeps growing. A rate of 0.5 is the one-step case; 0.1 converges slowly.

Q5. What does backpropagation compute?

Answer 3. Backpropagation is the gradient computation. The optimiser (SGD, AdamW) then uses those gradients to produce new weights; validation loss is a separate forward pass.

Q6. Training loss keeps falling while validation loss starts rising. What is happening and what should you do?

Answer 1. The gap between the curves is the definition of overfitting. Training longer or faster would widen it; validation loss, not training loss, is what predicts performance on new text.

Recap: five things to remember

  1. A neuron is weighted sum + bias + non-linearity; a layer is a matrix multiplication; an MLP is two layers; without the non-linearity depth is useless.
  2. Cross-entropy loss = −log p(correct token). Perplexity is e to the average loss. Minimising it on text is the same as compressing text.
  3. Gradient descent moves every parameter a small step against its gradient; the learning rate sets the step, and too large a rate diverges.
  4. Backpropagation computes all the gradients at once by passing blame backwards with the chain rule; AdamW turns those gradients into per-parameter updates on minibatches of millions of tokens.
  5. Watch validation loss, not training loss; and the whole workload is matrix multiplication, which is why GPUs with high memory bandwidth are the hardware of the field.

References

  1. The spelled-out intro to neural networks and backpropagation: building micrograd — Andrej Karpathy, YouTube, 2022. Builds backpropagation from scratch in Python in 2.5 hours; the best single resource for today's material.
  2. micrograd — Andrej Karpathy, GitHub. The 100-line autograd engine from the video; read engine.py.
  3. But what is a neural network? — 3Blue1Brown. The first of a four-part visual series; follow it with Gradient descent, how neural networks learn and What is backpropagation really doing?.
  4. Speech and Language Processing, Chapter 7: Neural Networks — Jurafsky & Martin, Stanford, 2025. Units, MLPs, cross-entropy and backpropagation written for language people.
  5. CS231n course notes — Stanford. The "Backpropagation, Intuitions" and "Neural Networks" notes are the classic written treatment of gradient flow and training tricks.
  6. Deep Learning — Goodfellow, Bengio & Courville, MIT Press, 2016. Free online; chapters 6 and 8 cover feedforward networks and optimisation rigorously.
  7. Learning representations by back-propagating errors — Rumelhart, Hinton & Williams, Nature, 1986. Four pages; the paper that made backpropagation standard.
  8. Adam: A Method for Stochastic Optimization — Kingma & Ba, ICLR 2015. The optimiser; Algorithm 1 on page 2 is all you need.
  9. Decoupled Weight Decay Regularization — Loshchilov & Hutter, ICLR 2019. Why AdamW is not just Adam with a regulariser.
  10. Gaussian Error Linear Units (GELUs) — Hendrycks & Gimpel, 2016. The activation in BERT and GPT; short.
  11. GLU Variants Improve Transformer — Shazeer, Google, 2020. The SwiGLU paper, five pages, with the honest closing line that the authors have no explanation for why it works.
  12. Dropout: A Simple Way to Prevent Neural Networks from Overfitting — Srivastava, Hinton, Krizhevsky, Sutskever & Salakhutdinov, JMLR 2014. The standard regulariser and a clear discussion of overfitting.
  13. Scaling Data-Constrained Language Models — Muennighoff et al., NeurIPS 2023. What happens when you must repeat data: the LLM-era view of overfitting.
  14. Let's reproduce GPT-2 (124M) — Andrej Karpathy, YouTube, 2024. The ten-line loop above, grown into a real GPT-2 training run on GPUs.

Next: Day 4 — Attention

Week 1 · Day 4

Attention: how a model decides what to look at

After today you can compute an attention layer by hand, explain why it replaced recurrence, and say in plain words what "multi-head", "causal" and "O(n²)" mean.

About 90 min · Builds on Day 3

Today's big idea

Attention lets every token ask a question of every other token and pull in exactly the information it needs. It is a soft lookup: a token's query is compared with every token's key, the match scores become weights that sum to one, and the output is a weighted blend of the tokens' values. Everything else in a Transformer is scaffolding around this one operation.

1. The problem: a word means nothing on its own

Read two sentences: "She sat by the bank and watched the river" and "She walked into the bank and asked for a loan." The word is identical; the meaning is not. Day 2 gave every token a vector, but that vector is the same in both sentences. Something has to let the vector for bank change depending on river or loan. That something is attention.

Before 2017 the standard answer was the recurrent neural network (RNN): a network that reads tokens one at a time and carries a summary vector, the hidden state, from each step to the next. The best-known kind, the LSTM, dates from 1997 Wikipedia. RNNs have two weaknesses. First, the summary has a fixed size, so a long sentence has to be squeezed into the same few hundred numbers as a short one. The 2014 "sequence to sequence" translation system did exactly that: it read the whole source sentence into one vector and generated the translation from it Sutskever 2014. A study the same year showed translation quality dropping rapidly as sentences grew longer Cho 2014. Second, information has to survive many hops. A word at position 3 reaches position 300 only by being copied through 297 updates, and each update can smudge it. Training signals travel the same long road backwards and fade (the vanishing gradients of Day 3).

The fix came in 2014: let the translator look back at every source word at every step, with learned weights saying how much to look at each Bahdanau 2014. That was the first attention mechanism, bolted onto an RNN. Three years later a Google team asked whether the RNN was needed at all, kept only attention, and titled the paper "Attention Is All You Need" Vaswani 2017. Their model, the Transformer, is the subject of Day 5 and of the Transformer paper review. Dropping recurrence had a bonus that mattered as much as accuracy: every position can be processed at the same moment instead of one after another, which is exactly what a GPU is built for.

2. Queries, keys and values

Attention gives every token three vectors, all computed from the token's current vector x by multiplying with three learned matrices:

q = x·W_Q    k = x·W_K    v = x·W_V

The query is the question this token is asking ("I am a verb; where is my subject?"). The key is the label each token advertises ("I am a singular noun near the start"). The value is what a token hands over if it is chosen: the content that actually gets mixed into the output. Three separate vectors, because what a token looks for, what it can be found by, and what it contributes are three different jobs. The word "the" is rarely worth looking up, so its key should match little, yet it may still carry a value worth passing along. Keeping the roles apart lets the model learn each one independently.

Analogy: the library that lends you a bit of every book

You walk into a library with a question (your query). Every book has a label on its spine (its key). You compare your question with every label and get a match score for each. In a normal library you then take the one best book. Attention is stranger: you take a little of every book, in proportion to how well its label matched, and blend their contents (the values) into a single page. A strong match contributes most; a weak match contributes almost nothing; nothing is ever ignored outright. The blend is the new, context-aware vector for the token that asked.

In GPT-2 small the token vector has 768 numbers. Attention splits that into 12 heads (Section 7), so each head's q, k and v are 64 numbers long. The figure shows the flow for one token in one head.

3. Four steps: score, scale, softmax, sum

Step 1, score. Compare the query with each key using the dot product: multiply the vectors entry by entry and add up. The dot product is large and positive when two vectors point the same way, near zero when they are unrelated, and negative when they point in opposite directions. So q·k is literally "how well does this token's question match that token's label".

Step 2, scale. Divide every score by √d, where d is the length of the key vector (64 in GPT-2 small, so divide by 8). The reason is practical. If the entries of q and k are of size about 1, a dot product over d entries is typically of size about √d. Feeding numbers like ±8 into a softmax produces weights that are almost exactly 0 or 1, and in that regime the gradient is tiny, so learning stalls. The authors wrote that large dot products "push the softmax function into regions where it has extremely small gradients" Vaswani 2017. Scaling keeps the scores in the range where softmax is still soft. You will see the effect with real numbers in the next section.

Step 3, softmax. Turn the scaled scores into positive weights that add up to 1 (Day 3). Each weight says what fraction of the output comes from that token.

Step 4, weighted sum. Multiply each token's value by its weight and add them all up. That sum is the output for the querying token: its old vector, updated with context.

In matrix form, with Q, K and V holding all tokens' queries, keys and values as rows, the whole layer is one line:

Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(Q·Kᵀ / √d)·V

Q·Kᵀ is an n × n table of scores, one row per querying token and one column per key. Keep that table in mind; it is where the cost of attention lives (Section 9).

4. A worked example with real numbers

Three tokens, "The cat sat", and two-dimensional vectors so you can check every number. Suppose the learned matrices have already produced the keys and values in the table, and the query of sat is q = [2, 0]. We will compute the new vector for sat.

TokenKey kValue vScore q·k÷ √2expWeight
The[0, 1][1, 0]2·0 + 0·1 = 00.001.000.045
cat[2, 1][0, 1]2·2 + 0·1 = 42.8316.920.768
sat[1, 0][1, 1]2·1 + 0·0 = 21.414.110.187

The softmax step: exp of each scaled score, then divide by the total (1.00 + 16.92 + 4.11 = 22.03). The weights are 0.045, 0.768 and 0.187; check that they sum to 1. Now the weighted sum of values:

output = 0.045·[1, 0] + 0.768·[0, 1] + 0.187·[1, 1] = [0.23, 0.95]

The vector for sat is now mostly the value of cat. In words: the verb has found its subject and absorbed information about it. That is the whole mechanism; a real model does the same thing with 64- or 128-dimensional vectors, for every token at once, in every head of every layer.

Now redo step 3 without the √2 scaling. The exps become 1, 54.6 and 7.39, and the weights become 0.016, 0.867 and 0.117. Same ranking, but the distribution is sharper. With 64-dimensional vectors the unscaled scores would be about eight times larger still, and the softmax would be a near-perfect one-hot: no gradient, no learning. Dividing by √d is a small trick with a large effect.

Try it: the widget below runs the same arithmetic on a real sentence. Tap a word to make it the query and watch the scores, the softmax and the resulting weights over every other word.

Notice three things. The weights always sum to 1. Pronouns and verbs tend to put weight on the nouns they depend on. And a token usually gives itself some weight too: a query can match its own key, and often should.

5. The causal mask: why a decoder may not peek

A GPT-style model is trained to predict the next token. During training it sees a whole document at once and is scored at every position: at position 5 it must predict token 6, at position 6 token 7, and so on (Day 1). If the attention at position 5 were allowed to read token 6, the model would learn the easiest possible trick, copy what you can see, and it would be useless at generation time, when token 6 does not exist yet.

So decoder attention is causal: each token may attend only to itself and to earlier tokens. The implementation is blunt. Before the softmax, every score where the key comes after the query is replaced by −∞. Since exp(−∞) = 0, those positions get exactly zero weight and the remaining weights still sum to 1. In the n × n score table the mask zeroes everything above the diagonal:

              keys:  The    cat    sat
query The  [        s₁₁    −∞     −∞   ]
query cat  [        s₂₁    s₂₂    −∞   ]
query sat  [        s₃₁    s₃₂    s₃₃  ]
softmax runs along each row; exp(−∞) = 0, so masked entries get weight 0

The mask is applied whenever the model processes several tokens at once, which includes reading your prompt, not only training. Two consequences you will meet again. Because earlier tokens never look at later ones, their keys and values never change once computed, so they can be stored and reused as the model generates; that is the KV cache of Day 7. And an encoder-style model such as BERT drops the mask entirely, so every token sees every other, which is better for understanding and useless for generation (Day 5).

6. Many heads, many questions

One attention head computes one notion of relevance. But a token needs several at once: a verb wants its subject, its object, and the tense of the previous clause. Multi-head attention runs several attention operations in parallel, each with its own W_Q, W_K and W_V, and concatenates the results. The original Transformer used 8 heads; GPT-2 small uses 12; GPT-3 175B uses 96 Brown 2020.

Heads are not extra cost stacked on top. The model dimension is split among them: 768 = 12 heads × 64, so each head works with 64-number vectors and the total arithmetic is about the same as one head of size 768. After the heads are concatenated back to 768 numbers, one more learned matrix W_O mixes them. The paper's reason for heads is that a single head would average over all the kinds of relevance at once, and averaging blurs Vaswani 2017.

What do heads actually learn? Nobody programs them; they emerge from training, and researchers have catalogued several recurring kinds:

Previous-token heads attend almost entirely to the token one step back, so each token's vector picks up a copy of what came before it. Induction heads do something cleverer: when the current token is A, they look for an earlier A in the context, attend to the token that followed it, B, and raise the probability of B coming next. On the pattern "… Mr Dursley … Mr" an induction head predicts "Dursley". Anthropic's interpretability team found that an induction head is really a two-head circuit, a previous-token head in an earlier layer feeding an induction head in a later one, that these circuits appear abruptly during training, and that their appearance coincides with the model becoming able to learn from examples in its context Olsson 2022 Elhage 2021. In BERT, specific heads were found attending from verbs to their direct objects and from nouns to their determiners, while many heads simply park on punctuation when they have nothing to do Clark 2019.

Not every head earns its keep. Two 2019 studies showed that a large fraction of heads in trained translation models can be removed after training with little loss, and that a few specialised heads do most of the work Voita 2019 Michel 2019. Heads are cheap redundancy, not a precise set of modules.

7. Self-attention and cross-attention

Everything so far was self-attention: queries, keys and values all come from the same sequence. The 2017 Transformer also had cross-attention, where the queries come from one sequence and the keys and values from another. Its decoder, generating a French sentence, sent queries to the encoder's representation of the English sentence; that is how each French word found the English words it should translate Vaswani 2017. It is Bahdanau's 2014 idea, kept intact inside the new architecture.

A decoder-only model such as GPT has no encoder and uses self-attention only. When it needs to read a document and then answer a question about it, the document is simply placed in the same context as the question, and self-attention does the rest. That is why retrieval-augmented generation (Day 12) needs no new mechanism: retrieved text is just more tokens. Cross-attention survives in encoder-decoder models such as T5 and in some multimodal systems, where image features serve as the keys and values that text queries look at.

8. The n² bill

Every head computes an n × n table of scores for a context of n tokens. With n = 1,024, that is about one million scores per head, and GPT-2 small has 12 heads in each of 12 layers, so about 150 million scores per forward pass. Make the context 128,000 tokens and the table has 16 billion entries per head. Doubling the context quadruples the score table. Both the time and, if you store the table, the memory grow with n², which is what "attention is O(n²) in sequence length" means. The rest of the network (the MLPs of Day 5) grows only linearly with n, so long contexts are where attention dominates the cost.

This is the reason long context was slow to arrive, and the reason it eventually did. FlashAttention computes exactly the same result without ever writing the full n × n table to the GPU's main memory: it processes the table in tiles that fit in the much faster on-chip memory, trading some recomputation for far fewer memory transfers Dao 2022. A second line of work shrinks the memory for keys and values by sharing them across heads: multi-query attention uses one key and value head for all query heads Shazeer 2019, and grouped-query attention is the compromise in between Ainslie 2023. Llama 3 8B, for instance, has 32 query heads but only 8 key/value heads Meta 2024. Day 11 covers both, with the FlashAttention paper review.

Going deeper (optional)

At generation time the picture changes. With the KV cache, the model computes only the new token's query and compares it with the stored keys of all previous tokens, so each new token costs O(n) rather than O(n²), and the total for generating n tokens is still O(n²) but spread over n steps. The real constraint becomes memory: the stored keys and values for a long context can exceed the model's own weights. Day 7 puts numbers on this. Also note that attention's cost depends on n but not on the vocabulary, while the final prediction step depends on the vocabulary but not on n; the two bottlenecks are different.

9. Attention is a soft lookup

A dictionary in a programming language is a hard lookup: you provide a key, and you get back exactly one value if the key matches and nothing otherwise. Attention is the soft version. Every stored key matches a little, the match strength is a dot product rather than equality, and the result is a blend of all values weighted by match. The softness is not a compromise; it is the point. A hard lookup has no gradient, so nothing about it could be learned. A soft lookup is a smooth function of the queries, keys and values, so gradient descent (Day 3) can tune the matrices that produce them until the lookups the model performs are the useful ones.

That gives a compact way to hold the whole day in your head. Attention: each token's query is matched against every token's key; softmax turns the matches into weights; the output is the weighted sum of values. Causal: only earlier keys are visible. Multi-head: several lookups in parallel, each with its own notion of "match". Cost: an n × n table per head.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a classroom where every kid is holding one word of a sentence. When it is your turn to understand your word, you shout a question, like "who is doing the action?" Every other kid holds up a sign saying what they are, like "I am a cat" or "I am the word 'the'". You look at all the signs and decide how interesting each one is to you: the cat gets most of your attention, "the" gets almost none. Then every kid whispers a bit of what they know, and you listen more closely to the kids you found interesting. Now you understand your word better, because you know who it is about. Every kid does this at the same time, and the class does it over and over, on several questions at once. The only rule: you cannot listen to kids holding words that come after yours, because when the model is writing, those words do not exist yet.

Common misunderstandings

  • "Attention weights show what the model is thinking." They show where information flows in one head of one layer, which is useful but partial. A head can attend to a token whose value is nearly empty, and different weight patterns can produce the same output, so weights are weak evidence about the model's reasoning Jain 2019.
  • "The model attends to words." It attends to positions holding vectors that earlier layers have already mixed. By layer 10 the vector at sat carries a good deal of the whole sentence, so "attending to sat" is not the same as "looking at the verb".
  • "More heads is always better." Many trained heads can be pruned with little loss. Heads are a cheap way to let several patterns coexist, not a knob where more is better.
  • "The causal mask is only a training trick." It is applied whenever several tokens are processed at once, including when the model reads your prompt. Without it, the model's predictions for a prompt would be computed with information it will not have when generating.

Check yourself

Q1. In attention, which vector is compared with the query to produce the scores?

Answer 2. Scores are dot products of the query with every key. Values are what gets blended after the weights are known; they never enter the scoring. Positions can influence the keys, but they are not what the query is compared against.

Q2. Why are the dot-product scores divided by √d before the softmax?

Answer 3. Dot products of d-dimensional vectors grow like √d; large scores push softmax to near one-hot weights with tiny gradients. The softmax already makes weights sum to 1 regardless of scaling, and the division costs more, not less.

Q3. In the worked example, why did cat get most of the weight for the query from sat?

Answer 1. Weights come only from query–key dot products: 4 for cat versus 2 and 0. Values do not affect the weights, distance plays no role in plain attention, and there is no rule about nouns; the matrices learn whatever helps prediction.

Q4. What does the causal mask do, mechanically?

Answer 4. The mask acts on the score table, not on the input or the values; after softmax, exp(−∞) = 0 removes future positions while the remaining weights still sum to 1.

Q5. You double the context length from 4,000 to 8,000 tokens. What happens to the number of attention scores per head?

Answer 2. The score table is n × n, so doubling n multiplies the entries by 4. The KV cache changes how the work is spread over generation steps, not how many scores exist.

Q6. What does an induction head do?

Answer 3. Induction heads implement "A B … A → B". Attending to the previous token is the job of a previous-token head, which is the other half of the circuit; attending to the first token is a common idle behaviour, not induction.

Recap: five things to remember

  1. A word's vector must change with context; RNNs did this through a fixed-size memory that forgot and could not be parallelised. Attention replaced it.
  2. Each token gets a query, a key and a value from three learned matrices. Scores are query–key dot products; scaling by √d keeps the softmax soft; the output is the weighted sum of values.
  3. A decoder uses a causal mask: future scores are set to −∞, so a token only sees itself and the past. This is also what makes the KV cache possible.
  4. Multi-head attention runs several lookups in parallel at the same total cost; trained heads include previous-token heads and induction heads, and many heads are redundant.
  5. Attention costs n² scores per head. FlashAttention and shared key/value heads (Day 11) are what made long contexts practical.

References

  1. Attention Is All You Need — Vaswani et al., Google, NeurIPS 2017. The Transformer paper; Section 3.2 is the ten-minute read that defines scaled dot-product and multi-head attention.
  2. Neural Machine Translation by Jointly Learning to Align and Translate — Bahdanau, Cho, Bengio, ICLR 2015 (arXiv 2014). The first attention mechanism; its alignment heatmaps show attention doing visible work.
  3. Sequence to Sequence Learning with Neural Networks — Sutskever, Vinyals, Le, Google, NeurIPS 2014. The fixed-vector bottleneck in its purest form.
  4. On the Properties of Neural Machine Translation: Encoder-Decoder Approaches — Cho et al., 2014. The measurement that quality falls as sentences get longer, which motivated attention.
  5. The Illustrated Transformer — Jay Alammar, 2018. The classic picture-by-picture walk through Q, K, V and multi-head attention.
  6. Attention in transformers, step-by-step — 3Blue1Brown, 2024. An animated version of today's lesson; watch it after reading, not instead.
  7. Let's build GPT: from scratch, in code, spelled out — Andrej Karpathy, 2023. Builds attention line by line in PyTorch, including the mask trick, in front of your eyes.
  8. The Annotated Transformer — Harvard NLP. The 2017 paper interleaved with runnable code; the reference implementation to read once you know the ideas.
  9. In-context Learning and Induction Heads — Olsson et al., Anthropic, 2022. The evidence that induction heads appear suddenly during training and power in-context learning.
  10. What Does BERT Look At? An Analysis of BERT's Attention — Clark et al., Stanford, 2019. A catalogue of what individual heads attend to, with pictures.

  14. Attention is not Explanation — Jain and Wallace, NAACL 2019. A caution against reading attention weights as the model's reasons.

Next: Day 5 — The Transformer, fully assembled

Week 1 · Day 5

The Transformer, fully assembled

After today you can draw the whole GPT-style stack from memory, count its parameters, and read any model's config file without flinching.

About 90 min · Builds on Day 4

Today's big idea

A decoder-only Transformer is: embed the tokens, run N identical blocks, normalise, and project to vocabulary scores. Each block does two things: attention mixes information across tokens, and a small per-token network (the MLP) processes each token on its own. Residual connections carry a running stream of information through the whole stack so that each block only has to add a correction. Everything else is choices of size.

1. The whole machine in one picture

Here is the entire architecture of GPT-2, Llama 3 and, as far as is public, every frontier language model of 2026, read from bottom to top:

token ids ──▶ token embedding (+ position information)
              │
              ▼
          ┌─────────── block, repeated N times ───────────┐
          │  x = x + Attention(Norm(x))     ← mixes tokens │
          │  x = x + MLP(Norm(x))           ← per token    │
          └───────────────────────────────────────────────┘
              │
              ▼
          final Norm ──▶ LM head (d_model × vocab) ──▶ logits ──▶ softmax

Call the vector x that runs down the middle the residual stream: one vector of d_model numbers per token, which every block reads from and adds to Elhage 2021. GPT-2 small has d_model = 768 and N = 12 blocks; Llama 3 70B has d_model = 8,192 and N = 80 Meta 2024. The shape is the same; only the numbers change. The original 2017 model had an encoder and a decoder with cross-attention between them Vaswani 2017; Section 9 explains why modern language models keep only the decoder half.

Analogy: an assembly line with a shared tray

Each token is a tray moving along a conveyor belt. At every station (block) two workers act on it. The first worker, attention, is allowed to look at all the trays that came before and copy useful parts onto this one. The second worker, the MLP, works only on this tray, with no view of the others, using a big book of rules. Neither worker replaces the tray; each adds something to what is already there (the residual connection). After N stations, a final inspector (the LM head) looks at the tray and reads off a score for every possible next word.

2. Tokens become vectors, and position has to come from somewhere

The first layer is a lookup table: row t of the token embedding matrix is the vector for token id t (Day 2). For GPT-2 that table is 50,257 rows × 768 columns, and it is the single largest piece of the model (Section 7). But attention, as defined on Day 4, is blind to order: shuffle the tokens and every query meets the same set of keys and values, so the outputs are the same set, shuffled. "Dog bites man" and "man bites dog" would be identical. Position has to be injected on purpose, and there are four common ways.

Learned absolute positions. A second lookup table, one row per position, added to the token vector. GPT-1 chose this over the 2017 paper's sinusoids Radford 2018, and GPT-2 and GPT-3 kept it. Simple, but position 1,025 has no row, so the model cannot read anything longer than it was trained on.

Sinusoidal positions. The 2017 paper added a fixed vector of sines and cosines at a spread of frequencies: PE(pos, 2i) = sin(pos / 10000^(2i/d)), PE(pos, 2i+1) = cos of the same. Low-frequency dimensions change slowly along the sequence, high-frequency ones quickly, like the hands of a clock, and the authors hoped the model could learn to attend by relative offset because any fixed shift is a linear function of these vectors Vaswani 2017.

Rotary position embedding (RoPE) is what Llama, Qwen, DeepSeek and most open models use today. Instead of adding a position vector to the token, RoPE rotates the query and key vectors. Take the 64 numbers of a head's query, pair them up into 32 two-dimensional points, and rotate each point by an angle proportional to the token's position, with a different rotation speed for each pair. Do the same to the keys. Now the dot product between a query at position m and a key at position n depends only on the difference m − n, because rotating both arrows by the same amount leaves the angle between them unchanged, and the angle between them is what the dot product measures Su 2021. So RoPE encodes absolute position in the vectors but relative position in the attention scores, which is the quantity that matters. The rotation speeds are set by one number, the base θ; Llama 3 raised it to 500,000 to make long contexts work Meta 2024. The RoPE paper review goes through the maths.

ALiBi drops position vectors entirely and instead subtracts a penalty from each attention score that grows with the distance between query and key, with a different slope per head. Models trained this way extrapolated to sequences longer than any seen in training Press 2021. It is less common in 2026 than RoPE but the idea, "position as a bias on scores rather than a feature in the vector", shows how many choices are open.

Try it: the widget shows the sinusoidal table as a heatmap, and a RoPE rotation that you can drive with a position slider.

In the heatmap, read down one column: the leftmost dimensions flip quickly with position, the rightmost barely move. In the RoPE view, slide the position and watch the query and key arrows turn together; the angle between them, and so their dot product, depends only on how far apart they are.

3. Inside the block, first stop: normalisation

Each block begins by normalising the token vector. Layer normalisation (LayerNorm) takes the d_model numbers of one token, subtracts their mean, divides by their standard deviation, then multiplies by a learned per-dimension gain and adds a learned bias Ba 2016. The effect is that whatever scale the residual stream has reached, the attention and MLP always see inputs of a standard size. RMSNorm is the simplification used by Llama and most newer models: skip the mean subtraction, divide by the root mean square, and keep only the gain Zhang 2019 Touvron 2023. It is slightly cheaper and works as well.

Where the norm sits matters more than which norm. The 2017 Transformer applied it after each sub-layer's residual addition (post-norm). GPT-2 moved it to the input of each sub-layer, with one extra norm after the final block (pre-norm) Radford 2019. A 2020 analysis explained why pre-norm trains more easily: with post-norm the gradients at the output layers are large at initialisation, which is why the original recipe needed a careful learning-rate warm-up; with pre-norm the gradients are well behaved from the start and warm-up can be dropped Xiong 2020. Every large model since has used pre-norm, and the pseudo-code in Section 11 is written that way.

4. Residual connections: the highway through the stack

Each sub-layer's output is added to its input rather than replacing it: x = x + f(x). This is the residual connection, introduced for image networks in 2015 when researchers found that plain networks got worse past about 20 layers, not because they overfit but because they could not be optimised He 2015.

Why it works: the gradient of x + f(x) with respect to x is 1 plus the gradient of f. That "1" is an identity path straight through every block, so the training signal reaches layer 1 of a 96-layer model without being multiplied by 96 small numbers on the way. The arithmetic is unforgiving without it: if each layer scaled the gradient by 0.9, after 96 layers it would be 0.9⁹⁶ ≈ 0.00004, and the early layers would never learn. The residual path makes depth cheap. It also changes what a block has to do. Instead of computing the complete representation afresh, a block only has to learn a correction, which is easier, and a block that has nothing useful to add can output something close to zero and do no harm. This is the view behind the "residual stream" picture: a shared memory that layers read from and write to Elhage 2021.

5. The MLP: four times wider, then back

After attention has mixed information across tokens, the MLP (also called the feed-forward network) processes every token separately with the same two matrices. The first expands the vector from d_model to 4 × d_model, a non-linearity is applied, and the second projects back down: 768 → 3,072 → 768 in GPT-2. The non-linearity is GELU in the GPT family, a smooth cousin of ReLU (Day 3) Hendrycks 2016. The MLP has no view of other tokens, which is why the two halves of the block are complementary: attention moves information around, the MLP transforms it.

The width matters because the MLP is where most of the parameters, and apparently most of the stored knowledge, live. One study showed that each MLP layer behaves like a key-value memory, with the first matrix's rows acting as pattern detectors and the second matrix's columns holding what to write when a pattern fires; lower layers respond to surface patterns, upper layers to meaning Geva 2020. Another located factual associations such as "the Eiffel Tower is in Paris" in the MLPs of middle layers and edited them directly Meng 2022.

Modern models use a variant, SwiGLU, which has three matrices instead of two: one branch is passed through a smooth gate (the Swish function) and multiplied element by element with the other branch before the down-projection. It performed better than plain GELU MLPs at the same parameter count Shazeer 2020, and Llama adopted it Touvron 2023. Because there are three matrices, the hidden width is reduced from 4× to keep the cost similar: Llama 3 8B expands 4,096 to 14,336, about 3.5× Meta 2024.

6. Stack it, normalise, and read out

Stack N blocks; each has its own weights. Apply the final norm. Then the LM head: one matrix of size d_model × vocab that turns the last vector for each position into a score (a logit) for every token in the vocabulary. Softmax turns the logits into next-token probabilities, which is the output you met on Day 1. GPT-2 does not have a separate LM head matrix at all: it reuses the token embedding table transposed, a trick called weight tying that saves 38 million parameters and slightly improves the model Press 2016. Many larger models untie the two, because at their scale the saving is negligible.

During training, the head is applied at every position and the loss is averaged across all of them, so one document of 1,024 tokens gives 1,024 training examples in one pass. During generation only the last position's logits are needed, which is why the per-token cost of generation is far below that of a full forward pass over the context once the KV cache (Day 7) is in place.

7. Counting GPT-2 small, parameter by parameter

Now the arithmetic. GPT-2 small: vocabulary 50,257, context 1,024, d_model 768, 12 heads, 12 layers, MLP width 3,072, LayerNorm with gain and bias, weight-tied head Hugging Face docs. Every linear layer has a bias, which adds a little.

PartShapeParameters
Token embedding50,257 × 76838,597,376
Position embedding1,024 × 768786,432
Per block: norm 12 × 7681,536
Per block: Q, K, V projections768 × 2,304 + 2,3041,771,776
Per block: output projection W_O768 × 768 + 768590,592
Per block: norm 22 × 7681,536
Per block: MLP up768 × 3,072 + 3,0722,362,368
Per block: MLP down3,072 × 768 + 7682,360,064
One block7,087,872
12 blocks12 × 7,087,87285,054,464
Final norm2 × 7681,536
LM headtied to token embedding0
Total124,439,808

So "GPT-2 small" is 124 million parameters, matching the published weights Hugging Face model card. The paper's table said 117M; OpenAI later noted that the original counts were wrong OpenAI 2019. Three things to notice. The token embedding alone is 31% of the model. Within a block, the MLP holds two-thirds of the weights (4.72M against 2.36M for attention). And the positional table is tiny, under 1%. In fp16, two bytes per parameter, the whole model is about 249 MB; a 7B model by the same rule is 14 GB.

Try it: tap each part of the block diagram below to read what it does and how many of GPT-2 small's parameters it owns.

Compare the counts with the table. The two residual additions and the softmax own no parameters at all; they are pure arithmetic, which is why they are often left out of diagrams despite doing essential work.

8. From 2019 to 2026: the same shape, bigger numbers

The table below is the single most useful thing to memorise today, because every model card you will ever read is a row of it.

KnobGPT-2 small (2019)GPT-3 175B (2020)Llama 3 8B (2024)Llama 3 70B (2024)
Parameters124M175B8B70B
Layers N12963280
d_model76812,2884,0968,192
Heads (query / key-value)12 / 1296 / 9632 / 864 / 8

Sources: GPT-2 from its config and paper Hugging Face docs; GPT-3's 96 layers, d_model 12,288, 96 heads of 128, context 2,048 and 4× MLP from its paper, which also says it reuses GPT-2's architecture, tokenizer and pre-normalisation Brown 2020; Llama 3 from its Table 3 Meta 2024. GPT-3 also alternated dense attention with locally banded sparse attention in some layers, an early attempt at the n² problem that later models dropped in favour of FlashAttention.

Read across a row and you see what changed. Head size settled at 128. Key/value heads shrank relative to query heads (grouped-query attention, Day 4) to keep the KV cache small. Vocabularies grew from 50K to 128K so that each token covers more text. Context grew from 1K to 128K through RoPE and long-context training rather than through a bigger position table. Two further knobs appear in the newest models and get their own days: mixture of experts, where the MLP is replaced by several MLPs and a router picks a few per token, so that DeepSeek-V3 has 671B parameters but uses 37B for any given token DeepSeek 2024 (Day 11, Mixtral paper review), and compressed attention variants that shrink the keys and values further (Day 11).

9. Encoder-only, decoder-only, encoder-decoder

The 2017 Transformer had two stacks. The encoder reads the whole input with unmasked attention, so every token sees every other. The decoder generates the output with causal self-attention plus cross-attention into the encoder. Three families grew from this.

Encoder-only models, led by BERT (2018), keep the unmasked stack and train it to fill in blanked-out words: hide 15% of the tokens and predict them from both sides Devlin 2018. They produce excellent representations for classification, search and sentence embeddings, and are still the backbone of most embedding models used in retrieval (Day 12, BERT paper review). But they cannot generate text: there is no notion of "next".

Encoder-decoder models such as T5 (2019) keep both stacks and cast every task as text-to-text, with a corrupted-span objective for pretraining Raffel 2019. They remain strong for translation and summarisation, where the input is fixed and fully known in advance.

Decoder-only models, the GPT line, keep only the causal stack and train on next-token prediction. This is what won for general-purpose generation, and the reasons are mostly practical. One objective covers everything: a document, a dialogue, code and a question followed by its answer are all just sequences to continue. Every position produces a training signal, where BERT's objective trains on only the 15% of positions that were masked. There is no gap between how the model is trained and how it is used: prompt in, continuation out. The causal structure allows the KV cache, so generation is cheap. And a 2022 study that trained all three families under matched conditions found that decoder-only models with next-token prediction generalised best to new tasks after pretraining alone, before any task-specific tuning Wang 2022. Add the scaling results of Day 6 and the field converged.

10. What the layers seem to do

Every block has the same shape, but trained blocks are not interchangeable. Probing studies, which train small classifiers to read specific information out of each layer, give a consistent rough picture. In BERT, the layers recover the classical language-processing pipeline in order: part of speech early, then syntax, then semantic roles and coreference late Tenney 2019. In GPT-2 small, attention in the middle layers lines up best with grammatical dependencies, and the deepest layers capture the longest-distance relations Vig 2019. MLP layers respond to surface patterns low in the stack and to meaning higher up Geva 2020, with factual recall concentrated in middle-layer MLPs Meng 2022.

So the folk summary, "early layers do local and syntactic work, later layers do semantic and factual work", is about right, with two cautions. First, the model is not a clean pipeline; information is spread and revisited, and later layers can overturn earlier guesses. Second, these are findings about particular models probed with particular tools, not laws. Day 13 covers interpretability properly, including the circuits view that explains induction heads (Day 4) and the sparse features that newer methods extract from the residual stream.

11. The forward pass in twenty lines

Here is a GPT-2-style forward pass with nothing hidden. Shapes are in comments: n is the number of tokens, d is d_model.

def forward(tokens):                        # tokens: n integer ids
    n = len(tokens)
    x = tok_emb[tokens] + pos_emb[0:n]      # (n, d)   residual stream starts here
    for layer in layers:                    # N blocks, each with its own weights
        h = norm1(x)                        # pre-norm
        q = split_heads(h @ Wq)             # (heads, n, d_head)
        k = split_heads(h @ Wk)
        v = split_heads(h @ Wv)
        s = q @ k.T / sqrt(d_head)          # (heads, n, n) scores
        s = s + causal_mask                 # −inf above the diagonal
        a = softmax(s) @ v                  # weighted sum of values
        x = x + merge_heads(a) @ Wo         # residual add 1
        h = norm2(x)
        x = x + gelu(h @ W1) @ W2           # MLP 4× wide, residual add 2
    x = final_norm(x)
    logits = x @ tok_emb.T                  # (n, vocab)  tied LM head
    return logits                           # softmax(logits[-1]) = next-token probs

Biases are omitted for readability. Replace norm with RMSNorm, drop pos_emb and rotate q and k by position inside the loop, swap the MLP line for the gated SwiGLU form, share k and v across groups of heads, and you have Llama 3. The working code behind this sketch is a few hundred lines in nanoGPT Karpathy 2022.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Think of a tall building with the same room on every floor. Each word in a sentence rides its own elevator up through the building, carrying a backpack. On every floor, two things happen. First, the word is allowed to peek into the backpacks of the words that came before it and copy anything useful into its own backpack. Second, the word opens its own backpack alone, thinks hard about what is inside using a big rule book, and adds some notes. It never throws anything away; it only adds. At the top floor, someone opens the last word's backpack and guesses which word should come next. A small model has 12 floors; big ones have about 100. The building is the same for every language, every topic and every question, because the rule books on each floor were learned from reading, not written by people.

Common misunderstandings

  • "The MLP is where words talk to each other." Only attention moves information between positions. The MLP sees one token's vector at a time; it is the same function applied independently to every position.
  • "Most parameters are in attention." In GPT-2 small each block's MLP holds twice the weights of its attention, and the token embedding is almost a third of the whole model. Attention is where the interesting computation happens, not where most of the numbers are.
  • "GPT-2 small has 117M parameters." That figure is from the paper's table and was later acknowledged as an error; the released weights have 124M, as the count above shows.
  • "Deeper always means better." Depth, width, data and training compute trade off against each other; Llama 3 8B has a third as many layers as GPT-3 175B and a twentieth of the parameters, and is the far stronger model, because of its data and training. Day 6 is about choosing the numbers.
  • "Position embeddings are what let the model handle long inputs." They are what let it know order at all. Handling long inputs is about attention cost, RoPE's base frequency and long-context training (Day 11).

Going deeper (optional)

Two details that trip people up when they read real code. First, the Q, K and V projections are usually stored as one matrix of width 3 × d_model (2,304 in GPT-2) and sliced, which is why the table above lists them together. Second, "d_model = heads × head size" is a convention, not a law: some models give each head a larger size than d_model / heads and make W_O rectangular. The arithmetic of the parameter count still works; you just have to read the config rather than assume it. Finally, DeepSeek-V3 replaces per-head keys and values with a compressed latent vector (multi-head latent attention), which cuts the KV cache without changing the rest of the block DeepSeek 2024; Day 11 returns to it.

Check yourself

Q1. Which part of a Transformer block moves information between different tokens?

Answer 3. Only attention reads other positions. The MLP and the norm act on each token's vector alone, and the residual connection just adds a sub-layer's output back to the same position's stream.

Q2. Why do residual connections make a 96-layer network trainable?

Answer 2. The derivative of x + f(x) with respect to x is 1 + f′(x); the 1 is the highway. Residuals add no parameters, do not connect distant layers directly, and normalisation is a separate component.

Q3. With RoPE, the attention score between a query at position 10 and a key at position 7 depends on position through:

Answer 4. Both vectors are rotated by angles proportional to their positions; the dot product sees only the angle between them, which is set by the difference. That is why RoPE is called a relative encoding even though each vector is rotated by its absolute position.

Q4. Which single component of GPT-2 small holds the most parameters?

Answer 1. 50,257 × 768 is 38.6M, about 31% of the model. All 12 blocks together hold 85M, but no single block comes close to the embedding table, and the position table and norms are tiny.

Q5. Which of these is a real reason decoder-only models won for generation?

Answer 3. Parameter count is a choice, not a property of the family; bidirectional visibility is BERT's feature, not GPT's; and all three families need position information.

Q6. In the pre-norm arrangement used by GPT-2 and Llama, the normalisation is applied:

Answer 2. Pre-norm normalises what goes into attention and the MLP while the residual stream itself passes through un-normalised, which keeps gradients stable at initialisation. Post-norm (option 1) was the 2017 arrangement.

Recap: five things to remember

  1. Decoder-only Transformer = embedding → N × (attention, MLP, each with pre-norm and a residual add) → final norm → LM head. The shape has not changed since GPT-2; the numbers have.
  2. Attention is order-blind, so position is injected: learned tables (GPT-2/3), sinusoids (2017), RoPE rotations that make scores depend on relative distance (Llama and most open models), or ALiBi score penalties.
  3. Residual connections give gradients an identity path and let each block learn a correction; pre-norm keeps training stable without careful warm-up.
  4. Attention mixes across tokens; the MLP (4× wide, or gated SwiGLU) transforms each token alone and holds most of the parameters and much of the stored knowledge.
  5. GPT-2 small is 124M parameters: 38.6M in the embedding, 7.09M per block × 12, tied LM head. You can now count any model from its config.
