Next: Day 6 — Scaling laws: how big, how much data, how much compute

Week 1 · Day 6

Pretraining at scale

After today you can take a model size and a token count and estimate, on the back of an envelope, how much compute, time and money a pretraining run needs, and say where each number comes from.

About 90 min · Builds on Day 5

Today's big idea

Pretraining is one tiny loop, predict the next token and nudge the weights, repeated trillions of times on thousands of GPUs. Nothing in the loop is new since Day 3. Everything hard about it is engineering: cleaning the data, choosing how big a model and how many tokens, splitting one model across machines, and surviving the hardware failing under you.

1. From the web to a training set

The raw material is the web. Common Crawl is a nonprofit that has crawled the public web since 2007; its archive holds over 300 billion pages and grows by 3 to 5 billion pages a month. Common Crawl 2026 Most of it is useless for training: menus, ads, spam and endless copies of the same page. Turning the crawl into a dataset is a pipeline of filters.

The FineWeb team documented their pipeline in detail, so it makes a good worked example. Penedo 2024

  1. Text extraction. Pull the main article text out of the raw HTML. FineWeb ran the trafilatura extractor on the raw pages rather than using Common Crawl's pre-extracted text, which kept too much boilerplate.
  2. URL filtering. Drop pages from a blocklist of adult and spam domains.
  3. Language identification: a small classifier (fastText) guesses the language of each page. FineWeb kept a page as English only if the classifier was at least 65% confident. Meta's CCNet pipeline did the same for a hundred languages. Wenzek 2019
  4. Quality filtering: rules that catch junk. FineWeb reused the heuristics from DeepMind's MassiveText (the Gopher dataset) Rae 2021 and from Google's C4: drop pages with "lorem ipsum", with curly brackets (code dumps), with too many very short lines, with too few lines ending in punctuation. For FineWeb-Edu they added a learned filter: Llama-3-70B-Instruct graded about 460,000 pages for educational value on a 0 to 5 scale, a small classifier was trained to imitate those grades, and only pages scoring 3 or higher survived. That left 1.3 trillion tokens out of 15 trillion. Penedo 2024
  5. Deduplication: remove copies and near-copies. FineWeb used MinHash, a trick that hashes overlapping 5-word chunks so two documents that share about 75% of their chunks are flagged as duplicates without comparing them word by word. Why bother? Lee and colleagues showed that deduplication makes models regurgitate memorized text far less often and reach the same or better accuracy in fewer steps. Lee 2021
  6. Mixing: decide the proportions. Web text is not everything. Llama 3's pretraining mix was roughly 50% general knowledge, 25% maths and reasoning, 17% code and 8% multilingual text, and the proportions were chosen by training small models on candidate mixes and seeing which did best. Grattafiori 2024

Several open datasets let you see the result. Sizes are in tokens unless noted, and the token counts depend on the tokenizer used (see Day 2).

DatasetYearSizeWhat it is
The Pile2020825 GiB of text22 curated sources (papers, code, books, forums) from EleutherAI, the first widely used open mix. Gao 2020
RedPajama-V22023~30T (from >100T raw)84 Common Crawl snapshots, deduplicated, with 40+ precomputed quality signals so you can filter it your own way. Together 2023
Dolma20243TAI2's fully documented corpus behind the OLMo models. Soldaini 2024
FineWeb202415T96 Common Crawl snapshots through the pipeline above. Penedo 2024
FineWeb-Edu20241.3TThe educational subset; small models trained on it score higher on knowledge benchmarks. Penedo 2024

The so-what: two models with the same architecture and compute can differ a lot because one team filtered better, and none of that work is visible in the final model.

2. One training step, and the knobs on it

The objective has not changed since Day 1: predict the next token. For every position in every sequence, the model outputs a probability for each token in the vocabulary, and the loss is how surprised it was by the real one:

loss = −log p(correct next token), averaged over every position in the batch

Every token is a training example, so "trained on 15 trillion tokens" means 15 trillion of these little predictions were scored. One training step looks like this:

for step in range(total_steps):
    batch = next_batch(tokens_per_batch)        # e.g. 4M tokens as 1,024 sequences of 4,096
    logits = model(batch.inputs)                # forward pass
    loss = cross_entropy(logits, batch.targets) # inputs shifted by one token
    loss.backward()                             # backward pass: gradient for every weight
    clip_grad_norm_(model.parameters(), 1.0)    # gradient clipping
    lr = schedule(step)                         # warmup, then cosine decay
    optimizer.step(lr)                          # AdamW update
    optimizer.zero_grad()
    if step % save_every == 0:
        save_checkpoint(model, optimizer, step)

Three knobs on this loop matter enough to have names.

Batch size, measured in tokens, is how many tokens are scored before each weight update. GPT-3 175B used 3.2 million tokens per batch. Brown 2020 The first LLaMA models used 4 million. Touvron 2023 Llama 3 405B started at 4 million, doubled to 8 million after 252 million tokens, and doubled again to 16 million after 2.87 trillion tokens. Grattafiori 2024 Big batches keep thousands of GPUs busy and average out gradient noise; too big and each extra token adds little. Growing the batch during training is the compromise: small while the gradient changes fast, large once it settles.

Learning-rate warmup means starting the learning rate near zero and raising it linearly over the first few thousand steps. At step zero the weights are random and the gradients point in strange directions; a full-size step then can wreck the model before it learns anything. Cosine decay then lowers the rate smoothly along a half-cosine curve until the end of training, an idea borrowed from a 2016 paper on restarting learning rates. Loshchilov 2016 LLaMA used 2,000 warmup steps, decayed to 10% of the peak, with AdamW (β₁ = 0.9, β₂ = 0.95) and weight decay 0.1. Touvron 2023 Llama 3 405B used a peak of 8×10⁻⁵, 8,000 warmup steps, and a cosine decay to 8×10⁻⁷ over 1.2 million steps. Grattafiori 2024 The Day 3 widget showed what too high a learning rate does: the loss diverges. Warmup and decay are the two ends of the same safety rule.

Gradient clipping: if the gradient vector is longer than a limit (almost always 1.0), shrink it to that length before the update. One bad batch, a corrupted file, a block of random bytes, can produce a huge gradient; clipping stops it from throwing the weights across the room. The trick comes from the recurrent-network era, where exploding gradients were a daily problem. Pascanu 2012 LLaMA clipped at 1.0. Touvron 2023

3. How much compute does a run need?

A FLOP is one floating-point operation, a multiply or an add. Training compute has a formula you can do in your head:

training FLOPs ≈ 6 · N · D

N is the number of parameters and D the number of training tokens. Where does the 6 come from? The forward pass uses every parameter in one multiply and one add per token, 2 FLOPs; the backward pass costs about twice that, another 4. Attention adds a little on top, small enough to ignore at normal context lengths. Kaplan 2020

Worked example: a 7-billion-parameter model on 2 trillion tokens.

6 × 7×10⁹ × 2×10¹² = 8.4×10²² FLOPs

Now the hardware. NVIDIA lists the H100 SXM at 1,979 TFLOPS for bf16 matrix maths, but that figure assumes structured sparsity, which dense training does not use, so the real peak is half: about 989 TFLOPS, close enough to 10¹⁵ FLOP/s. NVIDIA 2026 No run reaches peak. The share you get is called model FLOPs utilisation (MFU): useful FLOPs per second divided by the chip's peak. The PaLM paper introduced the measure and reported 21% for GPT-3, 33% for Gopher and 46% for PaLM itself. Chowdhery 2022 Llama 3 reported 38 to 43% on up to 16,000 H100s. Grattafiori 2024 Take 40% as the planning number: 4×10¹⁴ useful FLOP/s per GPU.

8.4×10²² ÷ 4×10¹⁴ = 2.1×10⁸ GPU-seconds ≈ 58,000 GPU-hours

On 1,024 GPUs that is about 57 hours, two and a half days. At the on-demand price a specialist cloud listed in October 2026, $3.99 per H100-hour, Lambda 2026 the compute alone is about $230,000. Big labs own their hardware or pay less; DeepSeek priced its V3 run at an assumed $2 per GPU-hour, and 2.788 million H800 GPU-hours came to $5.6 million. DeepSeek 2024 None of these figures include salaries, failed runs, data work or post-training, which is why "it cost $5 million" headlines are always too low.

Cross-check the formula against a real run. Llama 3 405B: 6 × 405×10⁹ × 15.6×10¹² = 3.8×10²⁵, and the paper reports 3.8×10²⁵ FLOPs. Grattafiori 2024 For the 8B model the formula gives about 500,000 GPU-hours at 40% MFU, and Meta's model card reports 1.3 million. Meta 2024 Reported totals are usually higher than the napkin number, because the napkin assumes steady utilisation and ignores restarts and evaluation runs. Treat 6·N·D as a floor, not a bill.

4. Scaling laws: how big, and how many tokens?

Given a compute budget, you face a trade: a bigger model on fewer tokens, or a smaller model on more? A scaling law is a fitted curve that answers this by training many small runs and extrapolating. Read the scaling-laws paper review for the full story; here is the short version.

Kaplan and colleagues at OpenAI found in 2020 that loss falls as a smooth power law in parameters, data and compute, across seven orders of magnitude. Their fit also said that for a fixed budget you should spend most of it on a bigger model and stop training early. Kaplan 2020 GPT-3 followed that advice: 175 billion parameters on only 300 billion tokens, under two tokens per parameter. Brown 2020

DeepMind redid the experiment in 2022 with one fix: they set the learning-rate schedule to match each run's length, rather than reusing one long schedule for short runs. The answer flipped. Parameters and tokens should grow in equal proportion. To prove it they trained Chinchilla, 70 billion parameters on 1.4 trillion tokens, with the same compute as their earlier Gopher, 280 billion parameters on 300 billion tokens, and Chinchilla won on almost every benchmark. Hoffmann 2022 1.4 trillion divided by 70 billion is 20, and "about 20 tokens per parameter" became the rule of thumb for a compute-optimal model: the lowest loss you can reach for a given number of training FLOPs.

Then the rule was broken on purpose. Llama 3 8B was trained on over 15 trillion tokens, nearly 1,900 tokens per parameter, a hundred times past Chinchilla-optimal. Meta wrote that the compute-optimal amount for an 8B model is about 200 billion tokens and that the model kept improving "even after the model is trained on two orders of magnitude more data". Meta 2024 Why train inefficiently? Because Chinchilla only counts training. Once deployed, every token a model serves costs about 2·N FLOPs, and a model that answers billions of requests pays that bill many times over; a smaller model trained longer is cheaper on every one of them. Sardana and colleagues extended the Chinchilla analysis to include expected inference demand and found exactly this: the more you plan to serve, the smaller and more overtrained the model should be. Sardana 2023 The Llama 3 paper review has the details of that run.

Try it: set 8B parameters and 15T tokens below and read off the FLOPs and GPU-hours; then drag the tokens back to the Chinchilla line (about 160B) and see how much cheaper the run gets and how much higher the loss curve sits.

Notice that moving along the Chinchilla line keeps training cheap for a given loss, while sliding right (more tokens, same size) buys a lower loss at a price. The log-log curves are straight lines: that straightness is what "power law" means, and it is what lets labs predict a big run from small ones.

5. Spreading one model over thousands of GPUs

Two reasons a model does not fit on one GPU. Time: 58,000 GPU-hours on one card is six and a half years. Memory: in training each parameter needs far more than two bytes. With mixed-precision Adam you store a 16-bit weight, a 16-bit gradient, a 32-bit master copy, and two 32-bit optimizer moments: 16 bytes per parameter. Rajbhandari 2019 A 7B model needs 112 GB before counting activations, and an H100 has 80 GB. NVIDIA 2026 So the model, and the work, must be split. There are four standard ways, and real runs combine them.

Data parallelism: every GPU holds a full copy of the model and processes a different slice of the batch. After the backward pass the GPUs average their gradients (an operation called all-reduce) so every copy takes the same step. Simple, but it does nothing for memory: each GPU still needs the whole model.

Tensor parallelism: split each weight matrix into slices, one per GPU, so each GPU computes part of every layer and the partial results are stitched together. This needs communication inside every layer, so it is used only between GPUs with very fast links, typically the eight in one server. NVIDIA's Megatron-LM showed how to do it for Transformer layers. Shoeybi 2019

Pipeline parallelism: put layers 1 to 10 on one GPU, 11 to 20 on the next, and so on, and pass activations down the line. To keep later GPUs from idling, the batch is cut into micro-batches that flow through like an assembly line; the idle time that remains is the pipeline bubble. GPipe introduced the scheme. Huang 2018

ZeRO and FSDP: data parallelism without the redundancy. Instead of every GPU holding every optimizer state, gradient and parameter, each GPU holds a shard, and the full weights of a layer are gathered just in time for its forward and backward pass, then released. Memory per GPU falls roughly in proportion to the number of GPUs. Microsoft's ZeRO described the idea in three stages, Rajbhandari 2019 and PyTorch's Fully Sharded Data Parallel is the mainstream implementation. Zhao 2023 PyTorch 2026

Llama 3 405B used all of these at once, plus context parallelism, which splits very long sequences across GPUs, on up to 16,000 H100s. Grattafiori 2024

Analogy

Think of a kitchen with many cooks. Data parallelism is several identical kitchens each cooking the same menu for different tables, then comparing notes. Tensor parallelism is several cooks chopping the same mountain of onions, each taking a share. Pipeline parallelism is an assembly line: one cook preps, the next fries, the next plates, and it only works if plates keep flowing. FSDP is a shared pantry: each cook stores one shelf of ingredients, and when a dish needs the full set, everyone passes their shelf over for a moment and takes it back.

6. Making the numbers fit: mixed precision and checkpoints

Every number in training has a format. 32-bit floats (fp32) are the safe default; 16-bit floats halve the memory and, on tensor cores, run far faster. Mixed precision does the heavy matrix maths in 16 bits while keeping an fp32 master copy of the weights for the small, precise updates. Micikevicius 2017 fp16 has a tiny range, so small gradients underflowed to zero and had to be rescaled. bf16 (brain float) spends its 16 bits differently, keeping fp32's 8-bit exponent and giving up precision in the fraction, so it covers the same range without rescaling. It is today's default; Llama 3 reports its MFU in bf16. Grattafiori 2024 The next halving is fp8: NVIDIA proposed two 8-bit formats in 2022 and showed training matched 16-bit accuracy on models up to 175B, Micikevicius 2022 and DeepSeek-V3, a 671B-parameter model, was trained with fp8 mixed precision with a loss that stayed within 0.25% of a bf16 baseline. DeepSeek 2024 Each halving roughly doubles throughput and halves memory traffic, which is why precision keeps falling.

A checkpoint is a snapshot of everything needed to resume: weights, optimizer state, the data position, the random seeds. At 16 bytes per parameter a 405B checkpoint is around 6.5 TB, and writing it stalls thousands of GPUs, so there is a real trade between saving often (less lost work when something breaks) and saving rarely (less time stalled). The Llama 3 team wrote that they tuned their storage so they could checkpoint more often with less pause time. Grattafiori 2024

Going deeper (optional)

Do not confuse checkpoints with activation checkpointing, an unrelated memory trick: keep only a few layers' activations from the forward pass and recompute the rest during the backward pass, trading about a third more compute for a large cut in memory. Most big runs use it, which is why the PaLM paper counts "model FLOPs" separately from "hardware FLOPs". Chowdhery 2022

7. When the machines break

With 16,000 GPUs, something is always failing. The Llama 3 paper gives the clearest public numbers: during a 54-day stretch of 405B pretraining there were 466 job interruptions, 47 planned and 419 unexpected. About 78% of the unexpected ones were confirmed or suspected hardware problems, and GPU faults alone were 58.7%. Despite this, effective training time stayed above 90%. Grattafiori 2024 Do the division: 419 unexpected stops in 54 days is almost eight a day, one every three hours, around the clock. Each one stops the whole job; the bad machine is swapped out and training resumes from the last checkpoint, redoing whatever was computed since.

That is why checkpoint frequency, automatic restart and fast fault diagnosis are a research topic in their own right: a run that loses 10% of its time to failures loses 10% of a budget measured in tens of millions of dollars. Meta reported more than 95% effective training time for the earlier Llama 3 runs on its 24,000-GPU clusters. Meta 2024

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine 16,000 kids each colouring one square of a giant picture that has to be finished by Friday. Every few hours one kid's crayon snaps, and the rule is that everyone must stop and go back to the last photo the teacher took of the picture. So the teacher takes photos often, keeps spare crayons ready, and has a helper whose only job is to spot a broken crayon in seconds. Training a big model is like that, except the kids are computers and the picture is a trillion tiny guesses about the next word.

8. Emergent abilities, or a trick of the ruler?

Scaling laws describe loss, which falls smoothly. But what people care about are abilities: doing arithmetic, following instructions, answering trivia. In 2022 a group of Google researchers catalogued tasks where accuracy sat near zero for small models and then jumped sharply at some scale, and called these emergent abilities: abilities "not present in smaller models but present in larger models", which cannot be predicted by extrapolating from the small ones. Wei 2022 If true, this is unsettling: you cannot know what a model will be able to do until you have spent the money.

A year later Stanford researchers argued that most of the jumps are a measurement artifact. The tasks that looked emergent were scored with all-or-nothing metrics such as exact match: a five-digit sum is right or wrong, so a model that gets four digits right scores the same as one that gets none. Under such a metric a smooth improvement in per-token accuracy looks like nothing, nothing, nothing, then a cliff. Score the same models with a continuous metric, such as how many digits are right, and the curves become smooth and predictable. They also showed they could manufacture "emergence" on ordinary vision tasks just by picking a harsh enough metric. Schaeffer 2023

The debate matters less for whether abilities arrive with scale (both sides agree they do) than for whether they can be forecast. The practical answer is that labs now fit scaling laws to benchmarks, not just to loss: the Llama 3 team predicted the 405B model's ARC Challenge score from small runs by fitting one curve from compute to the loss on the benchmark's answers, then a second from that loss to accuracy. Grattafiori 2024 Which is to say: with the right ruler, most of the magic is a straight line on log paper.

9. Reading the loss curve

The number everyone watches during a run is the training loss, the average −log p(correct token), measured in nats (natural-log units) per token. It is easier to feel as perplexity, e raised to the loss, which you can read as "the model is choosing between this many equally likely tokens". Wikipedia

perplexity = e^loss loss 2.0 → 7.4 loss 1.8 → 6.0

So "loss 2.0 versus 1.8" means the model went from being torn between about seven plausible next tokens to about six. Put in bits, 0.2 nats is 0.29 bits per token. That sounds like nothing. But the easy tokens (the "the", the closing bracket, the second half of a common word) are learned in the first few billion tokens; everything after that is spent on the hard ones, which is why the curve is almost flat for most of a run and why a tenth of a nat is worth millions of dollars at the top end. The shape is always the same: a fast drop in the first percent of training, a long slow power-law tail, the occasional spike when a bad batch or an instability hits, and a last visible dip when the cosine decay brings the learning rate down. The LLaMA paper's first figure shows this for four model sizes: bigger models sit lower along the whole curve, and all are still falling when training stops. Touvron 2023

Two cautions. Loss is only comparable between runs with the same tokenizer and the same held-out data: a bigger vocabulary packs more information into each token, so loss per token is higher even if the model is better. And loss is a proxy: nobody can tell from loss alone whether a model can write a working function.

Common misunderstandings

  • "Bigger model always wins." Chinchilla (70B) beat Gopher (280B) with the same compute by training on four times the data. Size, data and compute trade off.
  • "6·N·D tells you the cost." It tells you the floor in FLOPs. Real runs pay for utilisation below peak, restarts, evaluation, failed experiments and people.
  • "The model saw the whole internet." It saw a filtered, deduplicated subset. FineWeb kept 15T tokens from 96 crawls, and the educational filter kept under a tenth of that.
  • "Emergence means we cannot predict anything." Loss is predictable, and with continuous metrics most abilities are too. The argument is about the ruler, not about whether the abilities exist.

Check yourself

Q1. Using FLOPs ≈ 6·N·D, roughly how much compute did Chinchilla (70B parameters, 1.4T tokens) use?

Answer 2. 6 × 7×10¹⁰ × 1.4×10¹² = 5.9×10²³. The 10²⁵ figure is Llama 3 405B's scale; the others are off by a thousand or more.

Q2. The Chinchilla rule of thumb for a compute-optimal model is about how many training tokens per parameter?

Answer 3. Chinchilla trained 70B on 1.4T tokens, 20 per parameter. GPT-3 used under 2 (too few by this rule); Llama 3 8B used nearly 1,900 (overtrained on purpose for cheaper inference).

Q3. Why do training runs start with learning-rate warmup?

Answer 1. Warmup protects the weights while gradients are still noisy and ill-directed. The loss being high is not itself the reason: later in training you want large steps while the loss is still high. Hardware and data loading have nothing to do with it.

Q4. Which strategy splits a single weight matrix across several GPUs so each computes part of every layer?

Answer 4. Tensor parallelism slices matrices inside a layer. Pipeline parallelism assigns whole layers to different GPUs; data parallelism copies the whole model and splits the batch; clipping is not a parallelism strategy at all.

Q5. What was the central claim of Schaeffer et al. (2023) about emergent abilities?

Answer 2. The paper argues the sharpness comes from the ruler (exact-match scoring), not from the model. It does not claim abilities are absent at scale or present in small models; it claims the curve is smooth when measured well.

Q6. A model's loss is 2.0 nats per token. Its perplexity is about:

Answer 3. Perplexity = e^loss = e² ≈ 7.4: the model is roughly choosing among seven equally likely tokens. 4 would be 2² (the wrong base); 2 confuses loss with perplexity.

Recap: five things to remember

  1. Pretraining data is a filtering pipeline: extract, language-ID, quality-filter, deduplicate, mix. Open examples: The Pile, RedPajama, Dolma, FineWeb (15T tokens), FineWeb-Edu.
  2. One training step is forward, loss, backward, clip, AdamW update, on batches of millions of tokens, with learning-rate warmup then cosine decay.
  3. Compute ≈ 6·N·D FLOPs; divide by about 4×10¹⁴ useful FLOP/s per H100 (40% MFU) to get GPU-seconds. A 7B model on 2T tokens is about 58,000 GPU-hours.
  4. Chinchilla: about 20 tokens per parameter is compute-optimal for training; modern models are overtrained far past that because a smaller model is cheaper to serve.
  5. Big runs combine data, tensor, pipeline and sharded (FSDP) parallelism, run in bf16 or fp8, checkpoint constantly, and expect a hardware failure every few hours.

References

  1. Scaling Laws for Neural Language Models — Kaplan et al., OpenAI, 2020. The power laws and the 6·N·D accounting; read Sections 1 and 2.
  2. Training Compute-Optimal Large Language Models — Hoffmann et al., DeepMind, 2022. The Chinchilla paper; Table 3 is the "how many tokens" lookup table.
  3. The Llama 3 Herd of Models — Grattafiori et al., Meta, 2024. Section 3 is the most detailed public account of a frontier pretraining run, including the failure statistics.
  4. The FineWeb Datasets: Decanting the Web for the Finest Text Data at Scale — Penedo et al., Hugging Face, 2024. Every filtering decision, with ablations showing what each one bought.
  5. The Pile: An 800GB Dataset of Diverse Text for Language Modeling — Gao et al., EleutherAI, 2020. The first big open mix; good for seeing what "diverse sources" meant in 2020.
  6. Dolma: an Open Corpus of Three Trillion Tokens — Soldaini et al., AI2, 2024. Fully documented data pipeline with released tooling.
  7. RedPajama-Data-v2 — Together AI, 2023. 30T deduplicated tokens with precomputed quality signals.
  8. Deduplicating Training Data Makes Language Models Better — Lee et al., Google, 2021. Why dedup is worth the trouble: less memorisation, same accuracy sooner.
  9. ZeRO: Memory Optimizations Toward Training Trillion Parameter Models — Rajbhandari et al., Microsoft, 2019. The 16-bytes-per-parameter accounting and the three sharding stages.
  10. Megatron-LM: Training Multi-Billion Parameter Language Models Using Model Parallelism — Shoeybi et al., NVIDIA, 2019. Tensor parallelism for Transformers, with the diagrams that every later paper copies.
  11. PyTorch FSDP: Experiences on Scaling Fully Sharded Data Parallel — Zhao et al., Meta, 2023. The mainstream implementation of sharded data parallelism.
  12. DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report — DeepSeek-AI, 2024. FP8 training at scale and a frank cost table.
  13. Are Emergent Abilities of Large Language Models a Mirage? — Schaeffer, Miranda, Koyejo, Stanford, NeurIPS 2023. Short, and the figures make the argument on their own.
  14. Let's reproduce GPT-2 (124M) — Andrej Karpathy, 2024. Four hours of watching a real training loop get built, including mixed precision and the learning-rate schedule.
  15. CS336: Language Modeling from Scratch — Stanford, 2025. The university course that covers today's material in full, with assignments.

Next: Day 7 — Inference: how a model actually writes

Week 1 · Day 7

Inference: how a model actually writes

After today you can explain every setting on a model's "generate" call, say why generating text is slow in a specific and calculable way, and estimate what a request costs in memory, time and dollars.

About 90 min · Builds on Day 6

Today's big idea

A model does not write sentences; it produces a probability for every token in its vocabulary, one position at a time, and a sampler picks one. Generating is slow not because the maths is hard but because every single token requires streaming all the weights through the chip again, which makes inference a memory-bandwidth problem, and most of the clever engineering of the last three years is about moving fewer bytes per token.

1. From logits to a choice

Run the Transformer from Day 5 on a prompt and the final layer produces, for the last position, one raw score per vocabulary entry. These scores are the logits: unnormalised numbers, positive or negative, one for each of the roughly 128,000 tokens in Llama 3's vocabulary. Grattafiori 2024 Softmax, the same function as in attention on Day 4, turns them into probabilities that are all positive and sum to one:

p(token i) = exp(zᵢ) ÷ Σⱼ exp(zⱼ)

Now the model has a probability distribution over the next token and someone has to pick. Greedy decoding always takes the most probable token. It is deterministic and fine for short factual answers, but over long outputs it falls into loops, repeating a phrase because the phrase was the most likely continuation of itself. Hugging Face 2026 Sampling draws a token at random in proportion to its probability: a token with p = 0.3 is chosen three times in ten. This is where variety, and all the dials, come from. Whichever way a token is chosen, it is appended to the sequence, the model runs again on the longer sequence, and the loop repeats until a stop condition.

2. The sampling knobs

Temperature is a number T that divides the logits before the softmax:

p(token i) = exp(zᵢ ÷ T) ÷ Σⱼ exp(zⱼ ÷ T)

Take three tokens with logits 2.0, 1.0 and 0.5. At T = 1 their probabilities are 0.63, 0.23 and 0.14. At T = 0.5 the logits are doubled before softmax, which exaggerates the gaps: 0.84, 0.11 and 0.04. At T = 2 the logits are halved and the gaps shrink: 0.48, 0.29 and 0.23. As T approaches 0 the top token gets all the mass and sampling becomes greedy; as T grows every token approaches equal probability and the output becomes noise. Intuition: temperature does not change which token the model prefers, it changes how strongly it insists. Low temperature for code and facts, around 0.7 to 1.0 for conversation, higher for brainstorming.

Top-k keeps only the k most probable tokens and renormalises before sampling; k = 50 is a common default. It was introduced for story generation, where sampling from the full distribution occasionally picked absurd tokens from the long tail. Fan 2018 Its weakness is that k is fixed while the distribution is not: when the model is confident, the top 50 includes 45 junk tokens; when it is genuinely unsure between 200 reasonable words, 150 of them are cut.

Top-p, also called nucleus sampling, fixes that. Sort the tokens by probability, keep the smallest set whose probabilities add up to at least p (say 0.9), and sample from that set. When the model is sure, the nucleus is one or two tokens; when it is unsure, it widens. The paper that introduced it also made the case that pure likelihood maximisation produces dull, repetitive text, and that human text is full of tokens that were not the most likely. Holtzman 2019

Min-p is newer: keep every token whose probability is at least a fraction of the top token's probability. With min-p = 0.1 and a top token at 0.6, anything below 0.06 is cut; if the top token is at 0.05, anything below 0.005 is cut. The threshold scales with the model's confidence, which keeps output coherent even at high temperatures, where top-p starts to let noise through. Nguyen 2024

Repetition penalty divides the logit of every token that has already appeared in the text by a factor θ (so a positive logit shrinks). The CTRL paper that introduced it found θ ≈ 1.2 "yields a good balance between truthful generation and lack of repetition". Keskar 2019 It is a blunt tool: it also penalises words that must repeat, like a variable name in code, so most chat deployments use it lightly or not at all. All of these knobs are standard arguments of a generation call. Hugging Face 2026

Try it: the widget below starts from a fixed set of logits. Lower the temperature and watch the tallest bar swallow the others; raise it and watch them flatten. Then set top-p to 0.9 and see which bars survive at each temperature. Press "sample" a few times at each setting.

Notice that temperature changes the shape of the whole distribution, while top-k and top-p only decide where to cut it. Notice, too, that at T = 1 with no cutoff, the low bars still get picked now and then; that is the long tail nucleus sampling was invented to trim.

3. Beam search, and knowing when to stop

Beam search is the opposite philosophy to sampling: instead of picking one token at a time, keep the B most probable partial sequences (the beams), extend each by every candidate, keep the best B again, and at the end return the sequence with the highest total probability. It is the standard decoder for machine translation and speech recognition, where there is a right answer and you want the most probable one. Hugging Face 2026 For chat it is almost never used, for three reasons. The most probable long text is usually a bad one: Holtzman and colleagues showed beam search output from GPT-2 collapsing into repeated phrases while humans wrote text full of lower-probability choices. Holtzman 2019 Worse, in translation the single most probable output under the model is often the empty string, over half the time in one study, so an exact search finds "nothing" and beam search only works because it fails to search well. Stahlberg 2019 And it costs B times the compute per token, which matters when you are serving millions of users.

How does the model know it has finished? With a stop token: a special vocabulary entry, often called end-of-sequence (EOS) or end-of-turn, that the model learned to emit when a document or a reply ends. The generation loop checks each new token against the configured stop ids and halts when it sees one. Hugging Face 2026 That is all "the model decided to stop talking" means. Chat models learn their end-of-turn token during post-training (Day 8); if you fine-tune one and forget to include that token in your examples, it will never stop. There is also a hard ceiling, the maximum number of new tokens, which is why answers sometimes cut off mid-sentence.

4. Prefill, decode, and why decoding is slow

A generation request has two phases that behave completely differently. Prefill processes the whole prompt at once: every prompt token goes through every layer in one big parallel pass, the same kind of matrix-matrix multiplication as training. The chip's arithmetic units are the bottleneck; prefill is compute-bound. Decode then produces output tokens one at a time, and each step needs only one new token's worth of maths but must read every weight of the model from memory to do it. NVIDIA 2023

Put numbers on it. Llama 3 8B in bf16 is 16 GB of weights. An H100 moves 3.35 TB/s between its memory and its compute units. NVIDIA 2026 Reading 16 GB takes 16 ÷ 3350 ≈ 4.8 milliseconds, so one request alone can never go faster than about 210 tokens per second on that chip, whatever the arithmetic speed. Meanwhile the arithmetic for one token is 2 × 8×10⁹ = 1.6×10¹⁰ FLOPs, which at 10¹⁵ FLOP/s takes 16 microseconds. The chip spends 99.7% of each decode step waiting for bytes. Decode is memory-bandwidth-bound; this is the single most important fact about inference and it explains almost everything that follows. Pope 2022

It also explains the two numbers every serving dashboard shows. Time to first token (TTFT) is mostly prefill time, so it grows with prompt length: a 100,000-token prompt has to be pushed through the model before anything comes back. Tokens per second during the answer is the decode rate, set by how fast the weights (and, as we are about to see, the cache) can be streamed. NVIDIA 2023

Analogy

Decoding is a librarian who, to write each word of a letter, must walk the entire library once, touching every book, and then writes one word. The walk takes the same time whether she writes one letter or thirty letters at once, so the only way to go faster is to write many letters per lap (batching), carry fewer books (quantisation, Day 10), or send a fast assistant ahead to guess the next few words so she can check several per lap (speculative decoding, Section 7).

5. The KV cache

Attention lets each position look at every earlier position through their key and value vectors. During decode, the earlier positions have not changed, so recomputing their keys and values every step would be pure waste. The KV cache stores the key and value vectors of every token processed so far, for every layer, and each new token simply appends its own. Hugging Face 2026 Without it, generating token n would cost about n times more than with it. With it, the cost is memory, and the memory is easy to compute:

bytes per token = 2 (K and V) × layers × kv_heads × head_dim × 2 (bytes in bf16)

Llama 3 8B has 32 layers, 32 attention heads but only 8 key-value heads, and a head dimension of 128 (4,096 ÷ 32). Llama 3 70B has 80 layers, 64 heads, 8 key-value heads, and the same head dimension of 128. Grattafiori 2024

ModelLayersKV headsHead dimPer token8k context128k context
Llama 3 8B3281282×32×8×128×2 = 131,072 B = 128 KiB1 GiB16 GiB
Llama 3 70B8081282×80×8×128×2 = 327,680 B = 320 KiB2.5 GiB40 GiB
8B if it had 32 KV heads3232128512 KiB4 GiB64 GiB

Read the table twice. At 8k context the cache is small change. At 128k, one request on the 8B model needs a 16 GiB cache, as large as the weights themselves, and one 70B request needs 40 GiB, half an H100. Multiply by the number of users served at once and the cache, not the model, is what fills the GPU. The third row shows why modern models use grouped-query attention: sharing each key-value head between several query heads (8 KV heads serving 32 or 64 query heads) cuts the cache four- to eight-fold with little loss in quality. Ainslie 2023 And since decode is bandwidth-bound, every byte of cache is also read on every step, so a big cache makes each token slower as well as the GPU fuller.

Try it: pick a model size and a context length below and watch the cache grow token by token. Compare 8B at 8k with 70B at 128k, then switch the KV-head count and see the bars change.

Notice that the cache grows linearly with the number of tokens, that the model's weights are a fixed cost while the cache is a per-request cost, and how quickly a long context on a big model crosses the 80 GB line of a single GPU.

6. Serving many users at once

If one decode step reads all 16 GB of weights to produce one token, it can just as well produce sixty-four tokens for sixty-four different users in nearly the same time: the weights are read once, the arithmetic is still tiny. Batching is how inference gets anywhere near the chip's capacity, and it is why a busy API is far cheaper per token than a GPU running one chat. The limit is memory: every user in the batch brings their own KV cache.

Naive batching has a problem: requests finish at different times, and a batch assembled at the start must wait for its longest member while finished slots sit empty. Continuous batching (the Orca system called it iteration-level scheduling) re-plans at every decode step: finished sequences leave, waiting ones join, and no slot idles. Yu 2022

The other problem is where the caches live. Early servers reserved a contiguous block of memory for each request's maximum possible length, so memory sat unused and could not be shared; the vLLM team measured 60 to 80% of KV memory wasted this way. vLLM 2023 PagedAttention borrows the operating-system idea of virtual memory: the cache is kept in small fixed-size blocks scattered wherever there is room, with a lookup table mapping each sequence's logical positions to physical blocks. Blocks are allocated as tokens arrive, freed the moment a request ends, and shared between requests that start with the same prompt. The result was 2 to 4 times the throughput of the best previous systems at the same latency, Kwon 2023 and vLLM became the default open-source serving engine. Its documentation lists what a serving engine in 2026 is expected to do: paged caches, continuous batching, prefix caching, speculative decoding and a long menu of quantisation formats. vLLM 2026

7. Speculative decoding

Here is a trick that follows directly from decode being bandwidth-bound. Verifying k candidate tokens in one forward pass costs about the same as generating one, because the weights are read once either way. So let a small, fast draft model guess the next few tokens, then have the big model score all of them in a single pass. Tokens the big model agrees with are accepted; at the first disagreement the big model's own choice is used and drafting restarts from there. With a rejection-sampling rule for the acceptances, the output distribution is exactly the big model's, not an approximation. The draft is right often enough on easy tokens (the second half of a word, the closing bracket) that two papers in late 2022 and early 2023 reported 2 to 3× faster generation with identical outputs, Leviathan 2022 including 2 to 2.5× on the 70B Chinchilla model. Chen 2023 Modern variants replace the separate draft model with small extra heads on the main model or with n-gram lookups in the prompt; vLLM ships several. vLLM 2026 The catch: the speedup shrinks when the batch is already large, because then the GPU is no longer idle between tokens.

8. Long context: what it costs

A model's context window is the maximum number of tokens it can hold at once, prompt plus answer. Llama 3.1 supports 128k; Grattafiori 2024 Google's Gemini 1.5 report tested retrieval at up to 10 million tokens. Gemini Team 2024 Long context is not free, and it costs in three distinct places. Prefill: attention compares every token with every other, so prefill work grows with the square of the prompt length; doubling the prompt quadruples that part of the time-to-first-token, and making this tolerable is what the FlashAttention paper review is about. Memory: the KV cache grows linearly, 128 KiB per token for an 8B model, which is Section 5's table. Decode: each new token attends to the whole cache, so a long context makes every subsequent token slower too.

Providers pass these costs on. As of October 2026, Google charges $2 per million input tokens for Gemini 3.1 Pro on prompts up to 200k tokens and $4 above that, Google 2026 and Anthropic's Haiku 5.5 moves from $0.10 to $0.50 per million input tokens once a prompt passes 100k. Anthropic 2026 There is also a quality cost: models have been shown to use information at the start and end of a long context better than information buried in the middle, Liu 2023 which is one reason retrieval (Day 12) did not disappear when windows got long.

9. Running a model on your own machine

None of this needs a data centre. llama.cpp is a C/C++ inference engine with no dependencies that runs on laptops, phones and servers, ggml-org 2026 and it reads models in the GGUF format, a single binary file designed for fast loading that usually contains quantised weights. ggml-org 2026 Ollama wraps it in a one-command installer and model library, Ollama 2026 and LM Studio adds a desktop chat window and a local API server. LM Studio 2026

What fits? The rule of thumb is: file size of the model plus the KV cache plus a gigabyte or two of overhead must fit in your GPU memory, or in unified memory on a Mac or a phone. Ollama's library lists Llama 3.1 8B at 4.9 GB, 70B at 43 GB and 405B at 243 GB; the 8B file is a third of the 16 GB the bf16 weights would take, which tells you the weights are stored at roughly four bits each. Ollama 2026 So an 8B model runs comfortably on a 16 GB laptop, a 70B model needs a machine with 64 GB of unified memory or several GPUs, and 405B needs a rack. Speed follows Section 4's rule: a 4.9 GB model on a laptop with about 100 GB/s of memory bandwidth (a typical figure for a thin laptop, as an estimate) tops out near 20 tokens per second for a single chat, which is faster than most people read. For phones, Meta released 1B and 3B versions of Llama 3.2 tuned for Qualcomm, MediaTek and Arm processors, with a 128k context, Meta 2024 and 4-bit quantisation (Day 10) is what makes a 3B model's weights fit in about 2 GB.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a huge book of rules for guessing the next word, so huge that reading it once takes a few milliseconds even for a computer. To write one word, the computer reads the whole book, guesses, writes the word, and then reads the whole book again for the next word. That is why answers appear one word at a time. The computer also keeps a notepad of everything in the conversation so far so it does not have to re-read the conversation each time; the notepad gets bigger with every word, and for very long conversations the notepad can get bigger than the book. To go faster you can make the book smaller, answer many people with each reading, or let a little helper guess a few words ahead and only check them.

10. What it costs through an API

When you use a model through an API you pay per token, with separate prices for input (your prompt, charged at prefill) and output (the answer, charged at decode). Output costs more, typically five times more, because decode is the expensive, bandwidth-bound phase and output tokens cannot be batched as efficiently as prompts. Prices move often; these are the standard list prices on each provider's own pricing page as of October 2026, in US dollars per million tokens, excluding batch, cached-input and long-prompt tiers.

ProviderModelInput $/MOutput $/MNote
OpenAIgpt-6-astra10.0050.00Flagship. OpenAI 2026
OpenAIgpt-6.1-sol2.0010.00Mid tier.
OpenAIgpt-6-luna0.100.50Small tier.
AnthropicClaude Fable 5.110.0050.00Flagship. Anthropic 2026
AnthropicClaude Opus 5.54.0020.00
AnthropicClaude Sonnet 5.52.0010.00
AnthropicClaude Haiku 5.50.100.50Prompts up to 100k tokens.
GoogleGemini 4 Argon2.0010.00Introductory price to 30 Nov 2026, then 4.00 / 20.00. Google 2026
GoogleGemini 3.8 Flash0.753.75Introductory price to 31 Dec 2026.
GoogleGemini 3.5 Flash-Lite0.302.50
DeepSeekdeepseek-v4-pro1.323.96Peak hours; half price off-peak. DeepSeek 2026
DeepSeekdeepseek-flash0.301.20Peak hours; cache-hit input far cheaper.

Worked example: a 1,000-token prompt with a 500-token answer on Claude Sonnet 5.5 costs 1,000 ÷ 10⁶ × $2 + 500 ÷ 10⁶ × $10 = $0.002 + $0.005 = $0.007. The same request on the flagship tier is five times that. Two consequences are worth remembering. First, a chat is re-sent in full on every turn, so a long conversation's cost grows with the square of its length unless the provider caches the shared prefix, which is exactly the prefix sharing PagedAttention made cheap, and most providers now sell cached input at a steep discount. Second, agents that loop (Day 12) spend most of their money on input tokens, re-reading their own history, not on the answers.

Going deeper (optional)

Why is the ratio of output to input price about 5:1 across providers? Prefill of a 1,000-token prompt is one compute-bound pass that keeps the chip busy; decoding 1,000 tokens is 1,000 bandwidth-bound passes, each reading the full weights and a growing cache, and the chip is idle most of the time unless the batch is large. The price ratio is a rough measure of how much harder it is to keep GPUs busy during decode. It also tells you where the engineering effort goes: speculative decoding, quantised caches, and grouped-query attention are all attempts to make the output column cheaper.

Common misunderstandings

  • "Temperature 0 makes the model deterministic." It makes the sampler greedy, which is deterministic in principle, but batched floating-point arithmetic on GPUs can still vary from run to run, so two identical requests can differ in a late token.
  • "Decoding is slow because the model has to think." It is slow because each token requires streaming all the weights through the chip; the arithmetic per token takes microseconds. More parameters means more bytes, which is the real reason big models are slow.
  • "A bigger context window is free as long as it fits." Prefill work grows with the square of the prompt and the KV cache grows linearly; providers charge more above a threshold for exactly this reason.
  • "Speculative decoding changes the answers." With the standard acceptance rule the output distribution is identical to the big model's; only the speed changes.
  • "Beam search gives the best text because it finds the most probable sequence." The most probable sequence is usually repetitive and bland, and in translation it is often the empty string. Chat models sample instead.

Check yourself

Q1. What happens to the next-token distribution as temperature approaches zero?

Answer 4. Dividing logits by a tiny T stretches the gaps between them enormously, so the softmax puts nearly all its mass on the top token. Equal probability is what happens as T grows large; top-k is a separate knob.

Q2. With top-p = 0.9, which tokens can be sampled?

Answer 2. Nucleus sampling cuts the tail by cumulative probability, so the set is tiny when the model is confident and large when it is not. "The 90 most likely" describes top-k; a 0.9 per-token threshold would almost always leave nothing.

Q3. Why is the decode phase memory-bandwidth-bound rather than compute-bound?

Answer 1. For Llama 3 8B, streaming 16 GB of weights takes about 4.8 ms on an H100 while the arithmetic takes about 16 microseconds. The softmax and the sampler are negligible, and both phases run in the same precision.

Q4. A model has 48 layers, 8 key-value heads and a head dimension of 128, stored in bf16. How big is its KV cache per token?

Answer 3. 2 × 48 × 8 × 128 × 2 bytes = 196,608 bytes = 192 KiB. Forgetting the factor of 2 for keys and values gives 96 KiB; forgetting the 2 bytes per bf16 number gives 96 KiB as well.

Q5. What does speculative decoding change about a model's output?

Answer 2. The big model verifies every drafted token and the acceptance rule is designed so the final distribution equals the big model's own. Draft tokens are only kept when the big model would have produced them.

Q6. What problem does PagedAttention solve?

Answer 4. It stores the cache in small pages allocated on demand and shared across requests, recovering the 60 to 80% of KV memory earlier servers wasted. Quadratic attention is FlashAttention's problem; repetition is a sampling problem.

Recap: five things to remember

  1. The model outputs logits; softmax makes probabilities; a sampler picks. Temperature reshapes the distribution; top-k, top-p and min-p cut its tail; a stop token ends the loop.
  2. Prefill is compute-bound and sets time-to-first-token; decode is memory-bandwidth-bound and sets tokens per second. Streaming 16 GB of weights per token is the bottleneck, not the arithmetic.
  3. KV cache per token = 2 × layers × kv_heads × head_dim × 2 bytes: 128 KiB for Llama 3 8B, so 16 GiB at 128k context; grouped-query attention is why it is not four times larger.
  4. Serving tricks all move fewer bytes per token: batching, continuous batching, PagedAttention, speculative decoding, quantisation.
  5. Output tokens cost about five times input tokens; as of October 2026 flagship models list at about $10 in and $50 out per million, small ones at $0.10 and $0.50.

References

  1. The Curious Case of Neural Text Degeneration — Holtzman et al., University of Washington / AI2, ICLR 2020. Nucleus sampling, and the clearest demonstration that maximising probability gives bad text.
  2. Turning Up the Heat: Min-p Sampling for Creative and Coherent LLM Outputs — Nguyen et al., ICLR 2025. A short paper that explains why top-p fails at high temperature.
  3. CTRL: A Conditional Transformer Language Model for Controllable Generation — Keskar et al., Salesforce, 2019. Section 4.1 is the origin of the repetition penalty.
  4. On NMT Search Errors and Model Errors: Cat Got Your Tongue? — Stahlberg and Byrne, Cambridge, EMNLP 2019. The empty-string result that undermines "find the most probable output".
  5. Generation strategies — Hugging Face Transformers documentation, 2026. Greedy, sampling and beam search with runnable code.
  6. Mastering LLM Techniques: Inference Optimization — NVIDIA Technical Blog, 2023. Prefill versus decode, KV-cache sizing, batching and parallelism, in one long post.
  7. Efficiently Scaling Transformer Inference — Pope et al., Google, 2022. The analytical model of why decode is bandwidth-bound and how to split inference across chips.
  8. GQA: Training Generalized Multi-Query Transformer Models from Multi-Head Checkpoints — Ainslie et al., Google, 2023. Why Llama 3 has 8 KV heads and what it saves.
  9. Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention — Kwon et al., UC Berkeley, SOSP 2023. The vLLM paper; Section 3 on memory waste is the motivation.

  13. Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts — Liu et al., Stanford, 2023. Why a long window is not the same as using it.
