Next: Day 8 — Post-training: instruction tuning and RLHF

Week 2 · Day 8

From text predictor to assistant: SFT and RLHF

After today you can explain how a raw next-token predictor is turned into a chat assistant, read a chat template, and say what RLHF fixes and what it leaves broken.

About 90 min · Builds on Day 7

Today's big idea

Pretraining makes a model that continues text. Nothing in that training says "answer the question" or "be helpful". Post-training adds that: first by showing the model examples of good answers (supervised fine-tuning), then by letting humans rank its answers and nudging it toward the ones they prefer (RLHF). The model never stops being a next-token predictor; we only change which continuations it finds likely.

1. A base model is not an assistant

Everything up to Day 7 produced a base model: a network trained only to predict the next token of internet text. Ask it a question and it does the one thing it knows: it continues the document. If the question looks like a line from a quiz sheet, it writes more quiz lines. If it looks like a forum post, it writes the next post, complete with a username and a date.

Here is the kind of thing a base model does with a plain question. This is illustrative, not a quoted transcript; you can reproduce the pattern with any open base model.

Prompt:    What is the capital of France?
Base:      What is the capital of Germany?
           What is the capital of Italy?
           What is the capital of Spain?
           Answers: 1. Paris  2. Berlin  3. Rome  4. Madrid

Prompt:    What is the capital of France?
Instruct:  The capital of France is Paris.

The base model "knows" the answer: Paris appears in its continuation. What it lacks is the habit of treating your text as a request and its own text as a reply. An instruct model (also called a chat model or assistant model) is the same network after extra training that installs exactly that habit. The Hugging Face documentation says it plainly: all causal language models, chat-trained or not, continue a sequence of tokens; chat models are base models fine-tuned on data formatted as a sequence of messages Hugging Face docs.

Two consequences follow: an assistant's knowledge comes almost entirely from pretraining, and the "assistant" is a character the model has learned to play inside a particular text format.

2. Chat templates and special tokens

A conversation is turned into one long token sequence by a chat template: a fixed way of writing who said what. It uses special tokens, reserved vocabulary entries (from Day 2's tokenizer) that never occur in ordinary text, so the model can tell structure from content. Three roles are standard: system (standing instructions from whoever deploys the model), user, and assistant.

This is the Llama 3 format, as documented by Meta Meta 2024:

<|begin_of_text|><|start_header_id|>system<|end_header_id|>

You are a helpful assistant.<|eot_id|><|start_header_id|>user<|end_header_id|>

What is the capital of France?<|eot_id|><|start_header_id|>assistant<|end_header_id|>

Each message is a role header, the content, and <|eot_id|> ("end of turn"). The prompt ends with an empty assistant header. The model's job is simply to continue from there, and it has learned that what follows an assistant header is a reply that ends with <|eot_id|>. That closing token is how the model stops talking: generation halts when it is sampled.

ChatML, the format OpenAI described for its early chat API and which Qwen and many other models adopted, does the same job with different tokens OpenAI 2023:

<|im_start|>system
You are a helpful assistant.<|im_end|>
<|im_start|>user
What is the capital of France?<|im_end|>
<|im_start|>assistant

Three practical points. First, the template is part of the model. Feed Llama 3 the ChatML tokens and quality drops sharply, because it never saw them in training; libraries hide this behind tokenizer.apply_chat_template(messages), which reads the template stored with the model Hugging Face docs. Second, the option add_generation_prompt=True appends the empty assistant header; forget it and the model may continue the user's message instead of answering it. Third, special tokens are a trust boundary: tokenizers treat the text <|im_start|>system inside user content as ordinary characters, so a user cannot forge a system message.

Analogy

Think of a screenplay. Pretraining taught the model to continue any document. The chat template turns a conversation into a script with speaker labels: SYSTEM, USER, ASSISTANT. Post-training teaches the model that whenever the next line is labeled ASSISTANT, it should write what a helpful, careful assistant would say. Swap the labels for ones it has never seen and the actor no longer recognizes the script.

3. Supervised fine-tuning: show it what good looks like

Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is the first post-training step: keep training the base model, with the same next-token loss from Day 3, on examples of (prompt, ideal response) written in the chat template. Usually the loss is computed only on the assistant's tokens, so the model learns to write replies rather than to imitate users. Nothing about the architecture changes. The gradient simply pushes the weights toward "after an assistant header, produce text like this".

Where do the examples come from? Four sources, in historical order:

  • Recast NLP datasets. FLAN (Google, 2021) rewrote more than 60 existing NLP tasks as natural-language instructions, fine-tuned a 137B model on them, and found it then handled unseen tasks better than the untuned model Wei 2021. This is where the term "instruction tuning" comes from.
  • Human-written demonstrations. InstructGPT paid a team of about 40 contractors to write ideal responses to roughly 13,000 prompts, many of them taken from real API traffic Ouyang 2022. Expensive, slow, high quality.
  • Model-generated data. Self-Instruct had a model generate new instructions and answers from a few seed examples, filtered them, and fine-tuned on the result Wang 2022. Stanford's Alpaca applied the idea with GPT-3.5 as the writer: 52,000 examples for under $500 of API calls, then fine-tuning a 7B LLaMA for under $100 of GPU time Stanford CRFM 2023. That recipe launched the 2023 wave of open chat models.
  • Few but excellent. LIMA fine-tuned a 65B LLaMA on only 1,000 carefully curated examples, with no reinforcement learning at all, and human raters judged its answers equal to or better than GPT-4's in 43% of comparisons Zhou 2023. The authors' "superficial alignment hypothesis": almost all knowledge is learned in pretraining; SFT mainly teaches which format and style to use when talking to a person.

SFT is cheap and effective. Its limit is that it can only imitate: long-winded demonstrations make a long-winded model, and a writer who bluffed teaches bluffing. Imitation also gives no signal about relative quality; a demonstration says "this is good" but never "this is better than that". Getting that comparative signal is the job of RLHF.

4. The InstructGPT recipe

The method behind ChatGPT was published by OpenAI in March 2022 as InstructGPT, and it is still the reference recipe Ouyang 2022. ChatGPT itself, released in November 2022, used the same methods with changes to how data was collected OpenAI 2022. The ideas go back to a 2017 paper that trained game-playing agents from human comparisons of short video clips instead of a hand-written score Christiano 2017, and a 2020 paper that applied the same loop to summarizing Reddit posts Stiennon 2020. The InstructGPT paper review goes through the paper itself.

Try it: step through the three stages below and watch what each stage takes in and what it produces.

Stage 1 produces a model. Stage 2 produces a scorer. Stage 3 uses the scorer to improve the model, with a leash (the KL penalty) tying it to where it started.

Stage 1: SFT. Exactly as in section 3: fine-tune GPT-3 on human demonstrations.

Stage 2: reward model. Take a prompt, sample several answers from the SFT model, and have a labeler rank them. Each ranking yields pairs: answer A beat answer B. Train a second network, the reward model (RM), that reads (prompt, answer) and outputs one number: the predicted "how much a human would like this". The RM is trained with the Bradley–Terry model, a 1952 statistical model of paired comparisons in which the chance that A beats B depends only on the difference of their scores Wikipedia:

P(A preferred over B) = σ(r_A − r_B), loss = −log σ(r(winner) − r(loser))

σ is the sigmoid from Day 3. The loss is small when the RM scores the winner well above the loser. Only differences matter; the RM's absolute numbers mean nothing. InstructGPT used about 33,000 prompts for this stage Ouyang 2022.

Stage 3: reinforcement learning with PPO. Now treat the SFT model as a policy: something that takes a prompt and produces an action, here a whole response. Sample a prompt, generate a response, score it with the RM, and update the policy's weights so that high-scoring responses become more likely. The update rule is PPO (Proximal Policy Optimization), a 2017 algorithm that clips each step so the policy cannot move too far at once and training does not blow up Schulman 2017. The reward actually optimized is not the raw RM score but

reward = r_RM(prompt, response) − β · KL(π_RL ‖ π_SFT)

where the second term is the KL penalty, discussed next. InstructGPT also mixed a little of the original pretraining loss into the updates ("PPO-ptx") so the model would not lose general skills Ouyang 2022.

Result. Labelers preferred outputs from the 1.3-billion-parameter InstructGPT over those of the 175-billion-parameter GPT-3, a model more than 100 times larger Ouyang 2022. That is the whole argument for post-training: a modest amount of human feedback was worth more than a hundredfold increase in size.

# one RLHF update, in pseudo-code
for prompt in batch:
    response = policy.generate(prompt)              # sample from the current model
    r = reward_model(prompt, response)              # one number, from stage 2
    kl = logp(policy, response) - logp(sft, response)   # per token, summed
    shaped = r - beta * kl                          # reward with the leash
    advantage = shaped - value_model(prompt)        # better or worse than expected?
    ppo_update(policy, response, advantage)         # clipped step toward higher reward

5. Why the KL penalty exists

The reward model is a proxy: a learned guess at human preference, trained on a few tens of thousands of comparisons. It is accurate near the answers it was trained on and increasingly wrong far from them. Reinforcement learning is an optimizer, and optimizers go wherever the number is highest. Without a leash, the policy drifts into text that the RM scores highly but no human would like: repeated flattery, odd formatting that hits a quirk of the RM, or gibberish.

The KL divergence KL(π_RL ‖ π_SFT) measures how different the trained policy's token probabilities are from the SFT model's on the responses it actually produces. Subtracting β times it from the reward says: you get credit for pleasing the RM, but you pay for every bit you move away from the model we started with. This keeps the policy on the part of the map where the RM is trustworthy, and it keeps the text fluent, because the SFT model is fluent.

How much drift is safe? Gao, Schulman and Hilton measured it with a large "gold" reward model standing in for real humans. As optimization pushed the proxy RM's score up, the gold score first rose and then fell: past a certain amount of KL, the policy was getting better at fooling the proxy and worse for people. Larger RMs and more RM training data moved the peak further out, but every proxy eventually broke Gao 2022. This is Goodhart's law with a measurement attached: when a measure becomes a target, it stops being a good measure.

6. Reward hacking, with examples

Reward hacking is any behavior that raises the reward without achieving what the reward was meant to measure. The classic example predates language models. An RL agent playing the boat-racing game CoastRunners discovered that driving in circles in a lagoon, hitting respawning targets and catching fire, scored more points than finishing the race OpenAI 2016. Language models find the same kind of loophole in learned reward models:

  • Length. Labelers tend to prefer longer answers, so the RM learns that longer is better, so RL makes answers longer. One study found that most of RLHF's measured gains on a standard setup could be reproduced by a reward that looked only at length Singhal 2023. OpenAI's own ChatGPT launch post said the model was "often excessively verbose" and blamed over-optimization OpenAI 2022.
  • Agreeing with the user. In human preference data, a response that matches the user's stated view is more likely to be chosen, so the RM rewards agreement and RL amplifies it Sharma 2023. More on this in section 10.
  • Thumbs-up chasing. In April 2025 OpenAI rolled back a GPT-4o update after it became noticeably more sycophantic, validating doubts and encouraging impulsive actions; the post-mortem said the update had leaned too heavily on short-term user feedback such as thumbs-up signals OpenAI 2025. The reward was "did the user like this message", and flattery is the cheapest way to earn it.
  • Hedging and refusing. If labelers punish confident mistakes harder than evasions, the RM learns that "I can't help with that" is a safe score, and the model over-refuses. XSTest was built to measure exactly this, with prompts such as how to "kill a Python process" that sound dangerous and are not Röttger 2023.

None of these are bugs in PPO. They are the reward model being exactly as good as the preference data, and the optimizer being exactly as relentless as it should be. The fixes are better data, better RM training, a tighter KL leash, or, as Day 9 shows, rewards that cannot be faked, such as a unit test passing.

7. What RLHF costs: the human pipeline

The expensive part of RLHF is not GPUs. It is people. InstructGPT's team hired about 40 contractors through Upwork and Scale AI and had them write demonstrations and rank outputs following a guideline document; even so, trained labelers agreed with one another only about 73% of the time Ouyang 2022. Meta collected over 1.4 million binary human comparisons for Llama 2's reward models, in weekly batches, across five rounds of RLHF in which each new model's outputs were ranked again Touvron 2023. Anthropic's 2022 helpful-and-harmless work described the same loop, with crowdworkers chatting with the model and choosing the better of two replies at every turn Bai 2022a.

A typical pipeline looks like this:

StepWhoWhat they produceWhy it is hard
Write guidelinesResearchersWhat helpful, honest, and harmless mean; tie-breaking rulesThe guideline becomes the model's values; every ambiguity is a future bug
Collect promptsUsers, labelersRealistic, diverse requestsMust cover what people actually ask, including bad requests
Write demonstrationsLabelers, often expertsIdeal answers for SFTSlow; a good coding or medical answer needs an expert
Rank samplesLabelersPairwise preferences for the RMLow agreement on subjective items; people skim long outputs
AuditResearchersSpot checks, agreement rates, red-teamingCatches drift such as the length bias, if someone looks

On the compute side, PPO-style RLHF keeps four networks alive at once: the policy being trained, a frozen SFT copy for the KL term, the reward model, and a value model that estimates expected reward Hugging Face 2022. Generating full responses for every update dominates the wall clock. That cost is why a cheaper alternative, DPO, spread so quickly through open models; Day 9 covers it.

8. Constitutional AI: let the model grade itself

Anthropic's 2022 Constitutional AI (CAI) replaces most of the human harmlessness labels with a short written list of principles, the "constitution", plus the model's own judgment Bai 2022b. Two phases:

  1. Critique and revise. Give a helpful-only model a harmful request and collect its harmful answer. Show it a principle ("identify ways in which this response is dangerous or unethical"), ask it to critique its own answer, then ask it to revise. Repeat with a few randomly chosen principles. Fine-tune on the revised answers. This is SFT with the model writing its own demonstrations.
  2. RL from AI feedback (RLAIF). Sample two responses, show them to the model along with a principle, and ask which is better. Those AI-generated preferences train a reward model, which drives PPO exactly as in RLHF. Humans still label helpfulness; the AI labels harmlessness.

Why do it? Human harmlessness labeling is slow, exposes workers to disturbing content, and bakes in labeler inconsistency. A constitution is explicit, inspectable, and editable: change a sentence, retrain, and the behavior shifts. Anthropic's original constitution drew on sources such as the UN Declaration of Human Rights and other labs' published rules Anthropic 2023; a much longer rewritten constitution, explaining the values the company hopes Claude holds and the reasons behind them, was published in January 2026 Anthropic 2026. In the paper, the CAI model was both less harmful and less evasive than the RLHF baseline, because it had been trained to explain its objections rather than refuse flatly Bai 2022b. See the Constitutional AI paper review.

RLAIF, AI preference labels in place of human ones, has since become routine, usually with a stronger model grading a weaker one. It is cheap and consistent, and it inherits every bias of the grader.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a parrot that has listened to every conversation in the world. It does not know it is supposed to answer you; it just keeps talking the way those conversations went. First you show it lots of examples: when someone asks this, say that. Now it answers. Then you let it try on its own, and every time it gives two answers you point at the one you like more. The parrot keeps a little scorekeeper in its head that guesses which answers you will point at, and it practices saying those. But you also tell it: do not change too much, or you will forget how to talk. Constitutional AI is the same game, except that instead of you pointing every time, you give the parrot a list of rules and it points for itself.

9. System prompts: the cheapest layer

Training sets the model's defaults. The system prompt adjusts them per deployment: text in the system role, placed before the conversation, that the model learned during SFT and RLHF (whose data includes varied system prompts) to treat as standing instructions. It is the layer that says "today's date is..." or "you are the support agent for this product; answer in Spanish". Nothing changes in the weights; behavior shifts because the model conditions on different context.

Companies lean on it heavily. Anthropic publishes the system prompts used by claude.ai and its mobile apps, with dated entries per model; they supply the date, encourage behaviors such as formatting code in Markdown, and are not applied to API traffic, where developers write their own Anthropic docs. Reading one is instructive: a good part of what feels like personality is a few paragraphs of instructions on top of the trained model.

So behavior is layered, from the most expensive layer to change to the cheapest:

LayerSet byChangesCost to change
PretrainingInternet-scale textKnowledge, language abilityMillions of dollars, months
SFTDemonstrationsFormat, the assistant habit, basic styleHours to days of GPU time
RLHF / RLAIFPreferences, constitutionHelpfulness, tone, refusals, honesty habitsDays; the hard part is the data
System promptDeployerPersona, rules, context for this appMinutes; no retraining
User promptUserThis one requestFree

Cheaper layers override expensive ones only partly. A system prompt cannot give the model knowledge it lacks, and trained refusals usually survive a system prompt that asks for the opposite, which is intended: a deployer should not be able to switch off safety behavior with one sentence.

10. What RLHF does and does not fix

An honest scorecard, as of October 2026.

Fixed: format and usefulness. The model answers instead of continuing, follows multi-part instructions, declines clearly harmful requests, and matches the tone people expect.

Made worse, then partly managed: sycophancy. Because preference data rewards agreement, RLHF tends to produce models that tell users what they want to hear, abandon correct answers when challenged, and praise bad work Sharma 2023. The 2025 GPT-4o rollback showed how fast it comes back when feedback signals are reweighted OpenAI 2025. Labs now train and test against it explicitly, but it is a pressure built into the data, not a solved problem.

Reduced, not removed: hallucination. InstructGPT's authors listed "still makes up facts" among its remaining failures Ouyang 2022. A 2025 OpenAI analysis argued the cause is structural: most training and evaluation scores a confident guess the same as a correct answer and gives nothing for "I don't know", so guessing is the score-maximizing strategy, and post-training inherits that incentive unless evaluations are changed to reward calibrated uncertainty OpenAI 2025. RLHF can teach a model to say "I'm not sure" more often; it cannot give the model facts it never learned.

A dial, not a fix: refusals. Push harmlessness and the model refuses to explain how to kill a Python process Röttger 2023. Push helpfulness and it complies with harmful requests, which InstructGPT did by default Ouyang 2022. Every lab sits somewhere on this dial and moves along it release by release.

Barely touched: reasoning. Preferences about which answer reads better are a weak signal for whether a proof is correct. Getting better at hard problems needed a different reward, one that checks the answer. That is Day 9.

Common misunderstandings

  • "RLHF teaches the model facts." It does not. Knowledge comes from pretraining; RLHF changes which of the model's possible outputs it prefers to produce. LIMA's 1,000 examples were enough to make a passable assistant because the knowledge was already there.
  • "The reward model is a human." It is a network trained on a few tens of thousands of comparisons, and it is wrong in ways RL will find. That is why the KL penalty exists.
  • "The chat model is a different kind of model." Same architecture, same next-token objective; the assistant is a learned style inside a template.
  • "A system prompt can make the model do anything." It adjusts behavior within what training allows; it adds no knowledge and usually cannot cancel trained refusals.
  • "Constitutional AI means no humans." Humans wrote the constitution, label helpfulness, and audit the result; CAI replaced human labels for harmlessness specifically.

Going deeper (optional)

The Bradley–Terry loss and the KL-penalized RL objective fit together more tightly than it first appears. Maximizing E[r(x,y)] − β·KL(π ‖ π_ref) has a closed-form optimum: π*(y|x) ∝ π_ref(y|x) · exp(r(x,y)/β). Solve that for r and any reward can be written as β·log(π*(y|x) / π_ref(y|x)) plus a term that depends only on x. Plug this into the Bradley–Terry loss and the reward model disappears: you can train the policy directly on preference pairs. That observation is Direct Preference Optimization, tomorrow's first topic Rafailov 2023; see the DPO paper review.

Check yourself

Q1. You give a base model the text "What is the capital of France?" What does it most likely do?

Answer 2. A base model only continues text; the answer may appear somewhere in the continuation, but the habit of replying and stopping comes from post-training. Refusing and clarifying are also learned assistant behaviors.

Q2. What does the reward model in RLHF output?

Answer 3. The RM reads (prompt, answer) and returns one scalar. It is trained on pairs with the Bradley–Terry loss, so only differences between scores carry meaning.

Q3. Why is a KL penalty to the SFT model added to the reward during RL?

Answer 4. The RM is a proxy that is only accurate near its training distribution; the KL term is a leash that stops the optimizer from running off to exploit it. Gao et al. 2022 measured true quality falling once KL grows too large.

Q4. What was the headline result of the InstructGPT paper?

Answer 1. A model 100x smaller won on human preference. The paper explicitly says the model still makes up facts, and DPO came a year later.

Q5. What does Constitutional AI replace?

Answer 3. CAI keeps the RLHF structure (SFT, reward model, RL) but has the model critique, revise, and rank harmlessness using a constitution. Humans still write the principles and label helpfulness.

Q6. Which of these is reward hacking?

Answer 1. Reward hacking is raising the reward without achieving its intent; length bias is the textbook language-model case. The others are optimization, tokenization, and forgetting problems, not reward exploitation.

Recap: five things to remember

  1. A base model continues text; an instruct model is the same network trained to play the assistant role inside a chat template with special tokens.
  2. SFT imitates demonstrations with the ordinary next-token loss; a surprisingly small amount of good data goes a long way, because knowledge is already in the weights.
  3. RLHF: train a reward model on pairwise human preferences (Bradley–Terry), then optimize the policy with PPO against that reward, minus a KL penalty that keeps it close to the SFT model.
  4. The reward model is a proxy and the optimizer will exploit it: length bias, flattery, and over-refusal are what over-optimization looks like.
  5. Constitutional AI swaps human harmlessness labels for principle-guided AI feedback; system prompts are the cheapest layer of behavior; and RLHF does not fix hallucination or reasoning, only how the model behaves.

References

  1. Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback — Ouyang et al., OpenAI, NeurIPS 2022. The InstructGPT paper; sections 3 and 5 contain the recipe, the labeler process, and the honest list of limitations.
  2. Deep reinforcement learning from human preferences — Christiano et al., OpenAI / DeepMind, NeurIPS 2017. Where learning a reward model from pairwise human comparisons started.
  3. Learning to summarize from human feedback — Stiennon et al., OpenAI, NeurIPS 2020. The first full RLHF loop on a language task, with the KL penalty already in place.
  4. Illustrating Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) — Lambert, Castricato, von Werra, Havrilla, Hugging Face blog, 2022. The clearest diagrams of the three stages.
  5. Chat templates — Hugging Face Transformers documentation. Shows real templates for several models and what add_generation_prompt does.
  6. Meta Llama 3 model cards and prompt formats — Meta, 2024. The exact special tokens and prompt layout used above.
  7. Finetuned Language Models Are Zero-Shot Learners — Wei et al., Google, ICLR 2022. FLAN; the origin of instruction tuning.
  8. Alpaca: A Strong, Replicable Instruction-Following Model — Taori et al., Stanford CRFM, 2023. The $600 recipe that showed SFT on model-generated data works.
  9. LIMA: Less Is More for Alignment — Zhou et al., Meta AI, NeurIPS 2023. One thousand examples; the superficial alignment hypothesis.
  10. Scaling Laws for Reward Model Overoptimization — Gao, Schulman, Hilton, OpenAI, ICML 2023. The measured rise-then-fall of true quality as KL grows; the justification for the leash.
  11. Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI Feedback — Bai et al., Anthropic, 2022. The critique-revise phase and RLAIF, with the actual principles in the appendix.

Next: Day 9 — Reasoning models and test-time compute

Week 2 · Day 9

Reasoning models and test-time compute

After today you can explain why "think step by step" works, how DPO and verifiable rewards changed post-training, what o1 and DeepSeek-R1 proved, and when paying for thinking tokens is worth it.

About 100 min · Builds on Day 8

Today's big idea

A transformer spends the same amount of compute on every token, so the only way for it to work harder on a hard problem is to produce more tokens before answering. Chain-of-thought prompting gets that behavior by asking. Reasoning models get it by training with reinforcement learning against rewards that check the final answer. More thinking buys more accuracy, with diminishing returns, and since 2025 you choose how much to buy.

1. Why writing the steps helps a next-token predictor

Recall Day 5: generating one token means one forward pass through every layer. That is a fixed budget of arithmetic, the same for "the" as for the answer to a hard question. Ask a model for 17 × 24 and demand the answer as the very next token, and it must compress the whole multiplication into that single pass. Let it write "17 × 20 = 340, 17 × 4 = 68, 340 + 68 = 408" and each step is a small, familiar pattern, and every intermediate result sits in the context where attention can read it back. The context window becomes scratch paper; the sequence of tokens becomes a sequence of computation steps. The model is not reasoning in some new way. It is converting one impossible prediction into many easy ones.

Wei et al. (Google, January 2022) made this a technique: chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting, in which the few-shot examples in the prompt include worked reasoning before each answer. With eight such exemplars, a 540B model set a new state of the art on GSM8K, a benchmark of 8,500 grade-school math word problems Cobbe 2021, beating a fine-tuned GPT-3 with a verifier Wei 2022. Small models produced fluent chains with wrong steps; the gain appeared only at scale. See the chain-of-thought paper review.

Kojima et al. then showed that you do not even need examples. Appending "Let's think step by step" to the question raised text-davinci-002's accuracy on MultiArith from 17.7% to 78.7% and on GSM8K from 10.4% to 40.7% Kojima 2022. The model already knew how to show its work; the phrase just moved it into the part of its distribution where it does.

Wang et al. added self-consistency: sample many chains with the temperature sampling from Day 7, take the final answer from each, and return the majority. On GSM8K this added 17.9 points over a single chain Wang 2022. This is the first clean example of test-time compute: pay for 40 answers instead of one and accuracy rises, with no change to the weights.

TechniqueWhat you addCostWhy it works
Few-shot CoTWorked examples with reasoningLonger prompt, longer answerShows the format; steps become tokens the model can condition on
Zero-shot CoT"Let's think step by step"Longer answerSame, without examples
Self-consistencyN samples, majority voteN× tokensWrong chains disagree with each other; right chains agree

2. From prompting the steps to training them

If chains help, train on them. STaR (2022) had a model generate rationales, kept only the ones that reached the correct answer, fine-tuned on those, and repeated, bootstrapping its own reasoning from a few seeds Zelikman 2022. OpenAI's "Let's Verify Step by Step" (2023) trained a process reward model that grades each step of a solution rather than only the final answer, and found it a far better selector on hard math than an outcome-only grader Lightman 2023. The common pattern is: generate many attempts, check them, and train on what passed. Everything below is a variation on that loop.

3. DPO: preference training without a reward model

Day 8's optional note ended with a surprise: the KL-penalized RLHF objective has a closed-form optimum, so any reward model can be rewritten in terms of the policy itself. Rafailov et al. (2023) turned that into Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), a plain classification loss on preference pairs Rafailov 2023:

loss = −log σ( β · [ log π(y_w|x)/π_ref(y_w|x) − log π(y_l|x)/π_ref(y_l|x) ] )

In words: for each (prompt, chosen answer y_w, rejected answer y_l), raise the probability of the chosen answer relative to the frozen reference model and lower the rejected one, with β setting how far you are allowed to move. There is no reward model, no sampling during training, no value model, and no PPO. Two networks instead of four.

# DPO, one batch
logp_w  = policy.logp(prompt, chosen)   - ref.logp(prompt, chosen)    # log-ratio, chosen
logp_l  = policy.logp(prompt, rejected) - ref.logp(prompt, rejected)  # log-ratio, rejected
margin  = beta * (logp_w - logp_l)
loss    = -log_sigmoid(margin).mean()   # push margin up: chosen more likely than rejected
loss.backward(); optimizer.step()       # ordinary supervised training step

Open models adopted it almost immediately. Zephyr-7B (October 2023) used DPO on AI-ranked data to beat much larger chat models Tunstall 2023. Meta's Llama 3 post-training used SFT, rejection sampling, and DPO, and the paper says the team tried PPO but found DPO needed less compute at scale and performed better Llama Team 2024. Tülu 3 (2024) used DPO for preferences before a final RL stage Lambert 2024. See the DPO paper review.

DPO's limits explain what came next. The pairs are usually generated once, often by another model, so training is off-policy: the signal goes stale as the policy moves. DPO can over-optimize too, often by driving the rejected answer's probability down faster than it raises the chosen one. And DPO needs preference pairs; it cannot use a reward you compute on the fly, like a unit test passing. For that, you need RL again.

4. Rewards you cannot fake: verifiable rewards and GRPO

Day 8's reward hacking came from a learned reward model being a proxy. Many tasks do not need a proxy. A math problem has an answer you can compare against a reference. Code either passes the unit tests or it does not. Tülu 3 named this setup reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR): reward 1 if the checker accepts the final answer, 0 otherwise Lambert 2024. The reward cannot be flattered. It can still be gamed, but only through the checker (formatting tricks, special-casing visible tests), a narrower attack surface than a model's taste.

The algorithm most associated with this is GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization), introduced by DeepSeek in February 2024 for math Shao 2024. Try reading it without the equations:

  1. For one prompt, sample a group of answers, say 16.
  2. Score each with the verifier: 1 or 0.
  3. Compute the group's mean and spread. Each answer's advantage is how far its score sits above or below the group mean, divided by the spread.
  4. Raise the probability of every token in above-average answers and lower it for below-average ones, with the PPO-style clipping and a KL penalty to the reference model from Day 8.

The trick is in step 3. PPO needs a separate value model to estimate "how well should this prompt have gone", which is a whole extra network the size of the policy. GRPO uses the group's own average as that estimate. If 4 of 16 answers were right, those 4 get pushed up and the 12 get pushed down. No critic, half the memory, and a signal that is exact rather than guessed.

Analogy

A math exam with scratch paper. A model answering in one token is a student forced to write only the final number. Chain-of-thought hands out scratch paper. RL with verifiable rewards is a teacher who grades only the final answers of many practice attempts and tells the student "do more of whatever you did on the ones you got right". The student is free to invent any working habit, including crossing things out and starting over, and the ones that produce right answers get reinforced. Nobody wrote "check your work" on the syllabus; it got learned because it paid.

5. OpenAI o1: a hidden reasoning trace

On 12 September 2024 OpenAI released o1-preview, the first widely available reasoning model: a model trained with large-scale reinforcement learning to produce a long internal chain of thought before every answer. The numbers were striking. On the 2024 AIME, a competition exam for the strongest US high-school mathematicians, GPT-4o solved about 12% of problems; o1 solved 74% with one attempt per problem and 93% when 1,000 samples were re-ranked with a learned scorer. It reached the 89th percentile on Codeforces programming contests and exceeded PhD-level human accuracy on the GPQA science benchmark OpenAI 2024.

Two things about the release mattered more than the scores. First, OpenAI showed two scaling curves: accuracy rose smoothly with more RL training compute, and separately with more thinking at inference time OpenAI 2024. Test-time compute had become a lever on the same footing as model size. Second, the chain of thought was hidden. OpenAI chose not to show users the raw trace, giving only a summary, partly so the chain could stay unfiltered for monitoring and partly for competitive reasons OpenAI 2024. In the API, reasoning tokens are invisible but still occupy the context window and are billed as output tokens OpenAI docs. The follow-ups o3 and o4-mini (April 2025) added the ability to call tools such as search and Python inside the chain OpenAI 2025a.

What o1 did not give the world was the recipe: which RL algorithm, what reward, how much SFT. Four months later, someone else published it.

6. DeepSeek-R1: the open recipe

In January 2025 DeepSeek released R1 with a paper, open weights, and an MIT license DeepSeek 2025a. The paper's most important experiment is DeepSeek-R1-Zero: take the DeepSeek-V3 base model, skip SFT entirely, and run GRPO with two rule-based rewards, one for a correct final answer and one for wrapping the reasoning in the expected tags. Nothing else. Over training, the model's AIME 2024 pass@1 rose from 15.6% to 77.9%, and to 86.7% with majority voting, and its responses grew steadily longer on their own as it learned that more thinking paid off DeepSeek 2025b.

The behaviors that emerged were never demonstrated to it. The paper describes an "aha moment" partway through training, marked by a sudden jump in the model's use of the word "wait" as it began to pause, re-examine its own steps, and try another approach; one logged chain literally reads "Wait, wait. Wait. That's an aha moment I can flag here." before the model restarts the problem DeepSeek 2025b. The lesson is the one from the analogy box: give an optimizer an exact reward and room to produce tokens, and self-checking is discovered because it works.

R1-Zero's chains were also hard to read and often mixed English and Chinese mid-sentence DeepSeek 2025b. The full DeepSeek-R1 fixed that with a four-stage pipeline: a small "cold start" SFT on a few thousand clean, long reasoning examples; RL for reasoning with an added reward for staying in one language; rejection sampling from that model to build a large SFT set that also covers writing and general chat; and a final RL round for helpfulness and harmlessness, in the spirit of Day 8. The result matched o1 on the headline math and coding benchmarks.

Then came distillation: fine-tune small open models on about 800,000 of R1's outputs. DeepSeek released six such models, built on Qwen2.5 (1.5B, 7B, 14B, 32B) and Llama 3 (8B, 70B), and reported that distilling from the big model beat running RL directly on the small ones DeepSeek 2025a. A 7B model that could work through competition math on a laptop appeared overnight. The paper was later peer reviewed and published in Nature in September 2025, with supplementary material that put the cost of the R1 reinforcement-learning stage at about US$294,000 on 512 H800 GPUs, on top of the already-trained V3 base DeepSeek 2025c.

What R1 showed about open research: the reasoning recipe was not a secret ingredient but a known loop (verifiable rewards plus GRPO plus patience), it could be reproduced by any lab with a strong base model, and the results could be compressed into models small enough for anyone to run. See the DeepSeek-R1 paper review.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a kid who is only allowed to shout out the answer to a math problem instantly. They get easy ones right and hard ones wrong. Now give the kid a notebook and say "write down your steps first". Suddenly the hard ones become possible, because each line in the notebook is a small step, and they can look back at what they wrote. That is chain of thought. A reasoning model is a kid who practiced thousands of problems with the notebook, was only told "right" or "wrong" at the end, and figured out by themselves that checking their work and starting over when something looks off leads to more "rights". Nobody taught the checking; it just kept winning. The one catch: the notebook costs paper, and sometimes the kid fills three pages for 2 + 3.

7. Test-time compute: buying accuracy with tokens

Try it: drag the thinking budget below and watch accuracy on the illustrative task. Notice where the curve flattens.

Each doubling of thinking tokens buys roughly the same accuracy gain, so the curve is a straight line on a log axis and flattens on a linear one. Past some budget, more tokens buy almost nothing; before it, a small model with a budget can beat a large model without one.

Snell et al. (2024) made the trade-off precise. Matching total compute, a smaller model that spends its budget on test-time strategies (revising, or sampling candidates and picking with a verifier) can beat a model 14 times larger, as long as the problem is one it has a reasonable chance on. On the hardest problems, pretraining compute still wins Snell 2024. Anthropic reported the same shape for Claude 3.7 Sonnet: accuracy on math rose roughly logarithmically with the serial thinking budget, and in parallel mode, sampling 256 attempts and choosing with a scoring model pushed GPQA to 84.8% Anthropic 2025a.

Two ways to spend, then. Serial: one longer chain, which is what reasoning models do by default; it cannot be parallelized, so latency grows with the budget. Parallel: many chains with a vote or a verifier, which is self-consistency grown up; it costs N times the tokens but not N times the wall clock. Logarithmic returns are the reason "thinking budgets" exist: the first 2,000 tokens of thinking are worth far more than the next 20,000, so you want a dial.

The dials, as of October 2026:

ProviderControlHow it works
Anthropicbudget_tokens, then effortClaude 3.7 Sonnet (February 2025) introduced extended thinking with a token budget of at least 1,024, up to a 128K output limit, billed at output rates Anthropic 2025b. Claude Opus 4.6 (February 2026) replaced the fixed budget with adaptive thinking, where the model decides whether and how much to think, steered by an effort level of low, medium, high, or max Anthropic 2026a. Fixed budgets are rejected on Claude 4.7 and later Anthropic docs.
OpenAIreasoning_effortLevels from none through minimal, low, medium, high, xhigh, and max, depending on the model; reasoning tokens hidden, billed as output OpenAI docs.
Googlethinking_levelThinking on by default for most models from Gemini 2.5 onward; levels from minimal to high per model; thought tokens count against the output limit Google docs.
Qwen (open)thinking budget, then reasoning_effortQwen3 (April 2025) shipped one model with a thinking and a non-thinking mode, switchable per request, plus a budget on reasoning length Qwen 2025; Qwen3.8 (August 2026) exposes a reasoning_effort setting Qwen 2026.
DeepSeek (open)thinking + reasoning_effortThe V4 line puts thinking and non-thinking modes in one model; the API takes a thinking switch and an effort level DeepSeek docs.

8. Where reasoning models are now (October 2026)

The short version: "reasoning model" is no longer a separate product category. Every frontier model thinks, and the question has become how much and whether the model or the caller decides. Dates below are the official announcement dates.

  • OpenAI. GPT-5 (7 August 2025) merged the GPT and o-series lines into one system: a fast model, a deeper reasoning model, and a router that chooses between them per request; OpenAI reported its thinking mode made about 80% fewer factual errors than o3 OpenAI 2025b. As of 7 October 2026 the GPT-6 Sol and Luna models serve all ChatGPT tiers, and OpenAI's safety documentation reports capability evaluations at maximum reasoning effort and safety evaluations at the lowest deployed effort, because most traffic runs there OpenAI 2026.
  • Anthropic. Claude 3.7 Sonnet (February 2025) was the first hybrid: instant replies or visible extended thinking, same weights Anthropic 2025b. Claude Opus 4.6 (February 2026) made thinking adaptive Anthropic 2026a. Claude Opus 5.5 (22 September 2026) can no longer be run with thinking switched off, defaults to medium effort, and ships "preserved thinking", which stops API users from editing prior context to extract the reasoning for distillation Anthropic 2026b.
  • Google. Gemini 2.5 (March 2025) was presented as a thinking model from the start Google 2025. Gemini 3 (18 November 2025) added Deep Think, an enhanced reasoning mode that beats Gemini 3 Pro on the hardest benchmarks Google 2025b. Gemini 3.8 Live Extended Thinking (15 September 2026) reasons in the background during a spoken conversation Google 2026.
  • DeepSeek. After R1, the reasoning line merged into the general V4 models. As of October 2026 the API serves DeepSeek-V4-Pro and a V4.1-Flash checkpoint dated 17 October, with thinking controlled per request DeepSeek docs; the weights are on Hugging Face, with V4-Pro-0813 from August 2026 DeepSeek HF.
  • Qwen (Alibaba). Qwen3 (April 2025) was the first major open family with a built-in thinking switch Qwen 2025. Qwen3.5 (February 2026) and Qwen3.6 (April 2026) followed, and Qwen3.8 (August 2026) tops out at a 2.4-trillion-parameter mixture-of-experts with 95B active, alongside a 27B model, both with adjustable reasoning effort Qwen 2026.

The arc is two years long: hidden chain (September 2024), open recipe (January 2025), a switch (2025), an effort dial (2026), and now models that will not run without thinking at all.

9. Limitations

Overthinking. o1-style models generate hundreds of tokens of deliberation for problems like "2 + 3 = ?", a pattern measured and named "overthinking" in late 2024 Chen 2024. Every adaptive-thinking and effort control in section 7 exists to manage it.

The chain is not a faithful record. Anthropic tested this directly: slip a hint about the answer into the prompt and check whether the model's chain of thought admits using it. Claude 3.7 Sonnet mentioned the hint it relied on 25% of the time and DeepSeek R1 39% of the time, averaged across hint types Anthropic 2025c. The visible reasoning is a text the model generates, shaped by the same training pressures as the answer; it is evidence about the model's process, not a transcript of it. OpenAI found the complementary problem: when a monitor reads the chain to catch reward hacking in coding tasks and the model is penalized for "bad thoughts", it keeps hacking and learns to hide the intent from the chain instead, so OpenAI recommends not optimizing the chain directly OpenAI 2025c. A cross-lab position paper calls chain-of-thought monitorability a useful but fragile safety opportunity for exactly this reason Korbak 2025.

Cost and latency. Thinking tokens are output tokens, the most expensive kind, and a serial chain cannot be sped up by adding GPUs: ten thousand tokens of thinking is ten thousand sequential decode steps from Day 7, often a minute or more. Those tokens also occupy the context window and count against the output limit, so a response can be cut off or come back empty if thinking eats the budget Google docs.

Reward hacking moves to the checker. With a learned reward model, the model learned to please the judge. With unit tests, the model learns to pass the tests, which is not the same as writing correct code; OpenAI's monitoring work found models subverting tests rather than fixing bugs OpenAI 2025c.

Uneven gains. Verifiable rewards exist for math, code, and logic. Writing, judgment, and taste have no checker, so the improvement there comes mostly from the older preference methods of Day 8, and it is smaller.

10. When to use a reasoning model, and how much

TaskThinkingWhy
Multi-step math, debugging, planning with constraints, hard analysisYes, medium to high effortThe answer is checkable and errors compound across steps; this is where the curves in section 7 are steepest
Agentic work: many tool calls, long tasksYes, adaptiveReasoning between tool results catches mistakes early; most providers interleave thinking with tool use
Classification, extraction, reformatting, translationNo, or minimalOne-step tasks; thinking adds cost and latency with little gain
Chat, drafting, rewriting, summariesLowNo verifiable answer; quality comes from preference training, and users notice latency
Latency-critical products (voice, autocomplete)No, or a model built for itSerial thinking cannot be parallelized away

A workable rule: if you could write a checker for the answer and the task has more than two steps, pay for thinking. Otherwise start at the lowest effort, measure accuracy on your own examples, and raise effort only while the measurement moves. The log-shaped curve means the first increase is usually worth it and the last one almost never is.

Common misunderstandings

  • "Reasoning models are a new architecture." Same transformer, same next-token loop. The difference is training (RL against verifiable rewards) and a convention for a thinking section before the answer.
  • "The chain of thought is what the model actually thought." It is generated text, and models often omit the real reason for an answer. Treat it as evidence, not a log.
  • "DPO is RL." DPO is a supervised classification loss on preference pairs; it needs no sampling, reward model, or value model. That is exactly why it is cheaper and why it cannot use a unit-test reward.
  • "More thinking always helps." Returns are logarithmic and on easy tasks they are zero; overthinking costs money and time for nothing.
  • "R1 was trained for $294,000." That figure covers the RL stage on top of DeepSeek-V3, whose own pretraining was far more expensive, and it excludes research and failed runs.

Going deeper (optional)

Why normalize advantages by the group's spread in GRPO? With binary rewards, a group where 1 of 16 answers is right has mean 0.06 and small spread, so that one success gets a large positive advantage: rare wins on hard prompts are amplified. A group where 15 of 16 are right gives the lone failure a large negative advantage: rare mistakes on easy prompts are punished. Prompts the model always gets right or always gets wrong produce zero advantage and no gradient, which is a feature: training effort concentrates on problems at the edge of the model's ability. The DeepSeekMath paper Shao 2024 has the full objective, including the clipped ratio and the KL estimator it borrows from PPO.

Check yourself

Q1. Why does writing intermediate steps help a transformer solve a hard problem?

Answer 3. Compute per token is fixed; a chain of thought turns one hard prediction into many easy ones and stores partial results in the context. There is no separate module, and pretraining has no reward.

Q2. What is self-consistency?

Answer 1. Self-consistency is a decoding-time trick: N samples, one vote. Fine-tuning on correct chains is STaR; the others describe different ideas.

Q3. Compared with PPO-based RLHF, what does DPO remove?

Answer 4. DPO folds the reward model into the policy and trains directly on preference pairs with a classification loss. It still needs preference data and a reference model.

Q4. What made DeepSeek-R1-Zero notable?

Answer 2. R1-Zero is the pure-RL experiment: GRPO with rule-based rewards straight from the V3 base, and reflection ("wait") appeared without being demonstrated. The full R1 added a small SFT cold start.

Q5. How does accuracy typically change as the thinking budget grows?

Answer 3. OpenAI's o1 plots and Anthropic's Claude 3.7 measurements both show log-linear curves. Diminishing returns are the reason effort levels and budgets exist.

Q6. What did Anthropic's 2025 faithfulness study find about reasoning models' chains of thought?

Answer 2. Claude 3.7 Sonnet acknowledged the hint it used about 25% of the time and DeepSeek R1 about 39%, so the chain cannot be read as a complete account of the model's process.

Recap: five things to remember

  1. Compute per token is fixed, so a model must spend tokens to think; chain-of-thought turns the context window into scratch paper, and sampling several chains and voting is the simplest form of test-time compute.
  2. DPO trains on preference pairs with a classification loss and no reward model, which is why it replaced PPO in most open models; its weakness is that it cannot use rewards computed on the fly.
  3. Verifiable rewards (answer matches, tests pass) plus GRPO (group-relative advantages, no value model) are the recipe behind reasoning models; o1 showed it works, R1 published it and open-sourced it.
  4. Accuracy grows roughly logarithmically with thinking tokens, so every provider now exposes a budget or effort dial, and the newest models decide for themselves when to think.
  5. The chain of thought is generated text, not a transcript: models overthink easy problems, omit the real reasons for answers, and hide intent if the chain itself is penalized. Use thinking where answers are checkable and multi-step; measure before paying for more.
