Next: Day 10 — Fine-tuning on a budget: LoRA, QLoRA, and quantization

Week 2 · Day 10

Fine-tuning and shrinking models

After today you can decide whether a task needs a better prompt, a retriever, or a fine-tune; explain why LoRA and QLoRA make fine-tuning cheap; and read a model name like Q4_K_M, GPTQ or AWQ and know what you are trading away.

About 90 min · Builds on Day 9

Today's big idea

A pretrained model is a frozen block of expensive knowledge. Everything today is a trick for changing or shrinking that block without paying the pretraining bill again: train two thin matrices instead of the whole network (LoRA), store each weight in four bits instead of sixteen (quantization), or teach a small model to imitate a big one (distillation). The memory arithmetic is simple, and it predicts what fits on which GPU.

1. Prompt, retrieve, or fine-tune?

Most problems are solved by a better prompt, which changes nothing inside the model and can be undone in a second. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), covered on Day 12, looks up relevant documents at run time and pastes them into the prompt, so the model reads your facts instead of remembering them. Fine-tuning continues training on your own examples so the weights themselves change: the most powerful option and the easiest to get wrong, so it comes last.

One finding keeps coming up: fine-tuning is good at teaching form (a format, a tone, a skill) and surprisingly bad at teaching facts. Ovadia and colleagues compared fine-tuning with RAG for injecting new knowledge and found RAG consistently won, both for facts the model had seen in pretraining and for brand-new ones; the model struggled to learn new facts from fine-tuning unless it was shown many paraphrases of each. Ovadia 2023

You need…Start withWhy
A specific tone, format or lengthPromptA system prompt plus two examples usually does it. Fine-tune only when the prompt grows past a page and you call it millions of times.
Facts that change (prices, tickets, your documents)RetrieveWeights are frozen at training time; a retriever is updated by copying a file.
Facts the model never saw and that rarely changeRetrieve, then maybe fine-tune on topRAG beat unsupervised fine-tuning for knowledge injection in controlled tests.
A new skill: strict JSON output, a house style, a domain dialect, a tool protocolFine-tune (LoRA)Thousands of examples of the behaviour teach it more reliably than instructions do.
Lower latency or cost on a fixed taskFine-tune a small modelA fine-tuned 3B model with a short prompt can replace a 70B model with a 2,000-token prompt.
To run on a laptop or phoneQuantize, distil, or pick a small modelSize, not skill, is the constraint; see sections 7 to 9.

2. Full fine-tuning and what it costs

Full fine-tuning means running the same gradient descent you met on Day 3 over every weight in the model, on your data. The compute per token is the same ≈6·N FLOPs as pretraining (Day 6), but your dataset is thousands of times smaller, so compute is rarely the problem. Memory is.

Count the bytes per parameter with the standard mixed-precision Adam recipe. The forward and backward passes run in 16-bit floats: 2 bytes for the weight, 2 bytes for its gradient. The optimizer keeps an fp32 master copy of the weight (4 bytes) so that tiny updates are not rounded away, plus Adam's two running statistics, the momentum and the variance (4 + 4 bytes). Micikevicius 2017 Kingma 2014

training memory ≈ (2 + 2 + 4 + 4 + 4) bytes × N = 16 bytes × N, plus activations

That 16-bytes-per-parameter figure is the one the ZeRO paper used to motivate sharding optimizer state across GPUs. Rajbhandari 2019 Worked example: a 7B model needs 7 × 10⁹ × 16 bytes ≈ 112 GB before a single activation is stored. That does not fit on one 80 GB GPU. Activations add more: Hugging Face's memory guide estimates roughly 85 GB to train a 4B model at batch size 16 in mixed precision. Hugging Face docs A 70B model needs about 1.1 TB of weights, gradients and optimizer state alone, so full fine-tuning at that scale means a multi-GPU cluster with sharded optimizers, exactly the machinery used for pretraining.

Notice where the bytes go: 12 of the 16 exist only because we are training those weights. Train fewer weights and that cost vanishes for the frozen ones. That is the whole idea behind parameter-efficient fine-tuning.

3. LoRA: train a thin detour, not the whole road

Fine-tuning changes a weight matrix W into W + ΔW. The LoRA authors asked whether ΔW really needs as many degrees of freedom as W. Their bet, confirmed empirically, was that the useful change has a low rank, meaning it can be written as the product of two thin matrices. So LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) freezes W and learns only the detour. Hu 2021

h = W·x + (α/r)·B·A·x, with B of shape d×r and A of shape r×d, r ≪ d

A d×d matrix has d² weights; the pair B, A has only 2·d·r. For d = 4096 and r = 8 that is 65,536 trainable numbers in place of 16.8 million, about 0.4%. Four details matter in practice:

  • Initialization. A starts random and B starts at zero, so B·A = 0 and the model begins exactly as the pretrained one. Training can only move it away gradually.
  • Rank r and alpha α. r sets the capacity of the detour; the update is scaled by α/r, so α acts like a learning-rate knob for the adapter. The paper found a rank of 1 to 8 enough when adapting the attention query and value matrices; common 2026 defaults are r = 8 to 64 with α = 2r. Hugging Face docs
  • Which matrices. Under a fixed budget the paper did best adapting Wq and Wv together. Most recipes today attach LoRA to every linear layer, attention and MLP alike, because the extra parameters are still cheap.
  • Merging. After training, compute W′ = W + (α/r)·B·A once and save it: inference costs exactly what the base model costs. Or keep the adapter separate (a few tens of megabytes) and serve many adapters on one base model, swapping per customer.

Now the 7B arithmetic, using Mistral 7B's published shapes: 32 layers, model width 4,096, MLP width 14,336, and 8 key/value heads so the k and v projections are 4,096×1,024. Jiang 2023 With r = 16 on all seven linear matrices per layer (q, k, v, o, gate, up, down), one layer holds 16 × [(4096+4096)·2 + (4096+1024)·2 + (4096+14336)·3] = 1,310,720 adapter weights; times 32 layers is about 41.9 million, roughly 0.6% of the 7.2 billion base weights. With r = 8 on q and v only, the classic paper setting, it is 3.4 million, about 0.05%. The optimizer state for 42M parameters is under 1 GB; the frozen base still sits in memory at 14 GB in bf16, but carries no gradients or Adam moments. For GPT-3 175B the paper reported 10,000× fewer trainable parameters and about 3× less GPU memory, at equal or better quality. Hu 2021

Try it: drag the rank slider and watch the trainable count and the percentage of full fine-tuning.

The big square is the frozen W. The two thin strips are the adapter. Their area, not W's, is what you train, and it grows linearly with the rank while W's cost stays fixed.

Analogy

Full fine-tuning rewrites the textbook. LoRA leaves the book untouched and clips a thin stack of sticky notes onto the pages that matter; a reader sees book plus notes, and the notes can be peeled off and swapped for another set. Quantization, later today, is printing the same book in a smaller font: every sentence is still there, some fine detail is lost, and it fits in your pocket.

One honest caveat. Biderman and colleagues compared LoRA with full fine-tuning on large code and math datasets and found that at typical ranks LoRA learns less, but also forgets less of the base model's other abilities; full fine-tuning's weight changes had ranks 10 to 100× higher than common LoRA settings. Biderman 2024 For most product fine-tunes that trade is exactly what you want. See the LoRA paper review for the experiments.

4. QLoRA: a 65B fine-tune on one GPU

LoRA removes the optimizer bytes but the frozen base still costs 2 bytes per weight: 130 GB for a 65B model, before activations. QLoRA stores that frozen base in 4 bits and trains bf16 LoRA adapters on top of it, backpropagating through the quantized weights (they are dequantized on the fly for each matrix multiply). The result: fine-tuning a 65B model dropped from more than 780 GB of GPU memory to under 48 GB, and the authors' Guanaco models reached 99.3% of ChatGPT's score on the Vicuna benchmark, as judged by GPT-4, after about 24 hours on a single GPU. Dettmers 2023 Three inventions make it work:

  • NF4 (4-bit NormalFloat). Trained weights are roughly bell-shaped around zero. Instead of 16 evenly spaced levels, NF4 places its 16 levels at the quantiles of a normal distribution, so each level is used about equally often and the rounding error is smaller for the same 4 bits. Weights are quantized in blocks of 64, each block with its own scale.
  • Double quantization. Those per-block scales are themselves 32-bit numbers, and one per 64 weights adds half a bit per weight. Quantizing the scales to 8 bits (in blocks of 256) saves about 0.37 bits per parameter, around 3 GB on a 65B model.
  • Paged optimizers. Memory spikes, for example from one long sequence, used to crash runs. QLoRA uses NVIDIA unified memory so optimizer states are paged out to CPU RAM when the GPU fills and paged back when needed.

At hobby scale, a 7B base in NF4 is about 3.5 to 4 GB; with adapters and activations at a modest sequence length the run fits a 16 GB consumer card or a free Colab GPU. The implementation lives in the bitsandbytes library, which also ships 8-bit optimizers and LLM.int8() inference. Hugging Face docs The QLoRA paper review walks through the numbers.

5. Adapters, prefix tuning, prompt tuning

LoRA was not the first parameter-efficient method, and knowing its cousins explains why it won.

MethodWhat is trainedTrainable shareCatch
Adapters Houlsby 2019Small bottleneck layers inserted after attention and MLP≈3.6% per task, within 0.4 points of full fine-tuning on GLUEExtra layers add inference latency; cannot be merged away.
Prefix tuning Li 2021Learned vectors prepended to the keys and values of every layer≈0.1%Eats context length; harder to optimize.
Prompt tuning Lester 2021A few learned "soft tokens" at the input onlyTinyOnly matches full fine-tuning once models reach billions of parameters.
LoRA Hu 2021Low-rank detour on chosen weight matrices0.01 to 1%Merges into W: zero latency; works on any linear layer.

Soft prompts are worth remembering for one reason: they show that a frozen model already contains the skill, and training only finds an input that unlocks it, few-shot prompting (Day 8) with gradient descent choosing the examples.

6. Catastrophic forgetting

Catastrophic forgetting is when training on new data overwrites what the network knew before. The term comes from a 1989 study of small networks and was treated as a curiosity for decades. Wikipedia It is not a curiosity for LLMs: Luo and colleagues fine-tuned 1B to 7B models in sequence on new tasks and saw general abilities, reasoning and reading comprehension decay, with the effect growing with model size in that range. Luo 2023

The reason is mechanical: gradient descent only cares about your loss. If your 5,000 support transcripts never contain Python, nothing protects the weights that write Python, and the updates drift them. Defences, cheapest first: fewer steps at a lower learning rate; a small adapter (LoRA forgets less); a slice of general instruction data mixed back in so the loss still rewards old skills; and a general benchmark run before and after, so you see the damage before users do (Day 13 covers evaluation).

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a friend who has read every book in the library. You want them to answer phone calls for your lemonade stand in your exact style. You could make them re-read everything with your style in mind (full fine-tuning: slow, and it needs a giant desk). Or you could hand them a short cheat sheet (LoRA: quick, tiny, and each stand can get its own). If they practise only lemonade calls for a month they may start forgetting their maths; that is catastrophic forgetting, and the fix is to keep a few maths problems in the practice pile. And if the friend has to fit in a small car, you print their notes in a smaller font (quantization): still readable, a little blurrier.

7. Quantization: fewer bits per weight

Every weight is a number stored in some format. The format decides the file size, and because decoding is limited by how fast weights stream from memory (Day 7), it also decides the speed.

FormatBits (sign / exponent / fraction)Bytes per weight7B modelNotes
fp3232 (1 / 8 / 23)428 GBTraining master copy.
fp1616 (1 / 5 / 10)214 GBPrecise but narrow range; overflows above 65,504.
bf1616 (1 / 8 / 7)214 GBSame range as fp32, less precision; the default for LLM training and inference. Google 2019
int8817 GB256 levels; needs a scale per tensor or per group.
int4 / NF440.5 (plus scales)≈3.5 to 4 GB16 levels; the sweet spot for local inference.

Integer quantization is rounding with a ruler. Take a group of weights, find the largest magnitude m, set a scale s = m / 7 (for symmetric 4-bit, levels −8 to 7), store each weight as the integer round(w / s), and reconstruct it later as q·s. The error is at most s/2 per weight. Where the ruler is shared decides the damage. Per-tensor scaling uses one s for a whole matrix, so a single outlier weight of 50 among thousands near 0.01 stretches the ruler and rounds almost everything to zero. Per-group scaling gives every 32 to 128 consecutive weights their own scale: the outlier only ruins its own group, at the cost of storing more scales. That overhead is why a "4-bit" GGUF file like Q4_K_M actually spends about 4.9 bits per weight. llama.cpp docs Outliers are real: LLM.int8() found that models beyond a few billion parameters develop a few feature dimensions with huge magnitudes; keeping just those in 16-bit while 99.9% of the multiply runs in int8 preserved full quality at 175B. Dettmers 2022

Try it: the widget below quantizes a row of fp16 weights onto an int4 grid. Watch the rounding error per weight and the memory saved.

The grid has only 16 rungs. Weights that fall between rungs get pushed to the nearest one; the bigger the group's largest value, the coarser the rungs for everyone in the group.

Post-training methods try to be smarter than plain rounding, using a few hundred calibration examples and no retraining:

  • GPTQ quantizes one column at a time and uses approximate second-order information to nudge the remaining weights to compensate for each rounding error. It quantized 175B-parameter models in about four GPU-hours. On OPT-175B, WikiText perplexity went from 8.34 in fp16 to 8.37 at 4-bit and 8.68 at 3-bit, where naive rounding gave 10.54 and about 7,300 respectively. Frantar 2022
  • AWQ (activation-aware weight quantization) observes that about 1% of weight channels, the ones that multiply large activations, matter far more than the rest. Instead of keeping them in higher precision, it scales those channels up before rounding, which provably lowers their error, and it ships with a 4-bit kernel more than 3× faster than fp16 Hugging Face inference on desktop and mobile GPUs. Lin 2023
  • GGUF and llama.cpp are the format and runtime most people use on laptops. GGUF is a single, memory-mappable file carrying weights, tokenizer and hyperparameters as typed metadata; ggml docs llama.cpp runs it on CPUs and on Apple, NVIDIA, AMD and Intel GPUs with quantization from 1.5 to 8 bits. llama.cpp The names encode the recipe: Q4_K_M is a 4-bit "k-quant" that keeps the most sensitive tensors at somewhat higher precision; Q8_0 is plain 8-bit with per-block scales.

Real numbers, from the llama.cpp k-quants benchmark on the original LLaMA 7B and 13B (perplexity on WikiText; lower is better): llama.cpp 2023

Quant7B size7B perplexity13B size13B perplexity
F1613.0 GB5.90725.0 GB5.254
Q6_K5.15 GB5.9119.95 GB5.257
Q5_K_M4.45 GB5.9218.60 GB5.264
Q4_K_M3.80 GB5.9607.32 GB5.300
Q3_K_M3.06 GB6.1505.88 GB5.450
Q2_K2.67 GB6.7765.13 GB5.855

Read it twice. First, 6-bit and 5-bit are essentially free, 4-bit costs about 1% in perplexity, 3-bit about 4%, and 2-bit about 15%. Second, the 13B model at Q4_K_M (7.3 GB, perplexity 5.30) beats the 7B model in fp16 (13 GB, perplexity 5.91) at half the memory: for a fixed budget, a bigger model quantized harder usually wins, down to about 4 bits. Downstream tasks agree: Llama-2-7B scored 54.3 on the Open LLM Leaderboard average in fp16 and about 53.3 in 4-bit, with a smaller gap for 13B. Hugging Face 2023 Small models and reasoning-heavy outputs suffer most, so always test on your own task.

8. Knowledge distillation: a small model imitates a big one

Knowledge distillation trains a small student to match a big teacher. The classic version, from Hinton, Vinyals and Dean, trains the student on the teacher's full output distribution (the soft labels) rather than on the single correct answer. Hinton 2015 The soft labels carry far more information per example: a hard label says "the next token is cat"; the teacher's distribution says "cat 60%, kitten 25%, dog 3%, car 0.01%", which also teaches the student which mistakes are near-misses. DistilBERT applied this to BERT and kept 97% of its language-understanding score in a model 40% smaller and 60% faster. Sanh 2019

Distillation is now how most good small models are made:

  • Gemma 2's 2B and 9B models were trained with knowledge distillation instead of plain next-token prediction, and every Gemma 3 model, from 1B to 27B, is trained with distillation. Gemma Team 2024 Gemma Team 2025
  • Llama 3.2's 1B and 3B models were created by structured pruning of Llama 3.1 8B, then recovered by distillation using logits from the 8B and 70B models as token-level targets during pretraining. Meta 2024
  • DeepSeek-R1's distilled models (Qwen 1.5B to 32B, Llama 8B and 70B) use the other flavour, sometimes called sequence-level or data distillation: the student is simply fine-tuned on about 800k reasoning traces written by the 671B teacher, with no access to its logits. DeepSeek 2025 It is the same trick as synthetic-data SFT, and why a 7B model can suddenly "think" like a model a hundred times bigger on math problems. The DeepSeek-R1 review has the numbers.

Both flavours ship in Hugging Face TRL: DistillationTrainer and the generalized GKDTrainer for logit matching, and plain SFTTrainer on teacher-generated text. Hugging Face docs

9. Pruning and sparsity, briefly

Pruning deletes weights. Unstructured pruning zeroes individual weights: SparseGPT pruned OPT-175B to 50 to 60% zeros in one shot, with no retraining and a negligible perplexity increase, Frantar 2023 and Wanda gets close with an even simpler score, weight magnitude times the typical activation it meets. Sun 2023 The catch is hardware: scattered zeros do not make a GPU matrix multiply faster. Semi-structured 2:4 sparsity, exactly two zeros in every block of four, is the pattern NVIDIA tensor cores can skip, with a theoretical 2× throughput. NVIDIA 2021 Structured pruning removes whole heads, layers or width, which helps on any hardware but hurts more, so it is followed by distillation, as in Llama 3.2 1B and 3B and NVIDIA's Nemotron-H 47B (pruned and distilled from the 56B). NVIDIA 2025

10. How to fine-tune a small model today

As of October 2026 the standard open-source stack is Hugging Face transformers for the model, peft for LoRA and QLoRA, trl for the training loops (SFT, DPO, GRPO), and optionally Unsloth, which patches the same stack with hand-written kernels and advertises about 2× faster training with 70% less VRAM, plus free Colab notebooks. Hugging Face docs Unsloth A complete LoRA run on a chat dataset:

from datasets import load_dataset
from peft import LoraConfig
from trl import SFTConfig, SFTTrainer

data = load_dataset("trl-lib/Capybara", split="train")   # rows of {"messages": [...]}
lora = LoraConfig(r=16, lora_alpha=32, lora_dropout=0.05,
                  target_modules=["q_proj", "k_proj", "v_proj", "o_proj",
                                  "gate_proj", "up_proj", "down_proj"])
args = SFTConfig(output_dir="out", max_length=1024, learning_rate=1e-4,
                 num_train_epochs=1, per_device_train_batch_size=4,
                 gradient_accumulation_steps=4, bf16=True)
trainer = SFTTrainer(model="Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B", train_dataset=data,
                     args=args, peft_config=lora)   # add quantization_config=... for QLoRA
trainer.train()
trainer.save_model("out")          # writes only the adapter, a few tens of MB

The trainer applies the chat template, computes the loss on the assistant's reply rather than the prompt, and uses a higher learning rate (≈1e-4) than full fine-tuning because only new parameters are learned. Hugging Face docs The parts the code does not show are the parts that decide success:

  1. Data. 500 to 5,000 clean, consistent examples of exactly the behaviour you want. One bad template or a mixed tone costs more than any hyperparameter.
  2. Base model. An instruction-tuned small model (0.5B to 8B) that already does most of the job; fine-tuning is for the last stretch.
  3. Evaluation before and after, on a held-out slice of your task and on a general benchmark, to catch forgetting.
  4. Shipping. Merge the adapter (merge_and_unload()) and quantize to GGUF for local use, or serve the adapter next to the base model with a multi-LoRA server.

Going deeper (optional)

Why would the fine-tuning update be low-rank at all? The LoRA paper builds on earlier work showing that pretrained models have a low "intrinsic dimension": training restricted to a random low-dimensional subspace still reaches most of full fine-tuning's accuracy, so the task-specific change lives in a small subspace of the weights. LoRA picks that subspace per matrix and lets gradient descent choose its basis. Hu 2021 Two refinements in 2026 model cards: rank-stabilized LoRA scales by α/√r instead of α/r so that large ranks keep learning, and LoftQ initializes the adapter to cancel the quantization error of a QLoRA base. Hugging Face docs

Common misunderstandings

  • "Fine-tune the model on our documents so it knows them." Fine-tuning teaches style and skills far better than facts, and facts change. Retrieve the documents at run time; fine-tune only to teach the model how to use them.
  • "4-bit is exactly a quarter of the size." Scales and the few tensors kept in higher precision push real 4-bit files to about 4.5 to 5 bits per weight; a 7B model lands near 4 GB, not 3.5.
  • "LoRA is always as good as full fine-tuning." On large, hard datasets such as code and math, LoRA at low rank learns less; it wins on cost and on preserving base abilities, not on peak accuracy.
  • "Quantized models are slower because of the extra decoding step." Decoding is memory-bound, so streaming fewer bytes per weight makes it faster; dequantization is cheap by comparison. Only heavily batched prefill can lose.
  • "More epochs will fix it." Past one to three epochs on a small set, the model memorizes your examples and forgets more of everything else.

Check yourself

Q1. A 7B model is fine-tuned with Adam in mixed precision. Before activations, how much GPU memory do the weights, gradients and optimizer state need, and why?

Answer 2. The weights alone are 14 GB, but training also stores a gradient, an fp32 copy and two Adam statistics for every parameter, 16 bytes in total. Option 1 is inference memory, not training memory.

Q2. LoRA with rank r is attached to a d×d weight matrix. How many parameters are trained for that matrix?

Answer 4. B is d×r and A is r×d, so d·r + r·d. With d = 4096 and r = 8 that is 65,536 trainable numbers against 16.8 million in W, which stays frozen.

Q3. Which parts of a QLoRA run are stored in 4-bit?

Answer 1. The 4-bit NF4 base is dequantized on the fly for each matrix multiply; gradients flow through it into bf16 adapters, which are trained normally. Training the adapters in 4-bit would make the updates too coarse to learn.

Q4. Why does per-group scaling (one scale per 32 to 128 weights) beat per-tensor scaling (one scale per matrix)?

Answer 3. The scale is set by the largest magnitude in the group; sharing it across a whole matrix lets one outlier crush everyone else to zero. Per-group scaling costs slightly more bits (option 1 is backwards), which is why Q4_K_M is about 4.9 bits per weight.

Q5. In classic knowledge distillation the student is trained on the teacher's soft labels. What extra information do soft labels carry that hard labels do not?

Answer 2. A hard label is one token; a soft label is the whole distribution, so each example teaches the shape of the teacher's beliefs. DeepSeek-R1's distilled models instead train on the teacher's generated text, which is the sequence-level variant.

Q6. You fine-tune a 3B instruction model on 4,000 support transcripts and it stops writing working Python. The cheapest fixes are:

Answer 4. This is catastrophic forgetting; options 1 to 3 all move the weights further from the base model and make it worse. LoRA forgets less than full fine-tuning, and replaying general data keeps the loss rewarding old skills.

Recap: five things to remember

  1. Prompt first, retrieve for facts, fine-tune for skills and form. Fine-tuning is a poor way to add knowledge.
  2. Full fine-tuning costs about 16 bytes per parameter (weights, gradients, fp32 copy, two Adam moments): 112 GB for a 7B model before activations.
  3. LoRA freezes W and trains a thin detour B·A; with rank 16 on every linear layer of a 7B model that is about 42M trainable parameters, under 1%. Merge it back for zero inference overhead.
  4. QLoRA keeps the frozen base in NF4 with double quantization and paged optimizers, bringing a 65B fine-tune under 48 GB and a 7B fine-tune onto a free Colab GPU.
  5. For inference, 8-bit is lossless, 4 to 6 bits nearly so, and a bigger model quantized to 4 bits usually beats a smaller one in fp16 at the same memory. Distillation, pruning and quantization combine to make the small models you actually run.

References

  1. LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models — Hu, Shen, Wallis, Allen-Zhu, Li, Wang, Wang, Chen (Microsoft), ICLR 2022 (arXiv 2021). The method behind almost every fine-tune you will run; Section 7 on which matrices and ranks to adapt is the practical core.
  2. QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs — Dettmers, Pagnoni, Holtzman, Zettlemoyer, NeurIPS 2023. NF4, double quantization and paged optimizers; Section 3 is a short, readable account of all three.
  3. Parameter-Efficient Transfer Learning for NLP — Houlsby et al. (Google), ICML 2019. The adapter paper: the first demonstration that training 3.6% of the weights can match full fine-tuning.
  4. Prefix-Tuning: Optimizing Continuous Prompts for Generation — Li and Liang (Stanford), ACL 2021. Learned prefixes at every layer with 0.1% of the parameters; the ancestor of soft prompting.
  5. The Power of Scale for Parameter-Efficient Prompt Tuning — Lester, Al-Rfou, Constant (Google), EMNLP 2021. Shows soft prompts catch up with full fine-tuning only at billions of parameters; a clean example of a scale-dependent result.
  6. ZeRO: Memory Optimizations Toward Training Trillion Parameter Models — Rajbhandari, Rasley, Ruwase, He (Microsoft), SC 2020 (arXiv 2019). Section 3 contains the 16-bytes-per-parameter accounting used throughout this lesson.
  7. LLM.int8(): 8-bit Matrix Multiplication for Transformers at Scale — Dettmers, Lewis, Belkada, Zettlemoyer, NeurIPS 2022. Where the outlier-feature problem was discovered; explains why naive quantization breaks above 6B parameters.
  8. GPTQ: Accurate Post-Training Quantization for Generative Pre-trained Transformers — Frantar, Ashkboos, Hoefler, Alistarh, ICLR 2023. The one-shot 3- and 4-bit method with the perplexity tables quoted above.
  9. AWQ: Activation-aware Weight Quantization for LLM Compression and Acceleration — Lin et al. (MIT), MLSys 2024 best paper. The 1%-salient-channels observation and the scaling trick that protects them without mixed precision.
  10. llama.cpp — ggml-org, GitHub. The runtime behind local inference; read the quantize tool's README for what each Q-name means and what it costs.
  11. PEFT documentation — Hugging Face. The LoRA conceptual guide and LoraConfig reference; the place to check rank, alpha, target modules and merging options.
  12. TRL: SFT Trainer — Hugging Face. Dataset formats, chat templates, completion-only loss and the PEFT integration used in today's code example.
  13. Distilling the Knowledge in a Neural Network — Hinton, Vinyals, Dean (Google), 2015. Nine pages that define soft labels, temperature and "dark knowledge".
  14. DistilBERT, a distilled version of BERT — Sanh, Debut, Chaumond, Wolf (Hugging Face), 2019. The first widely used distilled language model; a short, concrete recipe.
  15. LoRA Learns Less and Forgets Less — Biderman et al. (Databricks Mosaic), TMLR 2024. The most careful public comparison of LoRA and full fine-tuning; read it before promising a client that LoRA is "just as good".

Next: Day 11 — Architectures beyond vanilla: MoE, long context, state-space models, multimodal

Week 2 · Day 11

Architectures beyond vanilla: MoE, long context, state-space models, multimodal

After today you can read a model-card line like "671B total / 37B active, MLA, 128k context, natively multimodal" and explain every term, including what it costs and what it does not buy you.

About 90 min · Builds on Day 10

Today's big idea

The vanilla Transformer from Day 5 pays three bills on every token: compute proportional to all its parameters, a key-value cache that grows with context, and attention work that grows with the square of context. Each architecture today attacks one bill. Mixture of Experts makes parameters cheap per token; FlashAttention, grouped-query attention, latent attention and sliding windows make attention cheap; state-space models drop the quadratic term; and multimodal designs feed pixels and audio through the same next-token machine.

1. Three bills every vanilla Transformer pays

Recall the Day 5 block: attention, then an MLP, repeated L times. For one new token at inference, three costs scale badly.

  • Compute per token ∝ parameters. Every weight is multiplied once per token, about 2·N FLOPs (Day 6): a 400B dense model does 400 billion multiply-adds to produce the word "the".
  • KV cache ∝ layers × KV heads × context. Each layer stores a key and a value per head for every past token (Day 7); long chats and big batches run out of memory before they run out of compute.
  • Attention work ∝ context². A 128k-token prompt means 128k × 128k score pairs per head per layer, about 1.7 × 10¹⁰, and naive implementations write that whole matrix to memory.

A fourth limit is a wall rather than a bill: the vanilla model reads only text. The rest of this day is one fix per line.

2. Mixture of Experts: more parameters, same compute per token

The MLP block holds about two-thirds of a Transformer block's parameters (8d² against attention's 4d², from the Day 5 counts). Mixture of Experts (MoE) replaces that one MLP with E copies, the experts, and adds a tiny router: a linear layer that scores each token against each expert, takes a softmax, and sends the token to the top-k experts. Only those k MLPs run; their outputs are blended using the router's weights. Attention, embeddings and norms are shared by all tokens as before.

y = Σ over the top-k experts i of g_i(x) · Expert_i(x), where g = softmax(W_router · x)

The payoff is a decoupling that dense models cannot offer: parameters (how much the model can know) grow with E, but compute per token grows only with k. Shazeer and colleagues introduced the sparsely-gated layer in 2017, scaling an LSTM-based model to 137B parameters with thousands of experts for "greater than 1000× improvements in model capacity with only minor losses in computational efficiency". Shazeer 2017 GShard put it into a 600B-parameter Transformer, Lepikhin 2020 and Switch Transformer simplified routing to a single expert per token (k = 1), reaching the same loss as T5-Base up to 7× faster at equal compute and training a trillion-parameter model. Fedus 2021

Try it: the widget routes a stream of tokens to the top-2 of 8 experts and keeps a tally of active versus total parameters.

Every token touches two experts, so the active count is a quarter of the expert parameters plus everything shared. Watch the per-expert counters: if the router falls in love with one expert, the others stop learning. That is the load-balancing problem.

3. Load balancing, and the models that proved MoE works

A router trained only on the language loss has a bad habit: it finds one slightly better expert, sends it more tokens, which trains it more, until most experts sit idle. Switch Transformer added an auxiliary load-balancing loss, the product of the fraction of tokens sent to each expert and the router probability it assigned, summed over experts, which is smallest when traffic is uniform, plus a capacity factor that caps how many tokens an expert can accept per batch (overflow tokens are dropped and pass through the residual connection). Fedus 2021 DeepSeek-V3 showed you can balance without an extra loss: each expert carries a bias that is added to its routing score for selection only, nudged down when it is overloaded and up when idle, so the language loss is never distorted. DeepSeek 2024

Three releases turned MoE from a research result into the default for big models:

  • Mixtral 8x7B (released December 2023, paper January 2024): 8 experts per layer, top-2 routing, 46.7B parameters in total but 12.9B used per token, 32k context, Apache 2.0. It matched or beat Llama 2 70B on every benchmark the authors reported while running at the cost of a 13B model. Mistral 2023 Jiang 2024 The name misleads: "8×7B" is not 56B because attention is shared and only the MLPs are replicated. See the Mixtral paper review.
  • DeepSeek-V3 (December 2024): 671B total, 37B active, 61 layers, each MoE layer with 256 small routed experts plus 1 shared expert that every token visits, 8 routed experts active per token. The fine-grained split and the always-on shared expert come from DeepSeekMoE, where many small experts combine more flexibly than a few large ones. Dai 2024 It was trained on 14.8T tokens in FP8 for 2.788M H800 GPU-hours. DeepSeek 2024
  • Llama 4 (April 2025): Meta's first MoE. Scout has 17B active of 109B total with 16 experts; Maverick has 17B active of 400B with 128 routed experts plus a shared one. Meta 2025

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Picture a hospital. In an ordinary one (a dense model) every doctor looks at every patient, so adding doctors makes each visit slower. In a mixture-of-experts hospital a receptionist (the router) sends each patient to the two doctors who fit best, so the hospital can hire hundreds of doctors and a visit takes the same time. The receptionist has to be fair: if she always picks Dr. Lee, the other doctors never learn. Attention is a doctor re-reading your whole file for every new sentence; grouped-query attention makes four doctors share one copy of the file; a Mamba doctor keeps one page of notes she updates as you talk. A multimodal hospital lets the doctor look at your X-ray and listen to your heartbeat, not just read the form.

4. The trade-off, and who uses MoE as of October 2026

MoE buys compute with memory. Mixtral runs like a 13B model but must hold 46.7B weights: 93 GB in bf16, a server model unless quantized to 4 bits (roughly 25 to 30 GB). DeepSeek-V3's 671B weights fill 671 GB even in 8-bit, so serving it means a multi-GPU node with expert parallelism: each GPU hosts some experts and tokens are shipped to wherever their expert lives. The win shows up at scale: serving thousands of users, GPU memory is shared anyway and what you pay per token is the 37B of compute, not the 671B of storage. For one user on one GPU a dense model of the same footprint is often the better deal, which is why Qwen3 and Gemma ship dense small models and MoE large ones.

Model (release)Total / activeExperts (active per token)
Mixtral 8x7B (Dec 2023) Jiang 202446.7B / 12.9B8 (2)
Grok-1 (2024, open weights) xAI 2024314B / not stated8 (2)
DeepSeek-V3 and R1 (Dec 2024, Jan 2025) DeepSeek 2024671B / 37B256 routed + 1 shared (8 + 1)
Llama 4 Scout and Maverick (Apr 2025) Meta 2025109B / 17B and 400B / 17B16, and 128 + shared
Qwen3-235B-A22B (Apr 2025) Qwen 2025235B / 22B128 (8)
Kimi K2 (Jul 2025) Kimi Team 20251T / 32BMoE; 15.5T training tokens
gpt-oss-120b (Aug 2025) OpenAI 2025117B / 5.1BMoE; fits one 80 GB GPU in MXFP4
Nemotron 3 Nano (Dec 2025) NVIDIA 202530B / 3BMoE inside a Mamba hybrid
Muse Spark Open (Oct 2026) Meta 2026816B / 42B256 (8)

Among closed models, Google said in February 2024 that Gemini 1.5 uses a Mixture-of-Experts architecture. Google 2024 OpenAI and Anthropic have not published their frontier architectures as of October 2026, so claims that GPT-5 or Claude are MoE are inferences from pricing and speed; OpenAI's open-weight gpt-oss models are MoE, which is suggestive and nothing more. "Every frontier model is MoE" is a reasonable guess: confirmed for most open models and for Gemini, undisclosed for the rest.

Going deeper (optional)

The Switch auxiliary loss is α·E·Σ f_i·P_i, where f_i is the fraction of tokens routed to expert i, P_i the mean router probability for it, E the number of experts and α a small coefficient (10⁻² in the paper). Since Σ f_i = Σ P_i = 1, the sum is minimized when both are uniform, and it is differentiable through P. The capacity factor sets each expert's buffer to (tokens per batch / E) × factor; above 1 it wastes memory but drops fewer tokens. Fedus 2021 DeepSeek-V3's bias-based balancing removes the auxiliary loss's gradient interference, which the paper credits for cleaner expert specialization. DeepSeek 2024

5. FlashAttention: the same answer, fewer trips to memory

A GPU has a small, very fast on-chip memory (SRAM, tens of megabytes) and a large, slower high-bandwidth memory (HBM, tens of gigabytes). Standard attention computes the N×N score matrix, writes it to HBM, reads it back for the softmax, writes it again, and reads it again to multiply by V. For N = 8k that is 67 million numbers per head per layer, and the time goes to moving them, not to arithmetic. FlashAttention is IO-aware: it splits Q, K and V into tiles that fit in SRAM, computes the softmax incrementally with a running maximum and sum, and never materializes the full matrix. The output is exactly the same attention. It was 3× faster on GPT-2 at 1k context and 2.4× faster on long-range benchmarks, and it drops attention's memory from quadratic to linear in N, which is why long contexts became trainable at all. Dao 2022 FlashAttention-2 doubled the speed by splitting work better across GPU threads, reaching 50 to 73% of the A100's peak, Dao 2023 and FlashAttention-3 rewrote it for H100s with asynchronous copies and FP8, hitting 75% utilization at 740 TFLOPs/s. Shah 2024 Every serious stack uses it or a descendant; see the FlashAttention paper review.

Analogy

FlashAttention is doing your sums at your desk instead of walking to the filing cabinet after every line: same numbers, far fewer trips. Grouped-query attention is a study group where four students share one set of notes instead of each copying their own: the notes (keys and values) take a quarter of the shelf, and the four still ask their own questions (queries).

6. Fewer keys and values: MQA, GQA, MLA and sliding windows

FlashAttention speeds up prefill. In decoding the bottleneck is different: each new token re-reads the whole KV cache, so cache size sets the speed and how many users fit on a GPU. The lever is to store fewer keys and values.

Multi-query attention (MQA) keeps many query heads but a single shared key head and value head, cutting the cache by the number of heads at a small quality cost. Shazeer 2019 Grouped-query attention (GQA) is the compromise: query heads are divided into groups and each group shares one KV head. Ainslie and colleagues converted existing multi-head checkpoints to GQA with about 5% of the original pretraining compute, landing near multi-head quality at near multi-query speed. Ainslie 2023 Llama 2 used GQA for its 34B and 70B models, Touvron 2023 Llama 3 for every size, Meta 2024 and nearly every open model since follows (see the Llama 3 paper review).

Worked example with Mistral 7B: 32 layers, 32 query heads, 8 KV heads, head size 128. Jiang 2023 Each past token costs 2 (K and V) × 32 layers × 8 heads × 128 numbers × 2 bytes = 131 KB; with 32 KV heads it would be 524 KB. At a 32k-token context that is 4.3 GB per conversation instead of 17 GB, which decides how many long conversations fit next to the 14 GB of weights on a 24 GB card.

Multi-head latent attention (MLA), DeepSeek's design, goes further: instead of caching K and V, it caches a single compressed latent vector per token (512 numbers in DeepSeek-V3) and reconstructs keys and values from it with small matrices at attention time. DeepSeek-V2 reported a 93.3% smaller KV cache than its 67B dense predecessor with no quality loss, and V3 kept the design. DeepSeek 2024

Sliding-window attention limits each token to the previous w tokens, making attention cost linear in context. Longformer introduced the windowed-plus-global pattern for long documents in 2020. Beltagy 2020 Mistral 7B uses a 4,096-token window in every layer; because a token in layer ℓ has read a window of layer ℓ−1's outputs, information can still travel 32 × 4,096 ≈ 131k tokens through the stack. Jiang 2023 Gemma 3 interleaves five local layers (window 1,024) with every global layer, which keeps the KV cache small at 128k context because only one layer in six stores the full history. Gemma Team 2025

7. Long context, and whether the model actually uses it

Why does a model trained on 8k tokens fall apart at 16k? Because of positions. With RoPE (Day 5, RoPE review) each position rotates the query and key by an angle proportional to its index; positions past the training length produce rotation angles the model has never seen, and the attention scores turn to noise. Su 2021 The fixes rescale the positions. Position interpolation squeezes the new, longer range into the old one (position 16,000 is presented as 8,000), which Chen and colleagues used to stretch Llama to 32k with about 1,000 fine-tuning steps. Chen 2023 YaRN is choosier about which frequencies to stretch: the slow-rotating dimensions that encode long range get interpolated, the fast ones that encode local order are left alone, and attention is slightly re-scaled; it needed 10× fewer tokens and 2.5× fewer steps than earlier methods. Peng 2023 YaRN-extended Llama 2 checkpoints reached 128k, Nous Research 2023 and YaRN is now a standard switch in model configs: Qwen3-Next is 262k native and stretches to about 1M with it. Qwen 2025

Where things stand as of October 2026: Llama 3.1 shipped 128k in 2024; Meta 2024 Gemini 1.5 Pro put 1M tokens into production in 2024 after testing 10M in research; Google 2024 Llama 4 Scout advertises 10M; Meta 2025 and 1M is now the default window for current Claude and Gemini models. Anthropic docs Google docs So 128k is the floor and 1M the norm, with 10M a marketing figure.

A long window is not the same as using it. The needle-in-a-haystack test hides one sentence in a long document and asks for it, sweeping context length and needle depth. Kamradt 2023 Models pass it easily, and it is almost the only test vendors quote. RULER added multi-needle, multi-hop and aggregation tasks across 17 models claiming 32k or more: nearly all passed the single needle, yet only about half kept acceptable scores at 32k on the harder tasks. Hsieh 2024 "Lost in the Middle" found a U-shaped curve: facts at the beginning or end of the context are used well, facts in the middle are often missed, even by long-context models. Liu 2023 Anthropic's documentation calls the degradation "context rot" and recommends curating what goes in. Anthropic docs So put instructions and key facts at the start or end, do not pad, and for large corpora retrieve (Day 12) rather than stuff.

8. State-space models: Mamba and the hybrids

Attention's superpower is also its cost: every token can look at every earlier token, so the memory of the past is the past itself, stored verbatim in the KV cache. A state-space model (SSM) returns to the recurrent idea from Day 4: keep one fixed-size state, a running summary, and update it once per token. Time is linear in length and per-step memory is constant. S4 showed in 2021 that a carefully structured version could beat Transformers on long-range benchmarks, solving a 16k-length task on which everything else failed. Gu 2021 But early SSMs used the same update rule for every token, so they could not decide what to remember.

Mamba (Gu and Dao, December 2023) made the update selective: how much of the old state to keep, and how much of the new token to write in, are computed from the token itself, so the model can copy an important name into memory and ignore filler. That broke the fast convolutional shortcut earlier SSMs relied on, so the authors wrote a hardware-aware scan that runs the recurrence in parallel on GPUs. The result had 5× the inference throughput of a Transformer, scaled linearly to million-token sequences, and Mamba-3B matched Transformers twice its size on language modeling. Gu 2023 Mamba-2 then showed that SSMs and a form of linear attention are the same object seen from two sides ("state space duality"), and used the connection to make the layer 2 to 8× faster. Dao 2024

Pure SSMs have a known weakness: exact recall of a specific earlier token (copying a long ID, in-context lookup) is hard when the past is compressed into a fixed state. The practical answer is a hybrid: mostly SSM or linear-attention layers, with a few full-attention layers for precise retrieval. As of October 2026 the family is large:

  • Jamba (AI21, March 2024): one attention layer per seven Mamba layers, MoE with 16 experts and top-2 routing, 52B total and 12B active, 256k context on a single 80 GB GPU. Lieber 2024
  • Nemotron-H (NVIDIA, 2025): 8B and 56B models that replace most attention layers with Mamba layers, up to 3× faster at inference than similar-size Transformers; NVIDIA 2025 followed by Nemotron 3 (December 2025), a Mamba-2 plus MoE hybrid family with a 30B-total, 3B-active Nano and context up to 1M. NVIDIA 2025
  • IBM Granite 4.0 (October 2025): Mamba-2 and Transformer blocks at 9:1, from a 3B dense hybrid to a 32B-total, 9B-active MoE, with IBM claiming over 70% less RAM than conventional Transformers on long, batched inputs. IBM 2025
  • Qwen3-Next 80B-A3B (2025): three Gated DeltaNet (linear-attention) blocks for every gated full-attention block, 512 experts with 10 plus one shared active per token, 262k native context. Qwen 2025
  • Kimi Linear (Moonshot, October 2025): a 48B-total, 3B-active model mixing its Kimi Delta Attention with MLA layers, cutting the KV cache by up to 75% and decoding up to 6× faster at 1M context. Kimi Team 2025

The pattern is consistent: labs that care most about serving cost ship hybrids, and the biggest closed models have not disclosed whether they do. Hybrids have not replaced attention; they have made it one ingredient among several.

9. Multimodal: pixels and sound as tokens

A Transformer does not care what a token means; it only needs a vector per position. That observation is the whole of multimodality. The Vision Transformer (ViT) cuts an image into 16×16-pixel patches, flattens each into a vector, and runs a plain Transformer encoder over the patch sequence, matching convolutional networks once pretrained on enough images. Dosovitskiy 2020 CLIP trained such an image encoder together with a text encoder on 400 million image–caption pairs with a contrastive objective: within a batch, pull each image's vector toward its own caption and push it away from the others. The result is an image encoder whose features are organized by language: it classifies ImageNet "zero-shot" by comparing with the text "a photo of a dog", matching a supervised ResNet-50 without one ImageNet training label. Radford 2021 SigLIP swapped the softmax contrastive loss for a per-pair sigmoid loss that scales better, and its 400M-parameter encoder is the one inside Gemma 3. Zhai 2023 Gemma Team 2025

Then connect the encoder to an LLM. The LLaVA recipe is a vision encoder (a CLIP ViT), a small projector that maps each patch feature into the LLM's token-embedding space, and an off-the-shelf LLM; image patches enter the Transformer as if they were a few hundred word tokens. Trained on instruction-following data that language-only GPT-4 generated from image captions, it reached GPT-4-style visual chat in 2023 at academic cost. Liu 2023 Flamingo had earlier bridged pretrained vision and language models with cross-attention, learning new tasks from a few in-prompt examples. Alayrac 2022 Llama 3's image, video and speech abilities use this "compositional" style, adapters on top of the text model. Meta 2024

Natively multimodal models skip the bolt-on: images, audio and text are tokenized and one network is pretrained on all of them from the start. Google described Gemini as "built from the ground up to be multimodal". Google 2023 OpenAI's GPT-4o is "an autoregressive omni model" trained "end-to-end across text, vision, and audio", so speech goes in and speech comes out without a separate transcription step. OpenAI 2024 Meta's Chameleon showed early-fusion token models that both read and generate images, Chameleon Team 2024 and Llama 4 uses early fusion of text and vision tokens in one backbone. Meta 2025 The costs are real: an image at useful resolution becomes hundreds to thousands of tokens, and mixed-modality training is harder to stabilize.

Two neighbours, briefly. For speech, Whisper is an encoder-decoder Transformer trained on 680,000 hours of audio paired with transcripts, and it remains the standard open recognizer; omni models fold the same idea into the LLM. Radford 2022 For image generation, most systems are not next-token models but latent diffusion: a network learns to remove noise step by step in an autoencoder's compressed latent space, conditioned on text through cross-attention, the design behind Stable Diffusion. Rombach 2021 Chameleon-style autoregressive image tokens are the main alternative, and the two are converging inside omni models.

10. Six open models, side by side

Model (year)Dense or MoE (total / active)AttentionContextNotable
Llama 3.1 (2024) Meta 2024Dense 8B, 70B, 405BGQA, RoPE128kOver 15T training tokens; 128k-token vocabulary; vision and speech via adapters. Meta 2024
Mistral 7B (2023) Jiang 2023Dense 7.2BGQA, 32 query / 8 KV heads; sliding window 4,0968k trainedBeat Llama 2 13B at roughly half the size.
