Paper Bible
The papers that built modern LLMs.

Eighteen landmark papers, each explained three ways: in one sentence, for a 10-year-old, and step by step for an adult who wants the real mechanism. With the headline numbers, how to read the original, and what came after.

2017 · Google Brain / Google Research

Attention Is All You Need

Replaced recurrence with attention, and every large language model since has been built on the result.

arXiv:1706.03762 · Vaswani, Shazeer, Parmar, Uszkoreit, Jones, Gomez, Kaiser, Polosukhin · NeurIPS 2017

In one sentence

A sequence model made only of attention and small feed-forward layers, with no recurrence or convolution, beat the best translation systems of 2017 at a fraction of their training cost, because every token is processed in parallel and any two tokens are one step apart.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a class translating a long sentence from English into German. The old way worked like a game of telephone: the first student reads one word, whispers a summary to the next, who adds the next word, and so on down the row. By the time the message reaches the end, the first words are fuzzy, and nobody can start until the person before them is done. This paper's way is different. Every student holds one word and can look at every other student at the same time. When the student holding "it" needs to know what "it" means, she glances around the room, notices "the animal" is waving, and pays attention to that word. Everyone does this at once, so the whole class finishes in one round instead of thirty. Then they do a few more rounds, each time looking around again with sharper questions. Because nobody waits in line, you can give the class a much longer sentence and many more students, and it still finishes quickly. That one trick, looking everywhere at once, is what all of today's chatbots are built on.

The problem they wanted to solve

In 2017 the best systems for turning one sequence into another, such as translating English into German, were recurrent neural networks (networks that read one token at a time and carry a memory forward). They had a built-in bottleneck: step t cannot start until step t−1 is finished, so one long sentence cannot be spread across the thousands of cores of a GPU. They also struggled with distance. To connect the first word of a sentence with the fortieth, information had to survive forty hand-offs. An earlier idea, attention (letting the decoder look back over all encoder states and weight them), had been bolted onto recurrent models to help with exactly that Bahdanau 2014, but the recurrence was still underneath. Convolutional alternatives ran in parallel but needed a number of layers that grew with the distance between two positions.

The key idea

Remove the recurrence entirely and let attention do all the work. In self-attention, every token builds a query, compares it against the keys of every other token in the sequence, and takes a weighted average of their values. One layer connects any pair of positions directly, so the maximum path length between two tokens is constant instead of growing with the sequence; Table 1 of the paper makes this comparison against recurrent and convolutional layers Vaswani 2017. Because nothing inside a layer depends on the previous position, all positions are computed at once as a few matrix multiplications, which is what GPUs are good at. Two supporting tricks make it work: multi-head attention (running several smaller attentions in parallel so different heads can track different relationships) and positional encoding (adding a position-dependent pattern to each embedding, because attention by itself has no idea which token came first).

How it works, step by step

  1. Each input token becomes a 512-number embedding, and a fixed sinusoidal pattern for its position is added to it (Section 3.5).
  2. The encoder is a stack of 6 identical layers. Each runs multi-head self-attention, adds the result back to its input (a residual connection) and normalises; then a two-layer feed-forward network (512 → 2048 → 512) is applied to each position separately, again with a residual and normalisation.
  3. Attention itself is softmax(QKᵀ/√d_k)·V. The division by √d_k matters: with 64-dimensional keys the raw dot products have variance about 64, which would push the softmax into a region with tiny gradients (Section 3.2.1).
  4. Multi-head: the 512 dimensions are split into 8 heads of 64; each head does its own attention, and the outputs are concatenated and projected back to 512.
  5. The decoder also has 6 layers, with one extra attention block that reads the encoder output, and its self-attention is masked so position i can only see positions before i. That mask is what lets the model generate one token at a time (Day 7).
  6. A final linear layer and softmax give a probability for every vocabulary entry; training minimises cross-entropy with label smoothing, using Adam with a learning-rate warm-up (Section 5).

Try it: in the widget below, tap a word and watch which other words it attends to, with the raw Q·K scores and the softmax that turns them into weights.

Every word gets its own weights over the whole sentence, all computed in one shot. Attention is a learned, soft lookup table. Day 4 builds this from scratch.

What the results showed

Model (WMT 2014)EN→DE BLEUEN→FR BLEUTraining FLOPs
ConvS2S, best prior single model25.1640.469.6×10¹⁸ / 1.5×10²⁰
ConvS2S ensemble26.3641.297.7×10¹⁹ / 1.2×10²¹
Transformer base (65M params)27.338.13.3×10¹⁸
Transformer big (213M params)28.441.82.3×10¹⁹

Numbers from Table 2 Vaswani 2017. The big model beat every previous result, including ensembles, by more than 2 BLEU on English–German, and the base model matched or beat prior single models at a small fraction of their training cost. Training took about 12 hours (base) or 3.5 days (big) on 8 P100 GPUs. A 4-layer version also reached 91.3 F1 on English constituency parsing with only the standard treebank, so this was not a translation-only trick (Section 6.3).

Why it mattered

Parallelism is the whole story. Once a model no longer had to process tokens one after another, training could be spread across hundreds of accelerators, which is what made the scaling of GPT-2, GPT-3 and everything after possible (Day 6). The two halves of the architecture each became a family: BERT kept the encoder, GPT kept the decoder, and the decoder-only form is what we now call a large language model (Day 5). The paper also fixed the vocabulary we still use: queries, keys, values, heads, residual stream, positional encoding.

Limitations, and what came after

Self-attention costs O(n²·d) per layer in sequence length n, cheap for a 50-token sentence and painful for a 100,000-token document; the paper itself notes this and suggests restricting attention to a window (Section 4). That cost drove years of follow-ups, from sparse patterns to the exact, memory-efficient kernels of the FlashAttention paper. Sinusoidal positions gave way to learned positions in GPT and then to rotary positions (RoPE paper). The original is an encoder–decoder for translation; modern LLMs drop the encoder, move the normalisation in front of each sub-layer (GPT-2) and often replace the feed-forward block with a mixture of experts (Day 11). But the core block, attention plus feed-forward plus residuals, is unchanged after nine years.

How to read the actual paper

It is 11 pages plus appendix and unusually readable. Start with Figure 1 (the architecture diagram) and Section 3.2 with Figure 2, which defines scaled dot-product and multi-head attention; then Section 3.5 for positional encoding and Table 1 with Section 4 for the argument about why self-attention is cheaper. Read Table 2 for the headline results and Table 3 for the ablations (number of heads, d_k, dropout, learned versus sinusoidal positions). Skip the optimiser details in Section 5 and the parsing experiment in Section 6.3 the first time through. Keep the Annotated Transformer open in another tab: it walks the same sections with runnable PyTorch. Budget about 90 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. Why is QKᵀ divided by √d_k before the softmax?

Answer 2. Dot products of 64-dimensional vectors have variance about 64; without scaling, the softmax becomes nearly one-hot and its gradients vanish. The other options describe things the scaling does not do.

Q2. Table 1 says self-attention has a maximum path length of O(1). What does that mean?

Answer 3. In a recurrent network, information between positions 1 and 40 must pass through 39 steps; in self-attention one layer connects them directly. Path length is about signal flow, not training time or head count.

Q3. Which part of the model tells it the order of the words?

Answer 1. Attention is a weighted sum over a set, so it is blind to order; the sinusoidal positional encodings inject it. The feed-forward block acts on each position independently and normalisation only rescales.

References

  1. Attention Is All You Need — Vaswani, Shazeer, Parmar, Uszkoreit, Jones, Gomez, Kaiser, Polosukhin, NeurIPS, 2017. The paper itself; short, and the figures carry the argument.
  2. NeurIPS 2017 proceedings entry — NeurIPS, 2017. The published version and reviews.
  3. The Illustrated Transformer — Jay Alammar, blog, 2018. The clearest pictures of Q, K, V and multi-head attention; read before the paper.
  4. The Annotated Transformer — Harvard NLP (Rush et al.), 2018, updated 2022. The paper re-typed as runnable PyTorch, section by section.
  5. Attention in transformers, step-by-step — 3Blue1Brown, video, 2024. Visual intuition for what attention heads compute.
  6. Neural Machine Translation by Jointly Learning to Align and Translate — Bahdanau, Cho, Bengio, ICLR, 2015. Where attention came from, bolted onto a recurrent model.
  7. Transformer: A Novel Neural Network Architecture for Language Understanding — Google Research blog, 2017. The announcement, with the animated coreference example.
2018 · OpenAI

Improving Language Understanding by Generative Pre-Training

Showed that one Transformer pre-trained to predict the next word, then lightly fine-tuned, beats hand-built models across a dozen tasks.

OpenAI PDF · Radford, Narasimhan, Salimans, Sutskever · OpenAI technical report, 2018

In one sentence

Pre-train a 12-layer decoder-only Transformer on 7,000 books with next-token prediction, then fine-tune that same network for each task with only a tiny input re-formatting and one linear layer, and you get a new state of the art on 9 of 12 benchmarks.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Suppose two kids enter a school contest with twelve different events: finishing stories, answering questions about a passage, spotting which two sentences mean the same thing, and so on. The first kid trains for each event separately, with a coach who builds a special drill for every one. The second kid does something odd. For months she just reads: seven thousand novels, cover to cover, playing one game the whole time, "guess the next word before I turn the page". She gets scarily good at that game. Then, a few days before the contest, she practises each event for a single afternoon. She wins nine of the twelve. Why? Guessing the next word in a story forces you to understand who did what, how people feel, and what usually happens next. That is exactly what the twelve events test, so a little practice is enough to point her skill at each one. This paper is that second kid, and its trick, read everything first and specialise later, is the recipe every big AI model has followed since.

The problem they wanted to solve

Labelled data for language understanding is scarce and expensive; raw text is almost free. By early 2018 people knew unlabelled text could help, but not how to use it well. Word vectors captured single words and nothing about context. ELMo gave contextual features from a bidirectional LSTM, but each task still needed its own architecture stacked on top Peters 2018. ULMFiT fine-tuned a whole LSTM language model, but only for text classification Howard 2018. Two questions were open: which pre-training objective transfers best, and how to move the learned representation to tasks whose inputs are pairs or triples of sentences without redesigning the network each time Radford 2018.

The key idea

Two stages, one network. Stage one is generative pre-training: train a Transformer decoder to maximise the probability of each next token in long stretches of book text. Stage two is discriminative fine-tuning: keep every weight, add a single linear layer on top of the last token's hidden state, and train briefly on the labelled task. The piece that makes "one network" possible is the set of task-aware input transformations: instead of building a new head for sentence pairs or multiple-choice questions, the inputs are rewritten as one token sequence with special delimiter tokens, so the pre-trained model sees the same kind of input it saw during pre-training. A small auxiliary language-modelling loss during fine-tuning (weight λ = 0.5) helps on the larger datasets.

How it works, step by step

  1. Data: BooksCorpus, over 7,000 unpublished books, chosen because it contains long contiguous text rather than shuffled sentences (Section 4.1).
  2. Model: a 12-layer decoder-only Transformer with masked self-attention, 768-dimensional states, 12 heads, 3072-wide feed-forward layers, learned position embeddings, GELU activations, and a byte-pair-encoding vocabulary with 40,000 merges (Day 2). This is the size later called 117M parameters.
  3. Pre-training: 100 epochs over mini-batches of 64 sequences of 512 tokens, Adam with a peak learning rate of 2.5e-4 and a cosine schedule. The objective is the plain next-token likelihood from Day 1.
  4. Input transformations (Figure 1, right): entailment becomes premise $ hypothesis; similarity is run in both orders and the two outputs added; multiple choice runs the context with each candidate answer separately and takes a softmax over the scores.
  5. Fine-tuning: usually 3 epochs, learning rate 6.25e-5, batch size 32, loss L2(C) + 0.5·L1(C) where L1 is the language-model loss on the task text.
  6. Evaluate on 12 datasets in four groups: natural language inference (5), question answering and commonsense (2), semantic similarity (3), classification (2).

What the results showed

TaskPrevious bestFine-tuned Transformer LM
MultiNLI, matched (accuracy)80.682.1
QNLI (accuracy)82.388.1
Story Cloze (accuracy)77.686.5
RACE (accuracy)53.359.0
CoLA (Matthews correlation)35.045.4
GLUE overall score68.972.8

From Tables 2–4 Radford 2018. The model set a new state of the art on 9 of 12 datasets; one honest miss was RTE, a 2,490-example dataset, where it scored 56.0 against 61.7 for a multi-task biLSTM. The analysis section is the most interesting part. Transferring more pre-trained layers helped steadily (Figure 2, left). Without pre-training, the same architecture averaged 59.9 instead of 74.7 across the tasks; swapping the Transformer for an LSTM with the same recipe gave 69.1 (Table 5). And the model's zero-shot ability on tasks it was never fine-tuned for rose steadily over the course of pre-training (Figure 2, right), a hint the authors followed up one year later in GPT-2.

Why it mattered

This is the template: a decoder-only Transformer, trained to predict the next token, then adapted. GPT-2 scaled it tenfold, GPT-3 scaled it a thousandfold, and the "fine-tune afterwards" stage became supervised fine-tuning and RLHF (Day 8) and later parameter-efficient methods like LoRA (Day 10). It also settled a design question: the authors chose a decoder with a causal mask so that the same network could both pre-train on raw text and generate, and that choice is why today's models can write.

Limitations, and what came after

Reading left-to-right only is a real handicap for understanding tasks, and four months later BERT showed it directly: with a bidirectional encoder of the same size, GLUE went from 75.1 to 79.6, and a left-to-right-only ablation of BERT lost most of that gain (Table 5 of that paper) Devlin 2018. The training corpus was small by any later standard (about 800M words), the context was 512 tokens, and every task still needed labelled examples and a separate fine-tuned copy of the model. The next step was to ask whether the fine-tuning stage could be dropped entirely, which is the question GPT-2 and GPT-3 answer.

How to read the actual paper

Twelve pages, no appendix. Figure 1 is the whole method: the left half is the architecture, the right half shows how each task type is rewritten as one sequence; read it with Section 3.3. Section 4.1 lists the exact hyperparameters, useful if you want to reproduce it with nanoGPT. Tables 2, 3 and 4 hold the results. Spend most of your time on Section 5 (Analysis): Figure 2 and Table 5 are the evidence for why pre-training and the Transformer both matter. About 45 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. What was pre-trained in this paper?

Answer 1. It is a 12-layer Transformer decoder with a causal mask and a next-token objective. Masked encoders are BERT; LSTMs are ELMo and ULMFiT.

Q2. How did the authors handle tasks with two input sentences without changing the architecture?

Answer 4. The task-aware input transformations rewrite pairs and multiple-choice items as single sequences, so only a linear output layer is new.

Q3. In the ablation, what happened to the average score when the same Transformer was trained on the tasks without pre-training?

Answer 3. Table 5: 59.9 without pre-training. 69.1 is the LSTM with pre-training; 72.8 is the GLUE score, a different number.

References

  1. Improving Language Understanding by Generative Pre-Training — Radford, Narasimhan, Salimans, Sutskever, OpenAI, 2018. The paper; Figure 1 and Section 5 are the parts to study.
  2. openai/finetune-transformer-lm — OpenAI, GitHub, 2018. Original code and weights; the model file is small enough to read in an evening.
  3. Deep contextualized word representations (ELMo) — Peters et al., NAACL, 2018. The strongest competitor at the time; shows what "features, not fine-tuning" looked like.
  4. Universal Language Model Fine-tuning for Text Classification (ULMFiT) — Howard, Ruder, ACL, 2018. The LSTM version of the same idea, with careful fine-tuning tricks.
  5. Semi-supervised Sequence Learning — Dai, Le, NeurIPS, 2015. The earlier proof that language-model pre-training helps a downstream classifier.
  6. The Illustrated BERT, ELMo, and co. — Jay Alammar, blog, 2018. Places GPT between ELMo and BERT with diagrams of the transfer-learning idea.
2018 · Google AI Language

BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding

Made "pre-train once, fine-tune for everything" the default for language understanding by letting a Transformer encoder read in both directions.

arXiv:1810.04805 · Devlin, Chang, Lee, Toutanova · NAACL 2019

In one sentence

Train a Transformer encoder to fill in randomly masked words using context from both sides, plus a next-sentence task, and a single added output layer then fine-tunes it to the top of eleven NLP benchmarks.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Picture a worksheet where a teacher has covered about one word in seven with little stickers, and your job is to guess what is under each one. "The ___ barked at the mail carrier" is easy, because you can look both left and right: "barked" and "mail carrier" practically shout "dog". Now imagine a friend who has to guess while holding a card over everything to the right of the sticker. He sees only "The ___" and must guess from nothing. You win every time, because you use the whole sentence. BERT is a computer program that played this sticker game on all of English Wikipedia and a big pile of books, millions of times, until it became excellent at working out what a sentence means from both directions. After that, teaching it a new job, like deciding whether a review is happy or angry, or finding the answer to a question inside a paragraph, took about an hour of extra practice. It was not built to write stories, but for understanding text it set new records on eleven tests at once.

The problem they wanted to solve

By late 2018 pre-training clearly worked, but the two leading recipes were both one-directional. GPT read left to right, so the representation of a word could only use what came before it. ELMo trained a left-to-right and a right-to-left LSTM separately and glued the outputs together, which is shallow: no layer ever saw both sides at once Peters 2018. The reason is a trap in the training objective. If you let a standard next-word model see the whole sentence, every word can indirectly see itself through the other direction and prediction becomes trivial. For token-level tasks such as finding an answer span, and for sentence-pair tasks such as entailment, the authors argued that both-sided context is exactly what matters Devlin 2018.

The key idea

Change the objective so bidirectionality is safe. A masked language model (MLM) hides 15% of the input tokens and asks the network to recover them from everything else, the old "cloze" test. Because the hidden tokens are removed from the input, there is nothing to cheat with, so every layer can attend in both directions. A second task, next sentence prediction (NSP), feeds two segments and asks whether the second really followed the first, to teach sentence-pair relationships. The architecture is just the encoder half of the Transformer; the same network, with one new output layer, is fine-tuned for every task, using a [CLS] token whose final state represents the whole input and a [SEP] token between segments.

How it works, step by step

  1. Input (Figure 2): WordPiece tokens, plus a learned position embedding and a segment embedding (A or B), with [CLS] at the front and [SEP] between segments. Sequences are up to 512 tokens.
  2. Masking: pick 15% of tokens. Of those, 80% become [MASK], 10% become a random token, and 10% are left unchanged. The 10/10 split exists because [MASK] never appears at fine-tuning time, and the model should not learn to ignore unmasked positions (Section 3.1).
  3. NSP: half the time segment B is the real continuation, half the time a random one; the model reports IsNext or NotNext from the [CLS] state.
  4. Two sizes: BERT-base with 12 layers, hidden size 768, 12 heads (110M parameters, matched to GPT for comparison) and BERT-large with 24 layers, hidden 1024, 16 heads (340M).
  5. Pre-training data: BooksCorpus (800M words) plus English Wikipedia (2,500M words); batches of 256 sequences of 512 tokens for 1,000,000 steps; each run took 4 days on 4 (base) or 16 (large) Cloud TPUs.
  6. Fine-tuning: all parameters are updated for a few epochs with one task-specific layer on top; the paper says every reported result can be reproduced in at most 1 hour on a single Cloud TPU (Day 10 covers cheaper ways to do this today).

What the results showed

BenchmarkOpenAI GPT (previous)BERT-baseBERT-large
GLUE average (Table 1)75.179.682.1
MNLI, matched (accuracy)82.184.686.7
SQuAD v1.1 test F1 (Table 2)——93.2 (ensemble, vs. 91.2 human)
SQuAD v2.0 test F1 (Table 3)——83.1

From Tables 1–3 Devlin 2018. The abstract quotes a GLUE score of 80.5, which is the leaderboard's averaging; the table in the paper gives 82.1. Table 5 is the ablation that carries the argument: on BERT-base, dropping NSP cost half a point on MNLI, but switching to a left-to-right model with no NSP cost 2.3 points on MNLI and a full 10.7 F1 on SQuAD (88.5 → 77.8). Bidirectionality, not NSP, was the ingredient that mattered most.

Why it mattered

For about two years BERT and its descendants were the default for anything that reads text without writing it: search ranking, classification, entity extraction, question answering. It made "download a checkpoint and fine-tune for an hour" a normal workflow, and it standardised the encoder with [CLS] pooling that embedding models still use. That matters today more than people expect: the dense retriever in the RAG paper is a pair of BERT-base encoders, and the embedding models behind most retrieval systems in Day 12 are still encoders of this shape.

Limitations, and what came after

BERT cannot generate text, so it needs a separate fine-tuned copy for every task and cannot be prompted; GPT-3 removed that need for general tasks two years later. The [MASK] token creates a mismatch between pre-training and fine-tuning, and the fixed 512-token window is short. NSP turned out to be unnecessary: RoBERTa dropped it, trained longer on more data with bigger batches, and beat BERT with the same architecture Liu 2019. The encoder line kept improving quietly; ModernBERT in late 2024 rebuilt it with rotary positions, 8,192-token context and modern attention kernels Warner 2024. For comparison with the decoder family see Day 11.

How to read the actual paper

Sixteen pages with appendix. Read Figure 1 (pre-training versus fine-tuning) and Figure 2 (input representation) first, then Section 3.1 for the two objectives, including the 80/10/10 masking rule. Table 1 (GLUE) and Table 2 (SQuAD) are the headline numbers. The part that proves the thesis is Section 5.1 with Table 5; Section 5.2 shows bigger models help even on small datasets. Appendix C.2 has the masking-strategy ablation. Skip Section 2 (related work) and Appendix B. About 60 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. Why can you not train a deeply bidirectional model with ordinary next-word prediction?

Answer 2. That leak is why BERT masks tokens out of the input instead. Transformers can attend in any direction; speed and vocabulary are unrelated.

Q2. What fraction of input tokens does BERT select for masking?

Answer 3. 15% are selected; of those, 80% become [MASK], 10% a random token, 10% stay the same. 80% is the share replaced by [MASK], not the share selected.

Q3. In the Table 5 ablation, which change hurt SQuAD F1 the most?

Answer 4. Left-to-right without NSP dropped SQuAD F1 from 88.5 to 77.8; removing NSP alone cost only 0.6. Batch size and steps are not in that table.

References

  1. BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding — Devlin, Chang, Lee, Toutanova, NAACL, 2019. The paper; Table 5 is the part to argue about.
  2. ACL Anthology entry (NAACL 2019) — NAACL, 2019. The published version.
  3. Open Sourcing BERT — Google AI blog, 2018. The release note, with the one-hour fine-tuning claim.
  4. google-research/bert — Google, GitHub, 2018. Original TensorFlow code and checkpoints.
  5. The Illustrated BERT, ELMo, and co. — Jay Alammar, blog, 2018. Pictures of masking, [CLS] and the fine-tuning heads.
  6. BERT in Hugging Face Transformers — Hugging Face docs. How to load and fine-tune it in a few lines today.
  7. RoBERTa: A Robustly Optimized BERT Pretraining Approach — Liu et al., Meta AI, 2019. Shows that NSP was unnecessary and that data and training length mattered more.
  8. ModernBERT — Warner et al., 2024. What a BERT-style encoder looks like with six years of architecture improvements.
2019 · OpenAI

Language Models are Unsupervised Multitask Learners

Showed that a bigger language model trained on more web text starts doing tasks nobody trained it for, just from the prompt.

OpenAI PDF · Radford, Wu, Child, Luan, Amodei, Sutskever · OpenAI technical report, 2019

In one sentence

A 1.5-billion-parameter decoder-only Transformer trained on 40 GB of web pages that Reddit users had linked to sets records on 7 of 8 language-modelling benchmarks with no fine-tuning, and does rough summarisation, translation and question answering when the task is simply written into the prompt.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine you never go to a single class, but you spend a year reading eight million web pages that other people recommended, playing the "guess the next word" game the whole time. Nobody ever teaches you to summarise, to translate, or to answer trivia. Then one day a friend writes a long article, adds the letters "TL;DR:" at the bottom, and hands it to you. You have seen "TL;DR:" thousands of times online, always followed by a short summary, so you write one. Another friend writes "cat = chat, dog = chien, house =" and you continue with "maison", because that pattern shows up on language-learning pages. You are not great at these jobs, a trained specialist still beats you, but you can do them a little, and the more pages you have read, the better you get. That was the surprise in this paper: the skills were hiding inside the reading game all along. The researchers also worried about what such a writer could be used for, so they released the biggest version in stages rather than all at once.

The problem they wanted to solve

Machine-learning systems of the time were, in the authors' words, narrow experts: trained on one dataset for one task, and brittle when the data shifted. Even the pre-train-then-fine-tune recipe of GPT and BERT still needed a labelled dataset and a separate fine-tuned model for every task. The paper asks whether that last supervised step is necessary at all. The hypothesis is that demonstrations of many tasks already occur naturally in ordinary text (Table 1 of the paper shows translation examples found in the wild), so a language model trained on enough diverse text should learn to perform those tasks as a side effect of predicting the next token Radford 2019.

The key idea

Treat the task as part of the input. A supervised model learns p(output | input); a general system should learn p(output | input, task), and in natural language the task can be stated in the same text stream as the input. If the model is large enough and the data diverse enough, you can specify a task zero-shot (no examples, no gradient updates) and the model will "infer and perform" it. Two engineering decisions supported this. First, data quality: instead of a raw web crawl, they built WebText from outbound links on Reddit with at least 3 karma, a cheap proxy for "a human found this worth reading". Second, a byte-level BPE tokenizer (Day 2) that can encode any Unicode string with no unknown tokens while keeping frequent words whole.

How it works, step by step

  1. Build WebText: 45 million Reddit outbound links, de-duplicated and cleaned to slightly over 8 million documents, 40 GB of text; Wikipedia pages were removed because so many evaluation datasets are built from them (Section 2.1).
  2. Tokenize with byte-level BPE and a 50,257-entry vocabulary; the context grows from 512 to 1024 tokens.
  3. Keep the GPT architecture but move layer normalisation to the input of each sub-block, add a final normalisation after the last block, and scale residual-layer weights at initialisation by 1/√N for N residual layers (Section 2.3). This pre-norm block is what Day 5 teaches as the standard block.
  4. Train four sizes: 117M (12 layers, width 768), 345M (24, 1024), 762M (36, 1280) and 1542M (48, 1600). The smallest matches the original GPT and the second matches BERT-large (Table 2).
  5. Evaluate zero-shot: language-modelling benchmarks directly, and other tasks by prompting, for example appending TL;DR: for summaries, giving a few english = french pairs for translation, or a few question–answer pairs for trivia.
  6. Check memorisation: measure 8-gram overlap between WebText and each test set (Table 6), and note that even the largest model still underfits WebText (Figure 4).

What the results showed

Zero-shot benchmarkPrevious bestGPT-2 (1542M)
LAMBADA perplexity (lower is better)99.88.63
LAMBADA accuracy59.2363.24
Children's Book Test, common nouns (accuracy)85.793.3
Penn Treebank perplexity46.5435.76
WikiText-103 perplexity18.317.48
One Billion Word perplexity21.842.16
CoQA reading comprehension (F1)89 (supervised BERT)55
WMT-14 French→English (BLEU)33.5 (best unsupervised)11.5
Natural Questions (exact match)30–50% (retrieval systems)4.1%

From Table 3 and Section 3 Radford 2019. Read the table in two halves. On pure language modelling, 7 of 8 records fell; the one loss, One Billion Word, is a dataset of shuffled sentences where long context cannot help. On the "tasks nobody trained it for", the numbers are poor in absolute terms but not zero: 55 F1 on CoQA without seeing any of its 127,000 training pairs, 11.5 BLEU on translation when only about 10 MB of French existed in WebText, and a Winograd Schema score of 70.7%. Everything improved steadily with model size (Figure 1), which is the finding that made the next two papers inevitable.

Why it mattered

This is where "prompting" began: the idea that you specify the task in text and the model's size does the rest, which GPT-3 pushed to few-shot learning and scaling laws turned into equations. Two engineering choices became permanent: byte-level BPE with a roughly 50k vocabulary and the pre-norm block. The 124M model is now the standard teaching example; Karpathy's nanoGPT reproduces it from scratch, and Day 5 follows the same block diagram. The staged release also started the field's public argument about how to publish capable models Solaiman 2019.

Limitations, and what came after

Zero-shot GPT-2 was far from usable: its summaries barely beat picking three random sentences from the article, and it answered 4.1% of Natural Questions. The memorisation analysis found real overlap between WebText and some test sets (13.2% of One Billion Word test 8-grams appear in the training set), a problem that returned in GPT-3. The context window was 1,024 tokens. And the model was still underfitting its own data, so more compute was clearly available to spend. The answer to "what if we keep going" came in 2020 with a model a hundred times larger and the first scaling laws; the answer to "how do we make it follow instructions" waited until InstructGPT.

How to read the actual paper

Nine pages of body plus many pages of samples. Section 2 is the method: 2.1 for WebText, 2.2 for byte-level BPE, 2.3 for the model changes. Table 2 gives the sizes and Table 3 the headline numbers; Figure 1 is the "everything grows with size" plot. Sections 3.5–3.8 (reading comprehension, summarisation, translation, question answering) are short and fun, and Table 5 lists the trivia questions it got right. Section 4 with Table 6 and Figure 4 is the honest part about memorisation and underfitting. Skip the appendix except Table 13, the unicorn story. About 60 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. How did GPT-2 produce a summary of a news article with no training for summarisation?

Answer 3. The task was specified in the prompt. Removing the hint cost 6.4 ROUGE points, which shows the hint was doing the work; there was no fine-tuning or extra head.

Q2. Why use a byte-level BPE tokenizer?

Answer 2. Bytes guarantee coverage of any input; BPE merges keep frequent words compact. It has nothing to do with GPU speed or the context length.

Q3. On which dataset did GPT-2 fail to beat the previous state of the art?

Answer 4. 42.16 versus 21.8 perplexity; the benchmark shuffles sentences, so a long-context model loses its advantage. The other three were records.

  2. Better language models and their implications — OpenAI blog, 2019. The announcement, with samples and the staged-release reasoning.
  3. GPT-2: 1.5B release — OpenAI blog, 2019. The final release note, with what they learned about misuse.
  4. openai/gpt-2 — OpenAI, GitHub, 2019. Model code and weights for all four sizes.
  5. karpathy/nanoGPT — Andrej Karpathy, GitHub, 2022. A few hundred lines that reproduce GPT-2 124M; the best way to understand the block.
  6. Let's reproduce GPT-2 (124M) — Andrej Karpathy, video, 2024. Four hours of building and training it live, including the 1/√N initialisation detail.
  7. The Illustrated GPT-2 — Jay Alammar, blog, 2019. Diagrams of the decoder-only block and masked attention.
  8. Release Strategies and the Social Impacts of Language Models — Solaiman et al., OpenAI, 2019. The report behind the staged release.
2020 · OpenAI

Language Models are Few-Shot Learners

Proved that at 175 billion parameters a plain language model can learn a new task from a handful of examples written into its prompt.

arXiv:2005.14165 · Brown, Mann, Ryder, Subbiah, Kaplan, Dhariwal, Neelakantan, Shyam, Sastry, Askell and 21 others · NeurIPS 2020

In one sentence

Scale the GPT-2 recipe a hundredfold (175B parameters, 300B training tokens, about 3.1×10²³ FLOPs) and the model becomes a few-shot learner: show it a few input→output pairs in the prompt and it does the task with no change to its weights.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Think of a new kid who has read more than any librarian alive. You want her to play a game she has never seen, say, turning words into their opposites. You do not send her to a class. You just write three examples on a card, "hot → cold, big → small, fast → slow", then write "happy →" and hand it over. She writes "sad". Nothing inside her head changed; she looked at the pattern on the card and continued it. The paper's big discovery is that this card trick barely works for a small reader, works a little for a medium one, and works surprisingly well for a huge one. The researchers built a reader a hundred times bigger than their last one and tested the card trick on dozens of games: trivia, arithmetic, unscrambling letters, translating, reading comprehension. The huge reader got many of them right with just a few examples. It also wrote short news stories that people could tell apart from human-written ones only about half the time, which is the same as guessing. That is why everyone started talking about "prompting".

The problem they wanted to solve

Fine-tuning needs thousands of labelled examples per task, tends to exploit quirks of the training set, and is nothing like how a person picks up a task from a short description. GPT-2 had shown that prompting a 1.5B model does something, but its zero-shot numbers were weak. The open question was whether the trend in GPT-2's Figure 1 would continue: if the model got much bigger, would task-agnostic, in-context performance keep improving, and by how much Brown 2020?

The key idea

In-context learning: the model is given a natural-language description of the task and K worked examples inside its context window, and must produce the answer for a new input, with no gradient updates. The paper names three settings, defined in Figure 2.1: zero-shot (description only), one-shot (one example) and few-shot (typically 10 to 100 examples, as many as fit in the 2,048-token window). The scientific move is to measure all three across eight model sizes, from 125M to 175B parameters, so that the effect of scale on in-context learning is visible as a curve rather than a single point.

How it works, step by step

  1. Architecture: the same decoder-only Transformer as GPT-2, at eight sizes (Table 2.1). The largest has 96 layers, width 12,288 and 96 heads; the 1.3B "XL" and 6.7B models reappear later as the base models of InstructGPT.
  2. Data (Table 2.2): 410B tokens of filtered Common Crawl (60% of the mix), 19B of WebText2 (22%), two book corpora (8% each) and Wikipedia (3%). Higher-quality sources are sampled more than once per run; Common Crawl is seen less than once.
  3. Training: every size sees 300 billion tokens. The 175B run cost 3.64×10³ petaflop/s-days, about 3.14×10²³ FLOPs (Table D.1), which Day 6 turns into GPU-hours.
  4. Evaluation: for each test item, K examples are drawn from the task's training set and placed in the prompt, followed by the test input; the model's continuation is scored. No weights change.
  5. Contamination check (Section 4): because a bug let some benchmark text into the training data, they re-score every benchmark on a "clean" subset with 13-gram overlap removed and report both.

What the results showed

Task (GPT-3 175B)Zero-shotOne-shotFew-shot
LAMBADA (accuracy)76.272.586.4
TriviaQA (accuracy)64.368.071.2
SuperGLUE test (average, K=32)——69.0
Two-digit addition (accuracy)——100.0
Human accuracy at spotting its news articles52% (95% CI 49–54%), chance is 50%

From Tables 3.2, 3.3, 3.8, 3.9 and 3.11 Brown 2020. Two patterns matter more than any single row. Few-shot beats zero-shot by a wide margin, and the gap widens with model size (Figure 1.2): big models get more out of each in-context example. And the training loss follows a smooth power law in compute over two more orders of magnitude than earlier work had covered (Figure 3.1), which is the empirical bridge to the scaling-laws paper whose first author is on this one.

Why it mattered

GPT-3 made "write a prompt" the interface. It was the first model served through a paid API rather than released as weights OpenAI 2020, and the API's prompts became the training data for InstructGPT. It started the debate about emergent abilities (capabilities that appear only above some model size) Wei 2022, and the counter-argument that many such jumps are artefacts of all-or-nothing metrics Schaeffer 2023. Day 13 picks up that thread.

Limitations, and what came after

Section 5 of the paper is unusually frank. Long generations repeat and lose coherence; few-shot scores on WiC and ANLI are near chance; the model is left-to-right only and weights every token equally; it has seen far more text than any human and is still less sample-efficient; it is expensive to run; and it inherits the biases of the web. The authors also admit they cannot tell whether few-shot learning learns a task at inference time or merely recognises one seen in training. Later work sharpened that doubt: swapping the labels in the demonstrations for random ones barely hurt GPT-3, suggesting the examples mostly convey format and the label space Min 2022. Two years on, Chinchilla showed that 175B parameters on 300B tokens was badly under-trained for the compute spent, and InstructGPT showed that a 1.3B model fine-tuned on human feedback was preferred to raw GPT-3. Scale was necessary; it was not sufficient.

How to read the actual paper

It is 75 pages; do not read it linearly. Read Section 1 with Figures 1.1–1.3 for the thesis, then Section 2: Figure 2.1 defines the three settings, Table 2.1 the sizes, Table 2.2 the data mix. From Section 3 pick 3.1 (LAMBADA), 3.2 (closed-book QA), 3.7 (SuperGLUE) and 3.9.1 (arithmetic, Figure 3.10). Section 4 is the contamination study and Section 5 the limitations; both are short and worth it. Appendix D has the compute table. Skip the per-task appendices. Two hours.

Check yourself

Q1. What changes inside GPT-3 when it "learns" a task from few-shot examples?

Answer 2. In-context learning uses no gradient updates at all; the examples are part of the input. Adapters and partial updates are fine-tuning methods from Day 10.

Q2. How many tokens was GPT-3 175B trained on?

Answer 3. All eight sizes saw 300B tokens. 40 GB is GPT-2's WebText; 1.4T is Chinchilla; 3B is just the Wikipedia slice of the mix.

Q3. How accurately could people identify news articles written by the 175B model?

Answer 1. Table 3.11 reports 52% with a confidence interval that includes 50%. Smaller models were easier to spot.

References

  1. Language Models are Few-Shot Learners — Brown et al., OpenAI, NeurIPS, 2020. The paper; read the sections listed above, not all 75 pages.
  2. NeurIPS 2020 proceedings entry — NeurIPS, 2020. The published version.
  3. OpenAI API — OpenAI blog, 2020. The announcement that GPT-3 would be served, not released.
  4. Scaling Laws for Neural Language Models — Kaplan et al., OpenAI, 2020. The companion paper that predicted the loss curve GPT-3 landed on.
  5. Emergent Abilities of Large Language Models — Wei et al., Google and others, 2022. The case that some abilities appear suddenly with scale.
  6. Are Emergent Abilities of Large Language Models a Mirage? — Schaeffer, Miranda, Koyejo, Stanford, 2023. The case that the jumps are mostly the metric's fault.
  7. Rethinking the Role of Demonstrations — Min et al., EMNLP, 2022. What few-shot examples actually teach the model.
  8. Stanford CS324: Large Language Models — Stanford, course site, 2022. Lecture notes written in the GPT-3 era that cover capabilities, harms and data.
2020 / 2022 · OpenAI · DeepMind

Scaling Laws and Chinchilla

Two papers that turned "bigger is better" into equations, then corrected the equation: for a fixed compute budget, grow the data as fast as you grow the model.

arXiv:2001.08361 · Kaplan, McCandlish, Henighan, Brown, Chess, Child, Gray, Radford, Wu, Amodei · 2020  |  arXiv:2203.15556 · Hoffmann, Borgeaud, Mensch, Buchatskaya, Cai, Rutherford, de Las Casas, Hendricks, Welbl, Clark and 12 others · NeurIPS 2022

In one sentence

Test loss falls as a smooth power law in parameters N, training tokens D and compute C; Kaplan et al. read their fits as "spend extra compute mostly on bigger models", and Hoffmann et al. re-measured with learning-rate schedules matched to each run and found N and D should grow in equal proportion, roughly 20 tokens per parameter.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Suppose you are baking a giant cake for a contest and you have exactly one hundred dollars. You can spend it on a bigger oven (a bigger brain) or on more ingredients (more reading). The first team measured lots of small cakes and found a neat rule: every time you double the money, the cake gets better by a steady, predictable amount, no surprises. They also concluded you should put most of the money into the oven. Everyone followed that advice and built enormous ovens with modest piles of ingredients. Two years later, a second team re-ran the experiment more carefully, baking four hundred practice cakes. Their rule said: split the money evenly. A medium oven with four times the ingredients beats a huge oven that is half empty. To prove it, they baked "Chinchilla", a cake one quarter the size of their earlier giant, using the same hundred dollars but far more flour, and it won almost every taste test. Since then the best models have been fed far more text than their size alone would suggest, and the "steady improvement" rule is how labs predict what the next, bigger model will do before they build it.

The problem they wanted to solve

Before 2020 nobody could say how much better a language model would get if you made it ten times larger, or whether the money was better spent on data. GPT-2 had shown a clean improvement across four sizes, and earlier work had found power-law trends in other domains Hestness 2017, but there was no quantitative rule for language models and no way to plan a training budget. Kaplan et al. set out to measure the dependence of cross-entropy loss on model size, dataset size and compute across many orders of magnitude Kaplan 2020. Hoffmann et al. revisited one specific conclusion two years later, after a generation of 100B-plus models had been trained on roughly the same 300B tokens as GPT-3 Hoffmann 2022.

The key idea

Kaplan: train many small models and fit power laws, curves of the form L = (N_c/N)^α that appear as straight lines on log-log axes. Three fits summarise the paper: L(N) with α_N ≈ 0.076, L(D) with α_D ≈ 0.095, and L(C_min) with α_C ≈ 0.050. Shape barely matters (depth versus width at fixed parameter count changes loss very little), larger models are more sample-efficient, and the compute-optimal strategy is to train a very large model and stop well before convergence. The allocation rule they derived was N_opt ∝ C^0.73 and D_opt ∝ C^0.27: most new compute into size, little into data.

Hoffmann: the Kaplan runs used a fixed number of training tokens and one learning-rate schedule for every model, which overstates the loss of short runs and therefore undervalues data (Section 2 of their paper). With the schedule length matched to each run's token count, three independent methods, the envelope of training curves, IsoFLOP profiles (fixing compute and sweeping model size) and a parametric fit L(N,D) = E + A/N^α + B/D^β, all give exponents near 0.5 for both N and D. The fitted constants are E = 1.69, A = 406.4, B = 410.7, α = 0.34, β = 0.28.

How it works, step by step

  1. Count compute as C ≈ 6·N·D FLOPs: about 2 FLOPs per parameter per token for the forward pass and 4 for the backward pass (Kaplan, Section 2.1; Day 6 derives it).
  2. Train a grid of models. Kaplan: non-embedding sizes from 768 to 1.5B parameters on WebText2 with a 1,024-token context. Hoffmann: over 400 models from 70M to over 16B parameters, trained on 5B to over 400B tokens.
  3. Fit loss as a function of N, D and C on log-log axes.
  4. Solve for the best split of a budget: Kaplan's Figure 3 sends most of a billion-fold compute increase into model size; Hoffmann's Figure 1 overlays the three new fits against Kaplan's line and they disagree by a large factor at GPT-3 scale.
  5. Validate the new rule by training one model at the predicted size: Chinchilla, 70B parameters on 1.4T tokens, with the same compute as the 280B Gopher trained on 300B tokens.

Try it: in the calculator below, set 280B parameters and 300B tokens, note the FLOPs, then set 70B and 1.4T. The compute is about the same; the token-per-parameter ratio goes from about 1 to 20.

The log-log loss curves are straight lines: that straightness is the whole reason "scaling laws" is a useful phrase. The 20-tokens-per-parameter guide is Chinchilla's rule of thumb, not a law of nature.

What the results showed

Estimate of how N and D should grow with computeN exponent aD exponent b
Kaplan et al. 20200.730.27
Hoffmann approach 1: training-curve envelope0.500.50
Hoffmann approach 2: IsoFLOP profiles0.490.51
Hoffmann approach 3: parametric fit0.460.54
ModelParametersTraining tokensMMLU (5-shot)BIG-bench average
Gopher280B300B60.0%54.4%
Chinchilla70B1.4T67.5%65.1%

From Table 2, Table 1 and Section 4.2 of Hoffmann et al. and Section 6 of Kaplan et al. Hoffmann 2022 Kaplan 2020. Chinchilla beat Gopher on 51 of 57 MMLU subjects and trailed it on only 4 of 62 BIG-bench tasks, while being a quarter of the size, which also makes it four times cheaper to run (Day 7). Their Table 3 is the planning table people actually use: for a 175B model the compute-optimal token count is about 3.7T, twelve times what GPT-3 saw.

Why it mattered

Scaling laws turned model training from an art into a budgeting exercise: pick a compute budget, read off N and D, predict the loss before spending the money. Chinchilla changed what labs built. LLaMA trained a 7B model on 1T tokens and its 65B model on 1.4T, and chose rotary positions and other details to make small models strong Touvron 2023. Once inference cost is counted, the optimum moves even further toward small models trained on far more data than Chinchilla suggests Sardana 2023, which is why open models from 2023 onward are trained for many times the 20-token rule (Llama 3 review).

Limitations, and what came after

Loss is not capability: both papers fit cross-entropy on their own data, and a lower loss does not guarantee any specific downstream skill (Day 13). The constants depend on the dataset, the tokenizer and the architecture, so they transfer as a shape, not as numbers. A 2024 replication found that Hoffmann's third approach reported confidence intervals too narrow to be real and could not be reproduced from the extracted data, though the first two approaches, and the overall conclusion, held up Besiroglu 2024. Neither paper counts inference, and neither considers running out of text, which is now the binding constraint for the largest runs. The Hoffmann paper itself lists the Gopher budget as 5.76×10²³ FLOPs in the text and 6.3×10²³ in its appendix, a reminder that FLOP accounting is approximate.

How to read the actual paper

Kaplan: Section 1.2, "Summary of findings", is a one-page version of the paper with every key equation; then Figure 1, Figure 2 (sample efficiency) and Section 6 with Figure 3 (allocation). Skip Sections 4 and 5 unless you want the fitting details. Hoffmann: Figure 1 first, then Section 3 (the three approaches, Figures 2–4), Table 2 (exponents), Table 3 (the budget table), and Section 4.2 for Chinchilla's results. Related Work, Section 2, has the explanation of why the two papers disagree. About 90 minutes for both.

Check yourself

Q1. What is the rule of thumb from the Chinchilla paper?

Answer 1. Chinchilla's 70B on 1.4T tokens is 20 to 1; Gopher's 280B on 300B was about 1 to 1. Later models deliberately exceed 20 to 1 because inference is cheaper for small models.

Q2. Roughly how many FLOPs does it take to train a model with N parameters on D tokens?

Answer 3. Two FLOPs per parameter per token forward, four backward. 2·N·D is the forward pass alone, which is what inference costs.

Q3. Which claim from Kaplan et al. did Chinchilla overturn?

Answer 4. The power-law shape, the weak effect of shape and sample efficiency all survived; the allocation exponent changed from 0.73 to about 0.5.

References

  1. Scaling Laws for Neural Language Models — Kaplan et al., OpenAI, 2020. Section 1.2 alone is worth the download.
  2. Training Compute-Optimal Large Language Models — Hoffmann et al., DeepMind, NeurIPS, 2022. The Chinchilla paper; Table 3 is the planning table.
  3. An empirical analysis of compute-optimal large language model training — Google DeepMind blog, 2022. Short official summary with the key figure.
  4. NeurIPS 2022 proceedings entry — NeurIPS, 2022. The published version of the Chinchilla paper, under its conference title.
  5. Deep Learning Scaling is Predictable, Empirically — Hestness et al., Baidu, 2017. Power-law scaling before it was called that.
  6. Chinchilla Scaling: A replication attempt — Besiroglu, Erdil, Barnett, You, Epoch AI, 2024. What held up and what did not when someone re-fit the data.
  7. Beyond Chinchilla-Optimal: Accounting for Inference in Language Model Scaling Laws — Sardana, Portes, Doubov, Frankle, MosaicML, 2023. Why production models are trained far past 20 tokens per parameter.
  8. LLaMA: Open and Efficient Foundation Language Models — Touvron et al., Meta AI, 2023. The first widely used models designed around the Chinchilla result.
2021 · Zhuiyi Technology

RoFormer: Enhanced Transformer with Rotary Position Embedding

Encoded position by rotating the query and key vectors, giving attention a built-in sense of relative distance that nearly every modern open model now uses.

arXiv:2104.09864 · Su, Lu, Pan, Murtadha, Wen, Liu · arXiv preprint 2021, revised 2023

In one sentence

Rotate each query and key vector by an angle proportional to its position, so the dot product between a query at position m and a key at position n depends only on n−m; it adds no parameters, costs almost nothing, works at any sequence length, and makes attention fade with distance.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a row of clocks, one for every word in a sentence. For the first word the hand points straight up; for the second it is turned a little; for the third a little more, and so on down the line. How much the hand turns depends only on the word's place in the line, not on the word itself. Now, if you want to know how far apart two words are, you do not need to know their actual places; you just compare the angle between their two clock hands. Word 3 and word 7 are four ticks apart, and so are word 50 and word 54: the angle between them is identical. This paper gives each word a whole bundle of clocks, some turning fast and some very slowly, so the model can tell both tiny gaps and huge ones apart. The nice part is that the same trick works for a sentence of a hundred words or a thousand, because you never run out of angles. That is why most of today's big open models use this spinning-clock idea to keep track of word order.

The problem they wanted to solve

Attention is a weighted sum over a set, so a Transformer cannot tell "dog bites man" from "man bites dog" unless position is injected somehow. The original paper added fixed sinusoids to the input embeddings; GPT and BERT replaced them with a learned vector per position. Both are absolute: they say "this token is at slot 17", and the attention score between two tokens only learns about their distance indirectly. Learned absolute positions also stop at the training length; slot 1,025 of a model trained on 1,024 has no embedding at all. Relative schemes fix this by adding a learned bias for each offset into the attention logits Shaw 2018, but they complicate the attention computation and do not fit the linear-attention variants the authors were interested in Su 2021.

The key idea

Encode absolute position as a rotation and get relative position for free. Treat the d-dimensional query as d/2 pairs of numbers, each pair a point on a plane. At position m, rotate pair i by the angle m·θ_i, with θ_i = 10000^(−2i/d), so the first pairs spin fast and the last ones spin slowly. Do the same to keys. A rotation by m followed by the transpose of a rotation by n is a rotation by n−m, so the score q_mᵀk_n = x_mᵀ W_qᵀ R_{n−m} W_k x_n depends on the two tokens and on their distance, but not on where in the sequence they sit (Section 3.2). Nothing is learned, nothing is added to the values or the residual stream, and because 10000 is the base of the slowest frequency the usable range of distances is very long. The authors also show the expected attention weight between two positions decays as they move apart (Section 3.4.3, Figure 2), a bias toward nearby context that language seems to want.

How it works, step by step

  1. In every attention layer, compute queries and keys as usual: q = W_q x, k = W_k x.
  2. Split q into pairs (q₁,q₂), (q₃,q₄), … For the token at position m, rotate pair i by angle m·θ_i.
  3. Implement the rotation without any matrix multiply (Equation 34): q_rot = q ⊗ cos(mθ) + rotate_half(q) ⊗ sin(mθ), where rotate_half swaps each pair and negates one element, and ⊗ is element-wise. Cache the cos and sin tables once.
  4. Rotate keys the same way with their own positions; leave values alone.
  5. Take dot products and softmax exactly as before. The relative offset is now inside the score.
  6. For linear attention, apply the same rotation to the feature-mapped queries and keys (Section 3.3), which is how the Performer experiment works.

Try it: the widget below shows the classic sinusoidal pattern as a heatmap and, beside it, a RoPE rotation. Drag the position slider and watch each pair of dimensions turn at its own speed.

Fast-spinning pairs resolve small distances; slow pairs resolve long ones. Compare with Day 5, which shows where this sits inside the block.

What the results showed

ExperimentBaselineWith RoPE (RoFormer)
WMT14 English→German (BLEU)27.3 (Transformer base)27.5
GLUE MRPC (F1)88.9 (BERT)89.5
GLUE STS-B (Spearman)85.887.0
GLUE QQP (F1)71.286.4
GLUE SST-2 (accuracy)93.590.7
CAIL2019-SCM long Chinese legal text, test accuracy68.10 (WoBERT, 512 tokens)68.29 at 512 tokens, 69.79 at 1024

From Tables 1, 3 and 5 Su 2021. The honest summary is "mixed but promising": RoFormer wins on three of six GLUE tasks and loses on three, and the translation gain is 0.2 BLEU. The two results that aged well are the pre-training curves, where a BERT-style model with RoPE reaches a lower masked-LM loss faster than the same model with learned positions (Figure 3), and the long-document task, where RoPE kept improving when the input was lengthened from 512 to 1,024 tokens, something an absolute embedding cannot do at all.

Why it mattered

The paper's experiments did not make RoPE famous; adoption did. The open-source GPT-NeoX-20B used it in 2022 Black 2022, Google's 540B PaLM used it in the same year Chowdhery 2022, and Meta's LLaMA removed absolute positions entirely in favour of RoPE Touvron 2023, after which it became the default in the open-weights world covered in Day 11. Its biggest practical pay-off came later: because position is an angle, you can stretch the angles. Position Interpolation scales positions down so a model trained on 2,048 tokens reads 32,768 with a little fine-tuning Chen 2023, and YaRN refines the idea per frequency band Peng 2023. The 128K-token windows of models like Llama 3 rest on those tricks Meta 2024.

Limitations, and what came after

RoPE does not extrapolate on its own: feed a model positions beyond anything it saw in training and quality collapses, which is why the interpolation methods above exist. ALiBi, published a few months later, instead adds a linear distance penalty to the scores and extrapolates somewhat better out of the box Press 2021, though RoPE won on adoption. The decay argument in Section 3.4.3 is a bound and a plot, not a proof. The base frequency 10000 is a knob: long-context models raise it to slow the fastest clocks. And the paper's benchmark evidence is thin by today's standards; the EleutherAI write-up that reproduced the gains at larger scale did more to convince people than the original tables Biderman 2021.

How to read the actual paper

Read Section 3.1 (what a position encoding must satisfy), 3.2 with Figure 1 (the 2-D case, then the general block-diagonal form), 3.3 (properties, including the linear-attention extension) and 3.4.2 (Equation 34, the version you would implement). Section 3.4.3 and Figure 2 give the decay argument. Section 4 is quick: Tables 1, 3 and 5 plus Figure 3. Skip Section 2.3's survey of relative schemes on a first pass. The EleutherAI blog post is the better implementation guide. About 45 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. RoPE is applied to which vectors?

Answer 1. Only q and k are rotated, because only their dot product needs to know about distance. Values and the residual stream are untouched, unlike additive absolute embeddings.

Q2. After RoPE, how does the attention score between positions m and n depend on position?

Answer 3. Rotation by m then by −n composes to rotation by n−m, so the score sees the relative offset and nothing else about position.

Q3. Why does RoPE use many rotation frequencies instead of one?

Answer 2. A single fast clock repeats every few tokens; a single slow one cannot separate neighbours. The geometric series of frequencies covers both, exactly like the sinusoidal encoding it replaces.

References

  1. RoFormer: Enhanced Transformer with Rotary Position Embedding — Su, Lu, Pan, Murtadha, Wen, Liu, Zhuiyi Technology, 2021. The paper; Sections 3.2–3.4 are the core.
  2. Rotary Embeddings: A Relative Revolution — Biderman et al., EleutherAI blog, 2021. The explanation and code most implementations copied, plus larger-scale experiments.
  3. Self-Attention with Relative Position Representations — Shaw, Uszkoreit, Vaswani, NAACL, 2018. The additive relative-position scheme RoPE competes with.
  4. Train Short, Test Long: Attention with Linear Biases (ALiBi) — Press, Smith, Lewis, ICLR, 2022. The main alternative, with better out-of-the-box length extrapolation.
  5. LLaMA: Open and Efficient Foundation Language Models — Touvron et al., Meta AI, 2023. Where RoPE became the open-weights default.
  6. Extending Context Window of Large Language Models via Positional Interpolation — Chen, Wong, Chen, Tian, Meta, 2023. How to stretch RoPE to 16 times the training length.
  7. YaRN: Efficient Context Window Extension of Large Language Models — Peng, Quesnelle, Fan, Shippole, 2023. The per-frequency refinement used by many long-context models.
  8. GPT-NeoX-20B: An Open-Source Autoregressive Language Model — Black et al., EleutherAI, 2022. An early large model built on RoPE, with ablations.
2022 · OpenAI

Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback

Turned a raw text predictor into an assistant by fine-tuning on human demonstrations and then on human preferences; the recipe behind ChatGPT.

arXiv:2203.02155 · Ouyang, Wu, Jiang, Almeida, Wainwright, Mishkin, Zhang, Agarwal, Slama, Ray and 10 others · NeurIPS 2022

In one sentence

Fine-tune GPT-3 on about 13k human-written demonstrations, train a 6B reward model on about 33k human rankings of model outputs, then optimise the policy with PPO against that reward plus a KL penalty, and labelers prefer the 1.3B result to the raw 175B GPT-3.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

A huge reading machine can finish any sentence, but ask it "Explain the moon landing to a six-year-old" and it might just list more questions, or copy the style of a web forum. It knows words, not what you meant. The researchers fixed this in three steps, the way you would train a helpful new assistant. First, they showed it a few thousand examples of good answers written by people: here is the question, here is what a helpful reply looks like. Second, they let it write several answers to the same question and had people rank them from best to worst, then taught a second program, a judge, to predict those rankings. Third, the assistant practised on tens of thousands of new questions while the judge scored every attempt, and the assistant nudged itself toward answers the judge liked, with one rule keeping it from drifting too far from how it used to talk. The result: a small assistant trained this way was preferred by people over a version a hundred times larger that skipped the lessons. The same three steps, with more data, produced ChatGPT a few months later.

The problem they wanted to solve

Predicting the next token of internet text is not the same objective as "do what the user asked, truthfully, without causing harm". GPT-3 would often ignore an instruction, continue the prompt instead of answering it, invent facts, or produce toxic text, and getting good behaviour out of it required careful prompt engineering. The paper calls this a misaligned objective and asks how to fine-tune a model so that it follows a broad, realistic range of user instructions, using human judgement as the training signal Ouyang 2022.

The key idea

Take reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), a method first used to teach agents from pairwise preferences Christiano 2017 and then applied to summarisation Stiennon 2020, and apply it to the whole distribution of prompts people actually sent to the GPT-3 API. Three stages: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on demonstrations, a reward model (RM) trained to predict which of two outputs a labeler prefers, and reinforcement learning with PPO that pushes the policy toward high reward while a KL penalty keeps it close to the SFT model. A variant, PPO-ptx, mixes in gradients from the original pre-training data to limit the loss of general ability, which the authors call the alignment tax.

How it works, step by step

  1. Collect prompts from API users and from about 40 contracted labelers, screened for agreement on sensitive cases (Section 3.2 and 3.4).
  2. SFT: labelers write ideal responses for about 13k prompts; GPT-3 at 1.3B, 6B and 175B is fine-tuned on them (Day 8).
  3. Reward model: for about 33k prompts, sample several outputs, have a labeler rank them, and train a 6B model to give a higher score to the preferred output in every pair. A 6B RM was used for all policy sizes because 175B RM training was unstable.
  4. RL: on about 31k new prompts, generate with the policy, score with the RM, and update with PPO. The objective is reward − β·KL(policy ‖ SFT model) + γ·pre-training loss; γ = 0 gives plain PPO.
  5. Evaluate with human preference against the 175B SFT baseline, using both the labelers who made the training data and held-out labelers (Figure 3), plus TruthfulQA, RealToxicityPrompts and public NLP benchmarks.

Try it: step through the pipeline below from pre-training to SFT to the reward model to RL, and watch where the KL penalty enters.

The reward model never sees absolute scores, only "A is better than B". That is what makes the labelling cheap and consistent enough to work.

What the results showed

MeasurementResult
175B InstructGPT vs 175B GPT-3, human preferencepreferred 85 ± 3% of the time
175B InstructGPT vs few-shot-prompted 175B GPT-3preferred 71 ± 4%
1.3B InstructGPT vs 175B GPT-3preferred, despite 100× fewer parameters
Hallucination rate on closed-domain tasks41% → 21%
RealToxicityPrompts, when asked to be respectfulabout 25% fewer toxic outputs
TruthfulQA, truthful and informative answersabout twice as often as GPT-3
Compute for 175B PPO-ptx training60 petaflop/s-days, vs 3,640 for GPT-3 pre-training

From Sections 4.1–4.2 and Appendix C Ouyang 2022. The compute line is the one to remember: the alignment step cost under 2% of pre-training and changed the model more, as users experience it, than a hundredfold increase in size. Held-out labelers agreed with the training labelers' preferences 77.3 ± 1.3% of the time, so the gains were not just the model learning the taste of its own annotators. The alignment tax was real: plain PPO regressed on SQuAD, DROP, HellaSwag and translation, and PPO-ptx recovered most but not all of it.

Why it mattered

This is the paper that made language models usable by people who do not write prompts for a living. The three-stage pipeline became the standard post-training recipe and the subject of Day 8; ChatGPT, released that November, was described by OpenAI as a sibling model to InstructGPT trained with the same method OpenAI 2022. It also introduced the questions that alignment work still argues about: who the labelers are, whose preferences the model should learn (Section 5.2, "Who are we aligning to?"), and what a model should do when an instruction is harmful.

Limitations, and what came after

The model is aligned to about 40 mostly English-speaking contractors following OpenAI's written guidelines, and most comparisons were labelled by a single person. InstructGPT still makes simple mistakes, still hallucinates, and follows instructions even when the instruction is harmful (Section 5.3). The reward model is a proxy that can be over-optimised, which is why the KL term exists. PPO itself is heavy: it needs four models in memory and is fiddly to tune, which pushed the field toward Direct Preference Optimization, which skips the reward model and the RL loop and fits the policy directly to the preference pairs (DPO paper). Constitutional AI replaced most human harmlessness labels with model-generated critiques guided by written principles (Constitutional AI paper). And the reasoning models of Day 9 use RL again, but with rewards that are checked automatically rather than learned from rankings.

How to read the actual paper

Figure 2 is the paper: three boxes, SFT, reward model, PPO. Read it with Section 3.5, which gives the loss for the reward model and the PPO-ptx objective with its β and γ terms. Figure 1 is the headline preference result and Figure 3 shows held-out labelers. Section 3.4 (who the labelers are) and Section 5.2 (who we are aligning to) are short and important. Section 4.2 and its figures cover truthfulness, toxicity and the alignment tax. Appendix B has the actual labeler instructions, which are more interesting than they sound. About 75 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. What is the KL penalty in the RL objective for?

Answer 4. The reward model is a learned proxy; without the penalty the policy finds outputs that score high and read badly. It does not speed training or save memory.

Q2. What data does the reward model learn from?

Answer 3. Labelers rank outputs; the RM is trained on the resulting pairwise comparisons. Demonstrations are the SFT data, not the RM data.

Q3. Which model did labelers prefer over the 175B GPT-3 despite being about 100 times smaller?

Answer 1. The 1.3B PPO-ptx model beat raw 175B GPT-3 in human preference. The reward model is a scorer, not a chatbot.

References

  1. Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback — Ouyang et al., OpenAI, NeurIPS, 2022. The paper; Figure 2 and Section 3.5 are the method.
  2. Aligning language models to follow instructions — OpenAI blog, 2022. The short version with side-by-side GPT-3 and InstructGPT samples.
  3. Deep reinforcement learning from human preferences — Christiano et al., OpenAI and DeepMind, NeurIPS, 2017. Where learning a reward from pairwise comparisons began.
  4. Learning to summarize from human feedback — Stiennon et al., OpenAI, NeurIPS, 2020. The same pipeline on one task, with a clearer analysis of reward-model over-optimisation.
  5. Proximal Policy Optimization Algorithms — Schulman et al., OpenAI, 2017. The RL algorithm used in stage three.
  6. Illustrating Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) — Lambert et al., Hugging Face blog, 2022. The best diagrams of the three stages.
  7. Direct Preference Optimization — Rafailov et al., Stanford, NeurIPS, 2023. The simplification that removed the reward model and PPO.
  8. Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI Feedback — Bai et al., Anthropic, 2022. Replacing human harmlessness labels with principle-guided AI feedback.
2020 · Facebook AI Research / University College London / New York University

Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP Tasks

Gave a text generator a search engine: fetch relevant Wikipedia passages at query time and condition the answer on them, so knowledge can be updated and traced.

arXiv:2005.11401 · Lewis, Perez, Piktus, Petroni, Karpukhin, Goyal, Küttler, Lewis, Yih, Rocktäschel, Riedel, Kiela · NeurIPS 2020

In one sentence

Pair a dense retriever over 21 million Wikipedia chunks with a seq2seq generator, train them end to end by averaging over the top retrieved passages, and you beat closed-book models on open-domain question answering while generating text that is more specific and more often factual.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Two students take a hard history test. The first has to answer from memory only. He has read a lot, so he does fine, but when he is not sure he sometimes makes up a confident-sounding answer, and if a fact changed after he studied, he has no way to know. The second student is allowed to bring a library card. For each question she first jots down the key words, runs to the shelves, grabs the five most useful pages, reads them, and then writes her answer in her own words. She is right more often, she can point to the page she used, and if the library swaps in a newer encyclopedia next year, her answers update automatically, with no re-studying. This paper built the second student out of two programs, a finder that is good at picking pages and a writer that is good at composing answers, and trained them together so the finder learns which pages actually help the writer. Almost every chatbot that answers questions about a company's own documents today works this way.

The problem they wanted to solve

Large pre-trained models carry a surprising amount of factual knowledge in their weights Petroni 2019, but that parametric memory has three problems: it cannot be updated without retraining, it cannot say where an answer came from, and it is unreliable, so models invent facts. On knowledge-intensive tasks such as open-domain question answering, a closed-book T5 with 11B parameters still trailed systems that retrieved documents and extracted a span. Those retrieve-and-extract systems, including REALM, could only copy answers out of the retrieved text Guu 2020. The authors wanted a model that could retrieve and then generate freely, trained with nothing but question–answer pairs Lewis 2020.

The key idea

Treat the retrieved document as a hidden variable and marginalise over it. Given input x, a retriever p_η(z|x) scores passages z, a generator p_θ(y|x, z) writes the output given the input and one passage, and the model's answer probability sums over the top-k passages weighted by their retrieval scores. Two versions differ in where the sum sits. RAG-Sequence uses one passage for the whole output and sums over passages at the end. RAG-Token lets every output token draw on a different passage, summing per token. Because the sum is differentiable, the loss on the final answer trains the generator and the query encoder together, with no labels saying which passage was the right one. The document encoder and its index are kept frozen, since re-encoding 21 million chunks after every update is too expensive and turned out to be unnecessary.

How it works, step by step

  1. Build the non-parametric memory: split a December 2018 Wikipedia dump into disjoint 100-word chunks, about 21 million of them; embed each with a BERT-base document encoder from Dense Passage Retrieval Karpukhin 2020; store the vectors in a FAISS index for fast maximum-inner-product search.
  2. Encode the question with a second BERT-base query encoder and fetch the top-k chunks (k of 5 or 10 during training).
  3. Concatenate the question with each chunk and feed it to the generator, BART-large, a 406M-parameter seq2seq Transformer.
  4. Combine: RAG-Sequence multiplies the retrieval score by the whole-sequence likelihood for each chunk and sums; RAG-Token sums over chunks at each token step.
  5. Train on (question, answer) pairs only, minimising the negative log-likelihood of the answer; gradients reach BART and the query encoder.
  6. Decode with beam search; RAG-Sequence needs a per-document beam and a "thorough" or "fast" merge (Section 2.5).

What the results showed

Open-domain QA, exact matchNatural QuestionsTriviaQAWebQuestionsCuratedTrec
T5-11B, closed book34.550.137.4—
REALM40.4—40.746.8
DPR, retrieve and extract41.557.941.150.6
RAG-Token44.155.245.550.0
RAG-Sequence44.556.845.252.2

From Table 1 Lewis 2020. On three of four datasets RAG set a new state of the art without ever being told which passage holds the answer. The generation results are more telling. On Jeopardy-style question writing, human judges rated RAG's output more factual than BART's in 42.7% of 452 pairs against 7.1% the other way (Table 4). In the ablation with the retriever frozen, Natural Questions fell from 43.5 to 37.8, so learning to retrieve mattered. And the hot-swap test in Section 4.5 is the demo everyone repeats: with a 2016 index the model answered 70% of questions about 2016 world leaders; swapping to the 2018 index raised the 2018 questions to 68% and dropped the 2016 ones to 4%, with no retraining.

Why it mattered

The paper gave the pattern its name. "RAG" now describes any system that retrieves text and puts it in a language model's context, which is how almost every enterprise assistant answers questions about private documents, and the subject of Day 12. The paper also settled two design choices that stuck: dense retrieval with a learned bi-encoder (the retriever itself is two BERT encoders) over small fixed-size chunks, and the argument that external memory is the right place for facts because it can be inspected, cited and swapped.

Limitations, and what came after

The generator is small by any later standard, the knowledge source is only Wikipedia, chunks are 100 words with no overlap, and exact match on short answers is a narrow lens. Marginalising over k passages scales badly with k; Fusion-in-Decoder instead encodes each passage separately and lets the decoder attend over all of them, which beat RAG on the same benchmarks a few months later Izacard 2020. Modern practice drops the joint training entirely: an off-the-shelf embedding model retrieves, and the passages are simply pasted into the prompt of a large instruction-tuned model, with rerankers and query rewriting layered on top Gao 2023. Long context windows (Day 11) and agents that search iteratively (Day 12) compete with and extend the idea, but retrieval errors still propagate straight into answers, and evaluating a RAG system is still mostly evaluating its retriever (Day 13).

How to read the actual paper

Figure 1 and Section 2.1, which has the two marginalisation formulas, are the whole method. Sections 2.2–2.4 cover the retriever, the generator and the decision to freeze the document encoder; Section 2.5 the decoding. Table 1 and Table 2 are the results, Table 4 the human evaluation.
