Do not skip Section 4.4 (the frozen-retriever ablation and the effect of k) or 4.5 (index hot-swapping), which are short. Appendix A has the exact k values used at test time. About 60 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. Which component was kept frozen during training?

Answer 3. Re-encoding 21M chunks after each update is too costly, and the authors found it unnecessary; the query encoder and BART are fine-tuned.

Q2. What is the difference between RAG-Sequence and RAG-Token?

Answer 2. The sum over passages sits outside the sequence likelihood in RAG-Sequence and inside each token step in RAG-Token. Same data, same tokenizer.

Q3. What happened when the 2018 Wikipedia index was swapped for a 2016 one?

Answer 4. 70% versus 4% on the 2016 questions depending on the index. That is the whole case for keeping facts outside the weights.

References

  1. Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP Tasks — Lewis et al., Facebook AI Research, UCL, NYU, NeurIPS, 2020. The paper; Section 2.1 and Section 4.5 are the parts to remember.
  2. NeurIPS 2020 proceedings entry — NeurIPS, 2020. The published version.
  3. Retrieval Augmented Generation: Streamlining the creation of intelligent NLP models — Meta AI blog, 2020. The announcement, with the authors' own framing of parametric versus non-parametric memory.
  4. RAG in Hugging Face Transformers — Hugging Face docs. Runnable RAG-Sequence and RAG-Token models with the original index.
  5. Dense Passage Retrieval for Open-Domain Question Answering — Karpukhin et al., Facebook AI Research, EMNLP, 2020. The retriever RAG is built on.
  6. REALM: Retrieval-Augmented Language Model Pre-Training — Guu et al., Google, ICML, 2020. The closest predecessor, retrieving during pre-training but extracting spans.
  7. Leveraging Passage Retrieval with Generative Models (Fusion-in-Decoder) — Izacard, Grave, Facebook AI Research, 2020. The follow-up that scaled to many more passages.
  8. Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Large Language Models: A Survey — Gao et al., 2023. Map of everything that came after, from naive to modular RAG.
2021 · Microsoft

LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models

Made fine-tuning a giant model cheap by training two thin matrices per layer instead of the whole thing.

arXiv:2106.09685 · Hu, Shen, Wallis, Allen-Zhu, Li, Wang, Wang, Chen · ICLR 2022

In one sentence

Freeze the pretrained weights, learn the change to each weight matrix as the product of two small matrices with a tiny rank r, and you train around 0.01% of the parameters, with no extra cost at inference because the product can be folded back into the original weights.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a huge library with a million books, and you want the library to also answer questions about your school. One way is to rewrite every book. That takes forever, and you would need a second building to store the new copies. LoRA does something sneakier. It leaves all the books alone and adds one thin notebook of sticky notes at the end of each shelf. The notes say things like "when someone asks about the cafeteria, also look at page 12 of this book." Writing the notes is fast because there are few of them. If you want the library to learn about a different school tomorrow, you swap in a different notebook, and the million books stay exactly as they were. When you are done, you can even glue the notes into the books, so looking things up is no slower than before. That is LoRA: keep the big model, learn a small set of notes, and swap notes for different jobs.

The problem they wanted to solve

By 2021, the best way to make a pretrained model good at a task was full fine-tuning: continue training every weight on task data (see Day 10). For GPT-3 175B that is painful in three ways. Every task produces a complete 175B-parameter copy, about 350 GB in 16-bit, so serving ten tasks means storing ten copies and reloading them on every switch. Training needs optimizer state for every weight, which pushed GPU memory for GPT-3 fine-tuning to roughly 1.2 TB. Hu 2021

Earlier fixes had their own costs. Adapters, small layers inserted into the network, must run in sequence at inference and add latency. Houlsby 2019 Prefix tuning trains virtual tokens prepended to the input, which eats context length and was hard to optimize. Li 2021 Section 3 of the paper, titled "Aren't existing solutions good enough?", walks through both.

The key idea

Prior work had shown that fine-tuning moves the weights within a surprisingly low-dimensional subspace. Aghajanyan 2020 LoRA bets that the update to each weight matrix is therefore low-rank: it can be written as a tall-thin matrix times a short-wide one. For a weight W of size d×k, LoRA keeps W frozen and learns ΔW = B·A where B is d×r and A is r×k, with r as small as 1 to 8. The forward pass becomes

h = W·x + B·A·x

B starts at zero, so training begins exactly at the pretrained model, and the adapter path is scaled by a constant α/r. Because B·A is just another d×k matrix, you can add it into W once training ends and serve the merged weight. No new layers, no sequential work, zero inference latency.

How it works, step by step

  1. Pick which matrices to adapt. The paper adapts only the attention query and value projections (W_q, W_v) in most experiments and leaves the MLPs alone.
  2. For each chosen matrix, create A (r×k, random Gaussian) and B (d×r, all zeros), so ΔW = 0 at the start.
  3. Run the forward pass as W·x plus the scaled B·A·x path.
  4. Train only A and B with Adam. W gets no gradients and no optimizer state, which is where most of the memory saving comes from.
  5. Save the adapter. For GPT-3 175B with r = 4 on W_q and W_v the checkpoint shrinks from 350 GB to about 35 MB.
  6. At deployment either merge W + B·A into one matrix, or keep adapters separate and hot-swap them per task on one shared base model.

Try it: the widget shows a weight matrix next to its A and B factors. Move the rank slider and watch the trainable-parameter count change against full fine-tuning.

Even r = 8 on a 4096×4096 matrix trains about 65 thousand parameters instead of 16.8 million, under 0.4%.

What the results showed

The headline table is GPT-3 175B on three tasks. LoRA with 4.7 million trainable parameters, about 37,000 times fewer than full fine-tuning, matches or beats it. Hu 2021

GPT-3 175B (Table 4)Trainable paramsWikiSQL acc.MNLI-m acc.SAMSum R1/R2/RL
Full fine-tuning175,255.8M73.889.552.0 / 28.0 / 44.5
LoRA4.7M73.491.753.8 / 29.8 / 45.9
LoRA37.7M74.091.653.4 / 29.2 / 45.1

On the systems side, training memory for GPT-3 dropped from about 1.2 TB to 350 GB, checkpoints shrank roughly 10,000×, and training ran about 25% faster because most parameters need no gradient. The analysis in Section 7 is the quiet star: adapting W_q and W_v together beats spending the same budget on any single matrix, and rank 1 performs almost as well as rank 64, which implies the needed update really is tiny.

Why it mattered

LoRA turned fine-tuning from a lab activity into a hobby. A 7B model in 16-bit is 14 GB, and with LoRA the trainable state on top is a few hundred megabytes, so a single consumer GPU can do it. Hugging Face built the PEFT library around it, and the same base model can serve many customers with a different adapter each, which is how most "custom model" products work. Hugging Face PEFT It also made the QLoRA paper possible, which shrinks the frozen base to 4-bit and trains LoRA on top.

Limitations, and what came after

Which matrices to adapt, and what rank, are still chosen by trial; the paper's own recipe (only W_q and W_v) was later found too narrow, and QLoRA showed you need adapters on every linear layer to match 16-bit fine-tuning. Dettmers 2023 And a low-rank update cannot learn everything: tasks that need a lot of new knowledge still favor full fine-tuning or continued pretraining. Follow-ups include DoRA, which splits the update into magnitude and direction, Liu 2024 and the whole parameter-efficient zoo catalogued in the PEFT docs. As of October 2026, LoRA-style adapters remain the default way to specialize open-weights models, and the lesson of Day 10 is that the first question is not "whether" but "which layers and what rank".

How to read the actual paper

Read Figure 1 and Section 4.1 first: the whole method is one equation. Then jump to Table 4 (GPT-3 results) and Section 4.2 for the memory and checkpoint numbers. Section 7 (which matrices, what rank, how ΔW relates to W) is the most interesting analysis and takes 15 minutes. Skip the GLUE tables in Section 5.2 and the appendix hyperparameters unless you are reproducing. Section 3 is a good, short argument for why adapters and prefix tuning fall short. Total: about 45 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. Why does LoRA add no latency at inference time?

Answer 3. B·A is a d×k matrix like W, so W + B·A is one matrix and the forward pass is unchanged. Adapters, by contrast, add layers that must run in sequence.

Q2. In the GPT-3 experiments, how small a rank was enough when adapting both W_q and W_v?

Answer 1. Table 6 shows rank 1 already works well for W_q and W_v together; the 4.7M-parameter LoRA uses r = 1 or 2. Higher ranks add little, which is the paper's evidence that the update is intrinsically low-rank.

Q3. During LoRA training, which of these receives gradient updates?

Answer 2. W is frozen; only A and B train. That is why there is no optimizer state for W and training memory falls by about two thirds.

References

  1. LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models — Hu et al., Microsoft, ICLR 2022 (arXiv 2021). The paper itself; short and readable.
  2. ICLR 2022 poster page for LoRA — ICLR, 2022. Confirms the venue and links the talk.
  3. microsoft/LoRA (loralib) — Microsoft, GitHub, 2021. The reference implementation; a few hundred lines of PyTorch.
  4. PEFT documentation — Hugging Face. The library most people actually use to apply LoRA; the method overview compares alternatives.
  5. Parameter-Efficient Transfer Learning for NLP — Houlsby et al., Google, ICML 2019. The adapter method LoRA was designed to beat on latency.
  6. Intrinsic Dimensionality Explains the Effectiveness of Language Model Fine-Tuning — Aghajanyan et al., Facebook AI, 2020. The evidence that fine-tuning lives in a low-dimensional subspace, which motivates LoRA.
  7. QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs — Dettmers et al., NeurIPS 2023. The sequel that puts the frozen base in 4-bit.
  8. DoRA: Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation — Liu et al., NVIDIA, 2024. A popular refinement; useful to see where plain LoRA falls short.
2022 · Google Research, Brain team

Chain-of-Thought Prompting Elicits Reasoning in Large Language Models

Showed that asking a big model to show its work, with a few worked examples, unlocks multi-step reasoning with no training at all.

arXiv:2201.11903 · Wei, Wang, Schuurmans, Bosma, Ichter, Xia, Chi, Le, Zhou · NeurIPS 2022

In one sentence

If the few-shot examples in your prompt contain the intermediate reasoning steps and not only the final answers, a large enough model (around 100B parameters and up) writes its own steps for new problems and gets far more math, commonsense and symbolic questions right: PaLM 540B went from 17.9% to 56.9% on grade-school math.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Think about a maths test. If your teacher only ever shows you the final answer, "42", you learn to guess fast, and you guess wrong on the hard questions. But if the teacher shows the working ("there were 5 boxes with 3 apples each, so 15 apples, then 2 were eaten, so 13"), you learn to go step by step, and the hard questions become easy because each small step is easy. Computers that write text learned by reading a huge amount of the internet, and they are very good at copying a pattern they just saw. So the trick in this paper is simply this: before you ask your question, show the computer a few questions answered with the working written out. The computer copies the style, writes its own working for your question, and because it takes small steps instead of one big leap, it gets the answer right much more often. The surprising part is that this only works once the computer is really big. Small ones write confusing steps and still get it wrong.

The problem they wanted to solve

Scaling up models had improved almost everything except multi-step reasoning. GPT-3 with ordinary few-shot prompting was nearly flat on math word problems, and the usual fix, fine-tuning on thousands of hand-written solutions, is expensive and must be redone per task. Cobbe 2021 The question was whether a frozen model already contains the ability to reason and just needs the right prompt (see Day 9 for the wider story of reasoning).

The key idea

A chain of thought is a series of short natural-language reasoning steps that lead to the final answer. Standard few-shot prompting gives the model pairs of ⟨question, answer⟩. Chain-of-thought prompting gives it triples of ⟨question, reasoning steps, answer⟩. Nothing is trained; the only change is a few hundred words of hand-written demonstrations in the prompt. The model, imitating the pattern, produces its own steps before answering, and the steps give it a place to do the arithmetic and bookkeeping that it cannot do in one token. Wei 2022

How it works, step by step

  1. Write a small set of example problems with step-by-step solutions. The arithmetic experiments use the same eight exemplars for every benchmark.
  2. Put the exemplars in the prompt, then the new question.
  3. Let the model generate with greedy decoding. It writes steps, then a line like "The answer is 13."
  4. Parse the number after "The answer is" and compare with the reference.
  5. Compare against standard prompting with the same eight problems but answers only, so the only variable is the presence of reasoning steps.
  6. Repeat across model families (LaMDA, GPT-3, PaLM) and sizes, and across arithmetic, commonsense and symbolic tasks.
Q: Roger has 5 tennis balls. He buys 2 more cans of 3 balls each.
   How many tennis balls does he have now?
A: Roger started with 5 balls. 2 cans of 3 balls each is 6 balls.
   5 + 6 = 11. The answer is 11.

Q: The cafeteria had 23 apples. They used 20 for lunch and bought 6 more.
   How many apples do they have?
A: <the model continues here, step by step>

The structure of a chain-of-thought exemplar, in the style of the paper's Figure 1: the worked example comes first, then the real question.

What the results showed

On GSM8K, the 8,500-problem grade-school math set, PaLM 540B with chain-of-thought prompting beat the previous best, a GPT-3 fine-tuned on solutions and paired with a trained verifier, using nothing but eight examples. Wei 2022

Task (PaLM 540B unless noted)Standard promptingChain of thoughtPrior best
GSM8K17.956.955 (fine-tuned GPT-3 + verifier)
GSM8K, GPT-3 175B15.646.9—
GSM8K, LaMDA 137B6.514.3—
SVAMP69.479.057.4
MAWPS79.293.388.4
Last-letter concatenation, 4 words (out of domain)0.063.0—
Coin flip, 4 flips (out of domain)54.890.2—
Sports understanding80.595.4—

Three findings matter more than any single number. First, scale: chain of thought hurts or does nothing below roughly 100B parameters and then takes off (Figure 4), which the authors call an emergent ability, a capability that appears abruptly with scale. Wei 2022b Second, the ablations in Section 3.3: giving only the equation, giving the same number of filler tokens, or putting the reasoning after the answer all fail, so it really is the natural-language steps before the answer that help. Third, robustness: different annotators writing different exemplars still get large gains, so the effect is not one lucky prompt.

Why it mattered

This paper made "prompting" a research subject and gave the field a cheap probe for reasoning. It also moved the goalposts: once you accept that a model can think out loud, you can sample many chains and vote (self-consistency raised GSM8K by a further 17.9 points), Wang 2022 or drop the exemplars entirely and just write "Let's think step by step" (zero-shot CoT took GPT-3 from 10.4% to 40.7% on GSM8K). Kojima 2022 The reasoning models of 2024 to 2026, which are trained to produce long chains of thought by reinforcement learning, are the direct descendants; see the DeepSeek-R1 review.

Limitations, and what came after

The chain is not guaranteed to be the real cause of the answer; models sometimes write a plausible chain and then answer something else, and the steps can be wrong even when the answer is right, so chains are not a faithful explanation. Gains are task-dependent: commonsense QA barely moved (78.1 to 79.9), while arithmetic and symbolic tasks jumped. The method costs tokens, since every answer now carries a paragraph, and it needed models that in 2022 only a few labs had. Later work verified individual steps with process reward models, Lightman 2023 and then trained models to produce chains without any exemplars at all, which is where Day 9 picks up. As of October 2026, "chain of thought" is less a prompting trick than the native output format of frontier models.

How to read the actual paper

Figure 1 is the whole idea in one picture. Then read Section 3.2 with Figure 4, the scale curves, and the ablations in Section 3.3, which are the most convincing two pages. Table 2 in the appendix has the exact arithmetic numbers; Sections 4 and 5 show the same pattern on commonsense and symbolic tasks, so skim them. The appendix also prints every exemplar prompt, worth copying if you want to try it. Skip Section 7 (related work). About 40 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. What exactly did the authors change compared with standard few-shot prompting?

Answer 2. Nothing is trained; the exemplars simply include the working. Decoding is greedy and the context is unchanged.

Q2. What happened when chain-of-thought prompting was applied to small models?

Answer 4. Figure 4 shows flat or negative effects for small models and a jump at scale; small models produce fluent but illogical chains.

Q3. In the ablation where the reasoning steps were placed after the final answer, what happened?

Answer 1. The benefit comes from computing the answer through the steps; a chain written afterwards cannot influence the answer token.

References

  1. Chain-of-Thought Prompting Elicits Reasoning in Large Language Models — Wei et al., Google Research, NeurIPS 2022. The paper; the appendix prompts are directly reusable.
  2. NeurIPS 2022 proceedings entry — NeurIPS, 2022. The published version.
  3. Language Models Perform Reasoning via Chain of Thought — Wei and Zhou, Google Research blog, May 2022. A five-minute version by the authors.
  4. Self-Consistency Improves Chain of Thought Reasoning in Language Models — Wang et al., Google, ICLR 2023. Sample many chains and take a majority vote; the first big follow-up.
  5. Large Language Models are Zero-Shot Reasoners — Kojima et al., University of Tokyo and Google, NeurIPS 2022. The "Let's think step by step" result.
  6. Training Verifiers to Solve Math Word Problems — Cobbe et al., OpenAI, 2021. Introduces GSM8K and the fine-tuned-plus-verifier baseline that chain of thought beat.
  7. PaLM: Scaling Language Modeling with Pathways — Chowdhery et al., Google, 2022. The 540B model behind the headline numbers.
  8. Emergent Abilities of Large Language Models — Wei et al., 2022. The companion paper arguing that abilities like chain of thought switch on with scale.
2022 · Stanford University / University at Buffalo

FlashAttention: Fast and Memory-Efficient Exact Attention with IO-Awareness

Made exact attention several times faster and far leaner by never writing the big attention matrix to GPU memory.

arXiv:2205.14135 · Dao, Fu, Ermon, Rudra, Ré · NeurIPS 2022

In one sentence

Attention on a GPU is slow because it moves the N×N score matrix in and out of slow memory, not because it does too many multiplications; FlashAttention splits Q, K and V into blocks that fit in the chip's small fast memory, computes the softmax incrementally, and recomputes instead of storing in the backward pass, so it returns exactly the same numbers with a fraction of the memory traffic.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine doing a giant jigsaw at a tiny desk while the pieces are kept in boxes in the garage. The slow way: for every piece, walk to the garage, bring one piece, try it, walk back, put it away. Most of your time is walking, not puzzling. FlashAttention is the smart way: bring a whole tray of pieces to the desk, do everything you can with that tray, write down only a few short notes, then fetch the next tray. You never need to lay the whole puzzle out in the garage, so you save space, and you walk far less, so you finish much faster. The finished puzzle is exactly the same picture. The only hard part is that one step (working out how important each piece is compared with all the others) normally needs to see everything at once. The authors found a way to do that tray by tray, by keeping a small running total and fixing it up whenever a new tray arrives.

The problem they wanted to solve

Self-attention (Day 4) compares every token with every other token, so for a sequence of N tokens it builds an N×N matrix of scores. Time and memory grow with N². Many "efficient attention" papers approximated the matrix with sparsity or low-rank tricks, and most did not run faster on real hardware. Dao 2022

The authors' diagnosis: the bottleneck is memory IO, not arithmetic. An A100 GPU has 40 to 80 GB of high-bandwidth memory (HBM) at 1.5 to 2.0 TB/s, but each of its 108 compute units has only 192 KB of on-chip SRAM that runs at roughly 19 TB/s. The standard implementation computes S = Q·Kᵀ, writes S to HBM, reads it back for the softmax, writes P, reads it again to multiply by V: N×N round trips while the multipliers sit idle.

The key idea

Count HBM reads and writes, and design the algorithm to minimize them. Two techniques do the work. Tiling: process K and V in blocks small enough to live in SRAM, and update the output one block at a time. The obstacle is softmax, which needs the maximum and the sum over a whole row; the fix is the online softmax, keeping a running max m and running sum ℓ per row and rescaling earlier partial results whenever a block raises the max. Milakov 2018 Recomputation: in the backward pass, instead of storing the N×N probability matrix for the gradient, store only the output and the two per-row statistics (size N), and recompute attention blocks on the fly. Everything runs in one fused CUDA kernel. The result needs Θ(N²d²/M) HBM accesses instead of Θ(N·d + N²), where d is the head dimension (64 to 128) and M is SRAM size, a large reduction for realistic values.

How it works, step by step

  1. Split Q into blocks of rows and K, V into blocks of columns, sized so one block of each fits in SRAM.
  2. Load one K/V block. For each Q block, compute the block of scores Q_i·K_jᵀ on-chip.
  3. Update the running row max m and row sum ℓ. If the new block has a larger max, rescale the partial output accumulated so far (the online-softmax correction).
  4. Accumulate O_i += P_ij·V_j in SRAM; never write S or P to HBM. After the last block, write O, m and ℓ.
  5. Backward pass: load Q, K, V, O and the saved statistics; recompute each block of P from them to get gradients. Extra arithmetic, far less memory traffic.

What the results showed

The output is exact, so the paper measures speed, memory and what longer context buys. Dao 2022

MeasurementBaselineFlashAttention
BERT-large training to target accuracy (Table 1)20.0 min (MLPerf 1.1 record)17.4 min (15% faster)
GPT-2 small training, 8×A100 (Table 2)9.5 days (Hugging Face)2.7 days (3.5×)
GPT-2 medium training (Table 2)21.0 days6.9 days (3.0×)
GPT-2 perplexity (Table 4)18.2 at 1K context17.5 at 4K context, still ~30% faster
Path-X, 16K tokens (Table 6)chance for all prior Transformers61.4%
Path-256, 64K tokens, block-sparse (Table 6)chance63.1%
Attention memoryN² baselineup to 20× smaller

The attention operation alone ran up to 7.6× faster than the PyTorch implementation on GPT-2 (Figure 1). The perplexity row is the one to remember: four times the context, less time, a better model.

Why it mattered

FlashAttention is why long context became normal. Every serious training and serving stack adopted it or its descendants: it is one of the fused backends behind torch.nn.functional.scaled_dot_product_attention in PyTorch, PyTorch docs and the official library is a dependency of most open-source training code. Dao-AILab It also changed how researchers think: the useful question is not "how many FLOPs" but "how many bytes move between memory levels", the same lens that explains why the KV cache dominates inference (Day 7) and why Day 11 architectures like grouped-query attention exist.

Limitations, and what came after

The first version required hand-written CUDA kernels per GPU generation, and it was still far from the hardware ceiling: it reached only 25 to 40% of the A100's theoretical peak FLOPs. Dao 2023 FlashAttention-2 (2023) reorganized the work across GPU threads, cut non-matmul operations, and reached 50 to 73% of peak, about 2× faster again; end-to-end GPT training hit 225 TFLOPs/s per A100, or 72% model-FLOPs utilization. FlashAttention-3 (2024) was rebuilt for the H100, overlapping matrix multiplies with softmax using the chip's asynchronous units and adding FP8 support: 1.5 to 2× faster than FA-2, up to 740 TFLOPs/s in FP16 (75% utilization) and close to 1.2 PFLOPs/s in FP8. Shah 2024 Attention is still quadratic in N, only with a smaller constant, so as of October 2026 very long contexts also lean on sparse or linear-time variants.

How to read the actual paper

Start with Section 2.1 (one page on GPU memory levels) and Figure 1. Then Section 3.1 with Algorithm 1, the core of the paper in about twenty lines, and read Theorem 2's statement in Section 3.2 without the proof. Section 4 is tables: Tables 1, 2 and 4 are the ones quoted above, Table 6 the long-sequence results. Skip Appendix B and C (proofs and the full backward algorithm) unless you plan to implement it. About an hour.

Check yourself

Q1. Why is FlashAttention called "exact"?

Answer 2. Unlike sparse or low-rank approximations, the math is identical; tiling and recomputation reorder the work without changing the result.

Q2. Which object does FlashAttention avoid writing to GPU high-bandwidth memory?

Answer 4. Q, K, V and O (all N×d) still live in HBM. The N×N intermediates stay in SRAM block by block.

Q3. How does the backward pass get the attention probabilities without storing them?

Answer 1. Recomputation trades extra FLOPs for far fewer memory accesses, and the saved max and sum make the recomputed softmax exact.

References

  1. FlashAttention: Fast and Memory-Efficient Exact Attention with IO-Awareness — Dao, Fu, Ermon, Rudra, Ré, NeurIPS 2022. The paper; Algorithm 1 is the part to study.
  2. NeurIPS 2022 proceedings entry — NeurIPS, 2022. The published version.
  3. FlashAttention-2: Faster Attention with Better Parallelism and Work Partitioning — Dao, 2023. The 2× follow-up; explains what the first version left on the table.
  4. FlashAttention-3: Fast and Accurate Attention with Asynchrony and Low-precision — Shah, Bikshandi, Zhang, Thakkar, Ramani, Dao, 2024. The H100 and FP8 version.
  5. FlashAttention-3 blog post — Tri Dao, July 2024. Readable summary of the whole lineage with charts.
  6. Dao-AILab/flash-attention — GitHub. The official library used by most training frameworks.
  7. Online normalizer calculation for softmax — Milakov and Gimelshein, NVIDIA, 2018. The running-max trick that makes tiling possible.
  8. Self-attention Does Not Need O(n²) Memory — Rabe and Staats, Google, 2021. The memory-saving idea FlashAttention turned into a wall-clock speedup.
2022 · Anthropic

Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI Feedback

Replaced human labels for harmlessness with a written list of principles and AI-generated feedback, producing an assistant that is harmless without being evasive.

arXiv:2212.08073 · Bai, Kadavath, Kundu, Askell, Kernion, Jones, Chen, Goldie and 43 others, last author Kaplan · arXiv preprint, December 2022

In one sentence

Write down a short "constitution" of principles, have the model critique and rewrite its own harmful answers against those principles (a supervised stage), then have an AI rather than humans judge which of two answers is more harmless and train with reinforcement learning against that judge (RL from AI feedback), so the only human input on harmlessness is the list of principles itself.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a very talented new helper who will answer any question you ask, even bad ones, like how to trick a friend. One way to teach the helper manners is to have thousands of people read its answers and say "good" or "bad" each time. That is slow, and the people get tired and sad from reading the bad stuff. This paper tries something else. First, the grown-ups write a short list of rules, like "be kind, don't help anyone get hurt, and explain why if you say no." Then the helper reads its own answer, checks it against one rule, writes down what was wrong, and writes a better answer. It does this many, many times and learns from its own corrections. Next, instead of people picking the better of two answers, a copy of the helper picks, using the rules. The nice result is a helper that does not just clam up and say "I can't talk about that". It explains gently why it won't help and offers something useful instead.

The problem they wanted to solve

Reinforcement learning from human feedback (Day 8, InstructGPT) needs tens of thousands of human comparisons for every behavior you want, and harmlessness data is the worst kind to collect: crowdworkers spend their day reading requests for violence and abuse. Anthropic's earlier helpful-and-harmless models had another flaw: trained to avoid harm, they became evasive, refusing anything near a sensitive topic with a flat "I can't help with that". Bai 2022a The authors also wanted the rules written down and inspectable, and a method that keeps working as models outgrow their human supervisors. Bai 2022b

The key idea

Supervise with principles, not labels. A constitution here is a list of sixteen short natural-language instructions such as "Identify specific ways in which the assistant's last response is harmful" and "Choose the response that a wise, ethical, polite and friendly person would more likely say." Those sentences are used in two places: as the prompt for the model to critique and revise its own answers, and as the prompt for an AI feedback model to pick the better of two answers. Human feedback is kept only for helpfulness. Everything about harmlessness flows from the written principles plus the model's own judgment, which the paper calls RLAIF, reinforcement learning from AI feedback.

How it works, step by step

  1. Start from a helpful-only RLHF model (52B parameters in the main experiments), which will cheerfully answer harmful requests.
  2. Sample its responses to 182,831 red-team prompts designed to elicit harm.
  3. Critique: prompt the model with one randomly drawn principle and ask it to identify how its response violates it.
  4. Revise: ask it to rewrite the response to fix the critique. Draw a new principle and repeat; the paper samples several revision rounds and uses the final revision.
  5. Fine-tune the pretrained model on the revisions, mixed with ordinary helpfulness data, for one epoch. This is SL-CAI.
  6. Generate pairs of responses from SL-CAI to harmful prompts. Ask a feedback model, given a principle, which response is better; use its normalized probabilities as soft labels, optionally after a chain-of-thought explanation.
  7. Train a preference model on the AI harmlessness labels plus 135,296 human helpfulness comparisons.
  8. Run reinforcement learning against that preference model, exactly as in RLHF. This is RL-CAI.

What the results showed

The paper reports quality as Elo scores from crowdworker comparisons (Figures 2, 3 and 8) and an absolute harmfulness score from 0 to 4 (Section 4.5). The text gives the directions rather than the exact Elo values, so the table lists what was measured and what the authors conclude. Bai 2022b

WhatValue or finding
Principles in the constitution16 (sampled one at a time for critiques, revisions and AI preference labels)
Human harmlessness labels used for training0
Human helpfulness comparisons135,296
Red-team prompts for critique and revision182,831
Harmlessness, RL-CAI vs RLHF (Figure 2/3)RL-CAI is significantly more harmless than both helpful RLHF and helpful-and-harmless RLHF, at similar helpfulness
Chain-of-thought feedbackRL-CAI with CoT is slightly more harmless and slightly less helpful than without
Absolute harmfulness over training (Section 4.5)Helpful RLHF gets more harmful as training proceeds; RL-CAI gets steadily less harmful
Evasiveness (Section 4.4)RL-CAI engages with harmful prompts and explains its objections instead of refusing flatly

One side result matters: Section 2 shows that a model's ability to judge helpful, honest and harmless behavior improves with scale and with chain-of-thought, which is what makes AI feedback viable at all.

Why it mattered

This paper is the training recipe behind Claude, and it introduced two ideas the whole field now uses. The first is AI feedback: later work from Google found RLAIF roughly as good as RLHF on summarization and dialogue, Lee 2023 and model-generated preferences are now routine in post-training. The second is the constitution as a public artifact: Anthropic published Claude's constitution in May 2023 and has updated it since, Anthropic 2023 and ran an experiment in which members of the public drafted the principles. Anthropic 2023b It also reframed harmlessness: the goal is a model that pushes back and explains, not one that hides. Day 14 returns to this under scalable oversight.

Limitations, and what came after

Somebody still writes the principles, and the paper is candid that its sixteen were chosen ad hoc by the authors; whose values go in is a political question, not a technical one. AI feedback inherits the feedback model's biases and blind spots, and the method was validated only on harmlessness, with helpfulness still human-labeled. Later work replaced the PPO stage with simpler preference losses (see the DPO review), and reasoning models made the "critique then revise" loop part of the model's own thinking (DeepSeek-R1). As of October 2026, "principles plus AI feedback" is standard, and the open question is the one the paper raised in its discussion: how to tell whether a model's judgment is trustworthy once it exceeds ours.

How to read the actual paper

Read Section 1.2 with Figure 1 for the pipeline, then Section 3.1, which walks through one real critique-and-revision example (the harmful prompt about hacking a neighbor's wifi) and is the most memorable page. Section 4.1 explains AI feedback and the chain-of-thought variant; Figures 2 and 3 are the results, Section 4.4 the evasiveness argument. Appendix C prints the constitution and takes five minutes. Skip Section 2 on a first pass and the scaling plots in 3.4. About an hour.

Check yourself

Q1. Which human labels does Constitutional AI still use during training?

Answer 3. Harmlessness labels come from the AI feedback model guided by the principles; the 135,296 human comparisons are for helpfulness.

Q2. What is the "constitution" in this paper, concretely?

Answer 1. The principles are plain sentences; one is sampled at a time to steer each critique, revision or comparison.

Q3. What does the paper mean by a "non-evasive" harmless assistant?

Answer 4. Earlier harmless models refused flatly; RL-CAI explains its objections, which the authors argue is both safer and more useful.

References

  1. Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI Feedback — Bai et al., Anthropic, 2022. The paper; Appendix C has the full constitution.
  2. Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI feedback (research page) — Anthropic, December 2022. Short summary and link to the PDF.
  3. Claude's constitution — Anthropic, May 2023 (updated 2026). The principles actually used for Claude, with explanations of where they came from.
  4. Collective Constitutional AI: Aligning a language model with public input — Anthropic and the Collective Intelligence Project, October 2023. What happens when the public writes the principles.
  5. Training a Helpful and Harmless Assistant with Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback — Bai et al., Anthropic, 2022. The RLHF predecessor whose evasiveness this paper set out to fix.
  6. Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback — Ouyang et al., OpenAI, 2022. The InstructGPT pipeline that Constitutional AI modifies.
  7. RLAIF vs. RLHF: Scaling Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback with AI Feedback — Lee et al., Google, ICML 2024. Independent evidence that AI feedback can match human feedback.
2023 · Stanford University

Direct Preference Optimization: Your Language Model is Secretly a Reward Model

Showed you can skip the reward model and the reinforcement-learning loop of RLHF and train directly on preference pairs with one classification-style loss.

arXiv:2305.18290 · Rafailov, Sharma, Mitchell, Ermon, Manning, Finn · NeurIPS 2023 (oral)

In one sentence

Because the best policy under RLHF's KL-penalized objective has a closed form, the reward can be rewritten in terms of the policy itself; plug that into the standard preference model and you get a loss that trains the language model directly on (prompt, preferred answer, rejected answer) triples, with no sampling, no reward model and no PPO.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Suppose you are teaching a friend to bake by taste. The old way has two steps. First you train a judge: you taste pairs of cakes, say which you liked, and the judge learns to give every cake a score. Then your friend bakes hundreds of practice cakes, the judge scores each one, and your friend slowly changes the recipe to get higher scores. It works, but it is slow and messy, and if the judge is a little wrong your friend learns to please the judge instead of baking well. This paper noticed something neat: you do not need the judge at all. Every time you say "I liked cake A more than cake B," you can directly nudge your friend's recipe toward A and away from B, just a little, while keeping it close to the recipe they already knew. No practice cakes, no judge, only the list of "A beats B" notes you already wrote down. The maths shows this is secretly the same as training the judge, so nothing is lost, and it is far simpler.

The problem they wanted to solve

The RLHF pipeline from InstructGPT (Day 8) has three stages: supervised fine-tuning, fitting a reward model to human preference pairs, then optimizing the policy with PPO to maximize reward while a KL penalty keeps it close to the SFT model. The last stage is the expensive and fragile one: four models in memory, thousands of sampled completions during training, and PPO's many hyperparameters and known instability. Rafailov 2023 The authors asked whether the preference data could be used to train the policy without ever building the reward model.

The key idea

Start from the RLHF objective: maximize expected reward r(x, y) minus β times the KL divergence from the reference policy π_ref. That problem has a known exact solution,

π*(y|x) = π_ref(y|x) · exp(r(x, y)/β) / Z(x)

where Z(x) is a normalizer that sums over all possible answers and is impossible to compute. Now invert the equation to express the reward in terms of the policy: r(x, y) = β·log(π*(y|x)/π_ref(y|x)) + β·log Z(x). Human preferences are modeled with the Bradley–Terry model, a 1952 formula in which the probability that answer A beats answer B depends only on the difference of their rewards. Wikipedia In a difference, the troublesome log Z(x) cancels. What remains is a loss written entirely in terms of the policy being trained:

L = −log σ( β·log(π(y_w|x)/π_ref(y_w|x)) − β·log(π(y_l|x)/π_ref(y_l|x)) )

Here y_w is the preferred answer, y_l the rejected one, and σ the logistic function. The quantity β·log(π/π_ref) acts as an implicit reward, which is the sense in which the language model is "secretly a reward model".

How it works, step by step

  1. Take the SFT model and freeze a copy of it as π_ref.
  2. Collect preference data: for each prompt x, a preferred completion y_w and a rejected one y_l. Existing RLHF datasets work as they are.
  3. For each pair, run both completions through the policy and the reference to get four log-probabilities.
  4. Compute the DPO loss above. β (typically 0.1 to 0.5) controls how far the policy may drift from the reference.
  5. Take a gradient step. The gradient raises the likelihood of y_w and lowers that of y_l, weighted by how wrong the implicit reward currently is, so already-correct pairs contribute little.
  6. Repeat. No sampling, no reward model: an ordinary supervised loop with two forward passes per example.
def dpo_loss(pi_logp_w, pi_logp_l, ref_logp_w, ref_logp_l, beta=0.1):
    # log-probs of the whole completion, summed over tokens
    chosen  = beta * (pi_logp_w - ref_logp_w)   # implicit reward of y_w
    rejected = beta * (pi_logp_l - ref_logp_l)  # implicit reward of y_l
    return -logsigmoid(chosen - rejected).mean()

The entire algorithm; PPO needs a rollout loop, a value head, advantage estimation and clipping.

What the results showed

Three experiments at modest scale, with GPT-4 as the judge for the open-ended tasks. Rafailov 2023

Task (model)Finding
IMDb controlled sentiment (GPT-2-large), Figure 2 leftDPO reaches higher reward at every KL budget than PPO, even PPO given the ground-truth reward
TL;DR summarization (GPT-J 6B), Figure 2 rightWin rate vs human reference summaries: DPO about 61% at temperature 0, PPO about 57% at its best temperature; DPO degrades less at high temperature
Anthropic HH single-turn dialogue (Pythia 2.8B), Figure 3DPO is the only efficient method that beats the dataset's chosen responses; comparable to best-of-128 sampling
GPT-4 as judge, Table 2GPT-4 agrees with humans 67% and 85% on two comparisons, versus human-to-human agreement of 65% and 87%

The last row was early evidence that a strong model is an acceptable proxy for human raters, now standard practice (Day 13).

Why it mattered

DPO made preference tuning something a single person could run. Hugging Face's TRL library added a DPOTrainer, TRL docs and within months Zephyr-7B, trained with DPO on AI-generated preferences, surpassed the 70B Llama 2 chat model on MT-Bench. Tunstall 2023 Then it reached the top: Meta's Llama 3 team ran six rounds of SFT, rejection sampling and DPO for post-training and reported that DPO needed less compute than PPO and did better on instruction following. Meta 2024 See the Llama 3 review.

Limitations, and what came after

DPO is offline: it learns only from the pairs you already have, so if the policy drifts into regions no pair covers, nothing corrects it, whereas RLHF samples fresh completions. The loss can be satisfied by lowering the probability of both answers as long as the gap grows, which hurts quality, and it tends to prefer longer answers. A theoretical critique argued that the logistic transform overfits when preferences are nearly deterministic and proposed IPO. Azar 2023 SimPO dropped the reference model and normalized by length. Meng 2024 And for reasoning, online RL came back: DeepSeek-R1 uses sampled rollouts with verifiable rewards, which DPO cannot express. As of October 2026 the practical split is: DPO-style losses for style and preference, RL for tasks with checkable answers.

How to read the actual paper

Section 3 is a one-page recap of RLHF; read it even if you know RLHF, because it fixes the notation. Section 4 is the derivation (Equations 4 to 7) and takes fifteen minutes with a pen; the key move is noticing that Z(x) cancels. Then Figure 2, Figure 3 and Table 2. Section 5.1 explains the title. Appendix A.1 has the one-page proof of the closed-form policy if you want it; skip the rest of the appendix. About 45 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. Which parts of the RLHF pipeline does DPO remove?

Answer 2. DPO still starts from an SFT model, still needs preference pairs, and still uses a frozen reference. It drops the reward model and PPO.

Q2. What does the hyperparameter β control?

Answer 3. β is the weight of the KL penalty in the original RLHF objective; larger β means smaller allowed deviation from π_ref.

Q3. Why is the language model "secretly a reward model"?

Answer 1. Inverting the closed-form optimal policy expresses the reward through the policy's log-ratio to the reference; DPO fits that implicit reward to the preferences.

References

  1. Direct Preference Optimization: Your Language Model is Secretly a Reward Model — Rafailov et al., Stanford, NeurIPS 2023. The paper; Section 4 is the part to work through.
  2. NeurIPS 2023 proceedings entry — NeurIPS, 2023. The published version.
  3. DPO Trainer — Hugging Face TRL documentation. The implementation most people use, with a table of the loss variants that followed.
  4. Zephyr: Direct Distillation of LM Alignment — Tunstall et al., Hugging Face, 2023. The first widely used model trained with DPO on AI-generated preferences.
  5. The Llama 3 Herd of Models — Meta AI, 2024. Section 4.1 explains why they chose DPO over PPO at 405B scale.
  6. A General Theoretical Paradigm to Understand Learning from Human Preferences — Azar et al., Google DeepMind, 2023. The IPO critique of DPO's overfitting.
  7. SimPO: Simple Preference Optimization with a Reference-Free Reward — Meng et al., 2024. Drops the reference model and fixes the length bias.
  8. Bradley–Terry model — Wikipedia. The 1952 paired-comparison model that both RLHF reward models and DPO rely on.
2023 · University of Washington

QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs

Let a single 48 GB GPU fine-tune a 65B-parameter model by freezing it in 4-bit and training LoRA adapters on top, with no measurable quality loss.

arXiv:2305.14314 · Dettmers, Pagnoni, Holtzman, Zettlemoyer · NeurIPS 2023

In one sentence

Store the frozen base model in a new 4-bit format (NF4), quantize the quantization constants too (double quantization), page optimizer memory to the CPU during spikes, and backpropagate through the 4-bit weights into 16-bit LoRA adapters; the result matched 16-bit fine-tuning and produced Guanaco, a chatbot that reached 99.3% of ChatGPT's score on the Vicuna benchmark after a day on one GPU.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Remember LoRA, where you leave the big library alone and only write a thin notebook of sticky notes? QLoRA fixes the other problem: the library itself is too big to fit in your room. The trick is to shrink every book. Instead of writing each number in the books with lots of decimal places, you round each one to the nearest of just sixteen allowed values, chosen cleverly so the rounding hurts as little as possible. The books now take a quarter of the space. You still cannot write in the shrunken books, but you never needed to; you only write in the sticky-note notebook, which stays full size. Two more tricks: the little labels that say how to un-shrink each page are themselves shrunk, and if your desk gets too crowded for a moment, you move some papers to the floor and pick them up later instead of giving up. With all that, a model that used to need a room full of computers fits on one card in a gaming PC.

The problem they wanted to solve

LoRA made the trainable part of fine-tuning tiny, but the frozen base model still has to sit in GPU memory in 16-bit: 65B parameters times 2 bytes is 130 GB before you add activations. Full 16-bit fine-tuning of LLaMA 65B needs more than 780 GB of GPU memory once gradients and optimizer state are counted. Dettmers 2023 So in early 2023 the largest open models could only be fine-tuned by people with a rack of A100s. The goal was to get the 65B model under 48 GB, the memory of one professional GPU, without losing quality (see Day 10 for quantization basics).

The key idea

Quantize the frozen weights to 4 bits, but keep the gradients and adapters in 16-bit, and dequantize on the fly only for the matrix multiply. Three pieces make this work. 4-bit NormalFloat (NF4) is a data type whose sixteen levels are placed at the quantiles of a normal distribution, which is the shape pretrained weights have; the paper shows this is information-theoretically optimal for such data and beats the plain 4-bit float used before. Double quantization quantizes the per-block scaling constants themselves from 32-bit to 8-bit, saving about 0.37 bits per parameter, roughly 3 GB on a 65B model. Paged optimizers use NVIDIA unified memory so optimizer states can spill to CPU RAM during the memory spikes caused by gradient checkpointing, instead of crashing with an out-of-memory error.

How it works, step by step

  1. Load the base model and cut each weight matrix into blocks of 64 values. Store every value as a 4-bit NF4 index plus one scale per block.
  2. Quantize the block scales themselves to 8-bit, with a second-level scale per 256 blocks (double quantization).
  3. Attach LoRA adapters in 16-bit to every linear layer, not only attention; Figure 2 shows this is needed to match full fine-tuning.
  4. Forward pass: for each layer, dequantize the 4-bit block to 16-bit, multiply, add the LoRA path, discard the dequantized copy.
  5. Backward pass: gradients flow through the dequantized weights into the adapters only; the 4-bit weights never change.
  6. Optimizer states live in paged memory and are evicted to CPU when the GPU fills.
  7. Fine-tune Guanaco models (7B to 65B) on the OASST1 conversation dataset; the 65B run takes 24 hours on one 48 GB GPU.

The memory arithmetic is the whole point. A 65B model in NF4 is about 33 GB of weights; the paper's Guanaco 65B fits in 41 GB with adapters and overhead, versus 130 GB just for 16-bit weights.

What the results showed

Two kinds of result: quality parity with 16-bit, and a chatbot built on the method. Vicuna-benchmark numbers are GPT-4 judgments relative to ChatGPT. Dettmers 2023

Model (Table 6)Memory, 4-bitVicuna score vs ChatGPT
Guanaco 65B41 GB99.3%
Guanaco 33B21 GB97.8%
Guanaco 13B10 GB90.4%
Guanaco 7B5 GB87.0%
Data type (Table 4, mean 5-shot MMLU, LLaMA 7B–65B)Score
BFloat16 (16-bit LoRA)53.0
NF4 + double quantization (QLoRA)53.1
FP4 (plain 4-bit float)52.2

In an Elo tournament judged by GPT-4 (Table 1), Guanaco 65B (1022) and 33B (992) ranked above ChatGPT (966), with GPT-4 itself far ahead at 1348. The authors fine-tuned more than a thousand models across eight instruction datasets, and two findings from that sweep are worth more than the leaderboard: dataset choice moved MMLU by 1.5 to 8 points while dataset size moved it by at most 0.5, and the dataset that was best for MMLU (FLAN v2) was the worst for chat quality, while 9,000 OASST1 conversations beat a 450,000-example FLAN v2 subset on the Vicuna benchmark.

Why it mattered

QLoRA is the reason "fine-tune a 30B or 70B model on one GPU" is a sentence that makes sense. Hugging Face shipped the integration the next day: 4-bit loading in bitsandbytes, LoRA via PEFT, a tutorial blog co-written by the authors. Belkada 2023 NF4 became the default 4-bit type for training in bitsandbytes. bitsandbytes docs Beyond the tooling, the paper's evaluation chapter is a quiet warning that Day 13 repeats: academic benchmarks and chat quality are different things, and a strong model judging outputs is a cheap, imperfect, but useful stand-in for people.

Limitations, and what came after

Dequantizing on every forward pass costs time, so QLoRA training is slower per step than 16-bit LoRA, and serving a 4-bit model well needs custom kernels. The parity claims were tested on MMLU-style tasks and chat; the paper itself flags that it did not test 33B and 65B at full 16-bit fine-tuning because that was too expensive. The Vicuna benchmark has only 80 prompts, and GPT-4-as-judge has known biases (the authors discuss order effects in Section 5.2). Later quantization methods target inference rather than training: GPTQ, which quantizes a trained model layer by layer with second-order information, is the usual companion. Frantar 2022 As of October 2026, QLoRA remains the standard way to adapt a large open-weights model on limited hardware, usually combined with a preference stage such as DPO.

How to read the actual paper

Figure 1 compares the memory footprint of full fine-tuning, LoRA and QLoRA in one picture. Section 3 (two pages) explains NF4, double quantization and paged optimizers; the NF4 construction is the only tricky part. Table 4 is the parity result, Table 6 the Guanaco results. Section 5.2 on evaluation and Section 6.2 and 8 on limitations are more honest than most papers and worth reading. Skip Section 2 if you have read the LoRA review. About 45 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. In QLoRA, which parts of the model are stored in 4-bit?

Answer 4. Adapters, gradients and the matrix multiplies are 16-bit; the frozen weights are dequantized on the fly from NF4.

Q2. What does "double quantization" quantize?

Answer 1. Each block of 64 weights has a 32-bit scale; storing those scales in 8-bit saves about 0.37 bits per parameter.

Q3. Which dataset gave the best chatbot quality in the paper's sweep?

Answer 2. Small but high-quality OASST1 won on the Vicuna benchmark, while FLAN v2 was best on MMLU and worst on chat, showing the two measure different things.

References

  1. QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs — Dettmers, Pagnoni, Holtzman, Zettlemoyer, University of Washington, NeurIPS 2023. The paper.
  2. NeurIPS 2023 proceedings entry — NeurIPS, 2023. The published version.
  3. artidoro/qlora — GitHub, 2023. Official code, including the Guanaco training scripts.
  4. Making LLMs even more accessible with bitsandbytes, 4-bit quantization and QLoRA — Belkada, Dettmers, Pagnoni, Gugger, Mangrulkar, Hugging Face blog, May 2023. The practical tutorial.
  5. bitsandbytes documentation — Hugging Face. The library that implements NF4, LLM.int8() and 8-bit optimizers.
  6. LLM.int8(): 8-bit Matrix Multiplication for Transformers at Scale — Dettmers et al., 2022. The authors' earlier 8-bit work and the origin of block-wise quantization in bitsandbytes.
  7. GPTQ: Accurate Post-Training Quantization for Generative Pre-trained Transformers — Frantar et al., 2022. The main alternative for inference-time 4-bit weights.
  8. LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models — Hu et al., ICLR 2022. The adapter method QLoRA builds on.
2024 · Mistral AI (background: Google Brain, 2021)

Mixtral of Experts (and Switch Transformer)

Put eight feed-forward "experts" in every layer, use two per token, and get a 47B-parameter model that runs at the cost of a 13B one and beats Llama 2 70B.

arXiv:2401.04088 · Jiang, Sablayrolles, Roux, Mensch and 22 others, Mistral AI · arXiv preprint, January 2024 · Background: arXiv:2101.03961, Fedus, Zoph, Shazeer, JMLR 2022

In one sentence

A sparse mixture-of-experts Transformer replaces each dense MLP with several MLPs and a tiny router that picks the best one or two for every token, so the total parameter count (what the model can know) grows many times while the compute per token (what it costs to run) barely changes; Switch Transformer showed this trains stably at trillion-parameter scale, and Mixtral turned it into a strong open-weights model.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine a hospital where every patient must see every doctor, even for a cold. That is a normal big model: every word goes through every part of the brain. A mixture-of-experts hospital works differently. At the front desk sits a receptionist who glances at each patient and sends them to the two most suitable doctors out of eight. The hospital can hire many specialists, so it knows a lot, but each patient only takes up two doctors' time, so it is fast and cheap. The catch is the receptionist: if she sends everyone to the same popular doctor, that queue explodes while the others sit idle. So the hospital gives her a small reward for spreading patients evenly. The Switch paper showed this works with huge numbers of doctors, even sending each patient to just one. Mixtral built a good, free-to-download hospital with eight doctors on every floor, two per patient, and it turned out to be better than a hospital with far more staff where everyone sees everyone.

The problem they wanted to solve

In a dense Transformer (Day 5) every parameter touches every token, so a model that knows more costs more per token, in training and in serving (Day 6). Mixture-of-experts layers break that link by activating only part of the network per token. The idea is old: a 2017 paper trained 137B-parameter sparsely gated models, Shazeer 2017 but the recipes were complicated, unstable in low precision and heavy on communication, Fedus 2021 and until late 2023 no strong MoE had open weights.

The key idea

Switch Transformer (2021) simplified routing to the extreme: send each token to a single expert (top-1), halving the expert capacity needed and simplifying communication. To keep the router from collapsing onto a few favorites, it adds a load-balancing loss, α·N·Σᵢ fᵢ·Pᵢ, where fᵢ is the fraction of tokens sent to expert i and Pᵢ the router's average probability for it, with α = 0.01. A capacity factor caps tokens per expert per batch; overflow tokens skip the expert via the residual connection. Training in bfloat16 diverged, so the router alone runs in float32, restoring stability at nearly bfloat16 speed. Fedus 2021

Mixtral 8x7B (2024) takes the Mistral 7B architecture and replaces every feed-forward block with eight SwiGLU experts plus a router that keeps the top two. For a token x the layer computes

y = Σᵢ softmax(top2(x·W_g))ᵢ · SwiGLUᵢ(x)

so each token sees 47B parameters but uses 13B. Attention is shared and dense; only the MLPs are sparse. Context is 32K and the weights are Apache 2.0. Jiang 2024

How it works, step by step

  1. A token's vector x enters the MoE layer after attention.
  2. The router, one small linear map W_g, produces eight logits for x.
  3. Keep the two largest logits, apply softmax over those two, and get two weights that sum to one.
  4. Run only the two chosen experts on x; the other six idle.
  5. Combine the two outputs with the weights and add the residual.
  6. In training, add the load-balancing loss; in serving, spread experts across GPUs, since every expert's weights must be resident even though only two run per token.

Try it: the widget routes a stream of tokens to the top-2 of 8 experts and keeps a running count of active versus total parameters.

Without balancing pressure a few experts would absorb most tokens and the rest would stay untrained.

What the results showed

Table 2 compares Mixtral with Llama 2 70B on one evaluation pipeline; Table 3 adds GPT-3.5 under different settings. Jiang 2024

Benchmark (Table 2)Mixtral 8x7BLlama 2 70B
MMLU (5-shot)70.669.9
HellaSwag84.485.4
ARC-Challenge59.756.5
GSM8K (8-shot, maj@8)74.469.6
MATH (4-shot, maj@4)28.413.8
HumanEval (0-shot)40.229.3
MBPP (3-shot)60.749.8
Active parameters per token13B70B

Against GPT-3.5 (Table 3) Mixtral is level on MMLU (70.6 vs 70.0) and ahead on GSM8K (58.4 vs 57.1, 5-shot) and MBPP (60.7 vs 52.2). The instruction-tuned Mixtral 8x7B-Instruct scored 8.30 on MT-Bench, the best open-weights result at the time. Mistral's release post adds that inference is about six times faster than Llama 2 70B. Mistral 2023

Switch Transformer's headline is sample efficiency: at equal FLOPs per token, Switch-Base with 64 experts reached T5-Base's quality about seven times faster, and the 1.6-trillion-parameter Switch-C (2,048 experts) reached T5-XXL's perplexity four times faster. Fedus 2021

Mixtral's Section 5 looks at what the router learned, and the answer is humbling: experts do not specialize by topic (arXiv, PubMed and philosophy texts route almost identically). Routing looks syntactic, with Python's self and indentation whitespace going to the same experts, and consecutive tokens repeat an expert far more often than chance (24 to 28% versus 12.5% in a middle layer).

Why it mattered

Mixtral settled an argument: MoE was the cheapest way to buy quality per unit of inference compute, and the weights were open so anyone could check. Within a year it was the default at the frontier. Mistral followed with Mixtral 8x22B (141B total, 39B active). Mistral 2024 DeepSeek pushed to fine-grained experts with a shared expert, Dai 2024 and DeepSeek-V3 scaled that to 671B total with 37B active, the base of DeepSeek-R1. DeepSeek 2024 Meta, which had deliberately kept Llama 3 dense, switched to MoE for Llama 4 in 2025. Meta 2025 Day 11 covers the design space.

Limitations, and what came after

Active parameters are not the whole cost. All 47B weights must be loaded, so Mixtral needs the memory of a 47B dense model (about 94 GB in 16-bit, an arithmetic estimate), so serving it cheaply means quantization or spreading experts across devices. Routing is discrete, so training is noisier and fine-tuning fiddlier, and the balancing loss trades a little quality for stability. The "experts" are not experts in any human sense, which makes the models harder to interpret. As of October 2026 nearly every frontier open-weights model is an MoE, and the dense 405B Llama 3 looks like the end of an era.

How to read the actual paper

Mixtral is short. Read Section 2.1 and Figure 1 for the routing equation, then Tables 2 and 3, then Section 5 on routing, which is the most surprising part. For Switch Transformer, read Section 2.1 and 2.2 (routing and the balancing loss), Figure 2 (the layer diagram), and Figures 4 and 5 (step and time speedups); skip Sections 4 and 5 unless you plan to train one. Together about 75 minutes.

Check yourself

Q1. How many parameters does Mixtral 8x7B use for each token?

Answer 2. Attention and embeddings are shared; the MoE layers use two experts per token. The name 8x7B is marketing: the total is 47B because attention is not duplicated.

Q2. Why does the Switch Transformer add a load-balancing loss?

Answer 4. Without balancing pressure, routing collapses; the auxiliary loss rewards even traffic across experts.

Q3. What did Mixtral's routing analysis find?

Answer 1. Document domains routed almost identically; tokens like Python's "self" clustered, and consecutive tokens often shared an expert.

References

  1. Mixtral of Experts — Jiang et al., Mistral AI, 2024. The paper; Section 5 is the part nobody expects.
  2. Switch Transformers: Scaling to Trillion Parameter Models with Simple and Efficient Sparsity — Fedus, Zoph, Shazeer, Google Brain, JMLR 2022 (arXiv 2021). The simplified MoE recipe Mixtral descends from.
  3. JMLR entry for Switch Transformers — JMLR 23(120):1–39, 2022. The journal version.
  4. Mixtral of experts (release post) — Mistral AI, December 2023. Headline claims, licensing and the inference-speed comparison.
  5. Outrageously Large Neural Networks: The Sparsely-Gated Mixture-of-Experts Layer — Shazeer et al., Google Brain, 2017. The modern origin of sparse MoE in deep learning.
  6. GShard: Scaling Giant Models with Conditional Computation and Automatic Sharding — Lepikhin et al., Google, 2020. The 600B MoE that preceded Switch.
  7. Mixture of Experts Explained — Sanseviero et al., Hugging Face blog, December 2023. The best tutorial-length overview, written the week Mixtral came out.
  8. DeepSeekMoE: Towards Ultimate Expert Specialization in Mixture-of-Experts Language Models — Dai et al., DeepSeek, 2024. Fine-grained and shared experts, the design that scaled to DeepSeek-V3.
2025 · DeepSeek-AI

DeepSeek-R1: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability in LLMs via Reinforcement Learning

Showed that reinforcement learning with simple correctness rewards, and no human-written reasoning examples, teaches a model to think at length, check itself, and match OpenAI's o1, with open weights.

arXiv:2501.12948 · DeepSeek-AI, Guo et al. (200 authors) · Nature 645, 633–638, 17 September 2025

In one sentence

Reward DeepSeek-V3-Base only for correct, well-formatted final answers, optimize with GRPO, and it teaches itself long chains of thought with reflection and self-verification, lifting AIME 2024 from 15.6% to 77.9%; add a small cold-start set and two more stages for readability, and the result, DeepSeek-R1, scores 79.8% on AIME and 97.3% on MATH-500, level with OpenAI's o1.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Imagine teaching a child to solve puzzles, but you are not allowed to show them how. You can only say "right" or "wrong" after each attempt, plus "please write your answer neatly in the box." At first the child guesses quickly and is wrong most of the time. But every time a longer, more careful attempt happens to be right, you say "right," and the child notices: being careful pays. Over thousands of puzzles the child starts writing more, checking their own steps, going back when something looks off, even saying "wait, let me try another way," all by themselves. Nobody taught those habits; they grew because they led to more "rights." That is what this paper did with a computer, and the computer got as good at hard maths as the best secret models. The first version had a problem: its notes were messy and mixed two languages. So the team first showed it a small number of tidy examples, then trained again, and the result was both clever and readable. Then they gave it away for free.

The problem they wanted to solve

In September 2024 OpenAI's o1 showed that a model trained to think before answering could jump from 12% to 74% on the AIME math competition, and that accuracy kept rising with more thinking time. OpenAI 2024 The recipe was secret. Open research had tried process reward models that score each step, Lightman 2023 tree search, and fine-tuning on big sets of reasoning traces. DeepSeek asked a cleaner question: can reasoning emerge from reinforcement learning alone, with no human-written reasoning examples and only verifiable outcome rewards? (Day 9)

The key idea

Two choices keep the method simple. The optimizer is GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) from DeepSeekMath: for each question, sample a group of answers, score each, and use its reward minus the group mean, divided by the group's standard deviation, as its advantage. Shao 2024 There is no value network, which saves a policy-sized model's memory. The rewards are rule-based: an accuracy reward (does the boxed answer match, does the code pass the tests) and a format reward (is the thinking inside <think> tags). No neural reward model is used for reasoning, because the authors found such models get gamed. With only this signal, R1-Zero developed longer responses, self-verification and reflection, including the "aha moment" where it interrupts itself mid-solution to re-examine its approach. DeepSeek-AI 2025

How it works, step by step

  1. R1-Zero. Apply GRPO with accuracy and format rewards directly to DeepSeek-V3-Base (671B total, 37B active). AIME 2024 pass@1 climbs from 15.6% to 77.9% (86.7% with 16-sample majority voting), but outputs are hard to read and mix languages.
  2. Cold start. Collect a few thousand long, readable chain-of-thought examples and fine-tune V3-Base on them, so RL starts from a model that already writes tidy reasoning.
  3. Reasoning RL. Repeat the R1-Zero procedure from the cold-start model, adding a language-consistency reward against mixed-language chains.
  4. Rejection sampling and SFT. Sample many answers from the RL checkpoint, keep correct and readable ones (about 600k), add about 200k non-reasoning samples (writing, QA, translation), and fine-tune V3-Base on all 800k for two epochs.
  5. RL for all scenarios. A final RL stage mixes rule-based reasoning rewards with helpfulness and harmlessness reward models on general prompts. This is DeepSeek-R1.
  6. Distillation. Fine-tune Qwen2.5 and Llama 3 models (1.5B to 70B) on the same 800k samples, with no RL.

Try it: the widget shows an illustrative accuracy-versus-thinking-tokens curve; the paper's Figure 1 shows the real one, with response length and accuracy growing together during R1-Zero training.

More thinking tokens buy accuracy with diminishing returns, which is why the paper lists overthinking as a limitation.

What the results showed

Table 4 of the January 2025 preprint puts R1 beside OpenAI's o1 models and the strongest non-reasoning models of the time. DeepSeek-AI 2025

BenchmarkDeepSeek-R1OpenAI o1-1217o1-miniDeepSeek-V3GPT-4o-0513
AIME 2024 (pass@1)79.879.263.639.29.3
MATH-500 (pass@1)97.396.490.090.274.6
GPQA Diamond (pass@1)71.575.760.059.149.9
Codeforces rating2029206118201134759
SWE-bench Verified (resolved)49.248.941.642.038.8

The distilled models were almost as striking: DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-32B scored 72.6 on AIME and 94.3 on MATH-500, above o1-mini (63.6 and 90.0). Running the same RL directly on a 32B base reached only 47.0 on AIME, so for small models reasoning transfers better through data than through RL. One number changed between versions: the preprint reported 71.0% for R1-Zero on AIME; the Nature version reports 77.9%. Guo 2025

Why it mattered

R1 was the first open-weights model to match o1, released under an MIT license with the full recipe, Hugging Face and it made "RL with verifiable rewards" the standard way to build reasoning models through 2025 and 2026. It showed that reflective, self-correcting reasoning need not be hand-written; it is what a model converges to when rewarded only for being right. The distilled models put real math and code reasoning on laptops. And in September 2025 it was published in Nature after formal peer review, reported to be a first for a major LLM. Guo 2025 Day 14 follows what came next.

Limitations, and what came after

The paper's own list is long: R1 is weaker than V3 at structured output and tool use; it overthinks easy questions; it mixes languages other than Chinese and English; few-shot prompting makes it worse; gains on software engineering were small because those rewards are slow to evaluate; and learned reward models for uncheckable tasks get gamed. The appendix on unsuccessful attempts explains why process reward models and tree search were abandoned, which saved other labs a detour. The method also needs verifiable tasks, which math and code provide and most of life does not. As of October 2026 the open questions are how to reward unverifiable work, how to keep reasoning traces faithful, and how to spend thinking tokens only where they pay.

How to read the actual paper

Read Section 2.2 (reward design) and 2.3 with Figure 1, the training curves for accuracy and response length, and the "aha moment" table; that is the heart of the paper and takes twenty minutes. Then Section 3 and the pipeline figure for how R1 differs from R1-Zero, the main comparison table, and Section 6 for limitations. The appendix on unsuccessful attempts is the most useful page for anyone planning their own experiments. The Nature version is shorter (eleven pages); the arXiv version is easier to read end to end. About an hour.

Check yourself

Q1. What reward signal trained DeepSeek-R1-Zero?

Answer 3. Accuracy and format rewards only. The authors avoided neural reward models for reasoning because they were gamed during RL.

Q2. Why did the authors add cold-start data before RL for DeepSeek-R1?

Answer 1. R1-Zero proved RL alone works; the cold start fixes readability and gives RL a better starting distribution.

Q3. How were the small DeepSeek-R1-Distill models made?

Answer 4. Distillation through R1's samples worked better than applying RL directly to small models, according to the paper's comparison.

References

  1. DeepSeek-R1: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability in LLMs via Reinforcement Learning — DeepSeek-AI, 2025 (v2, January 2026). The arXiv version with the full appendix.
  2. DeepSeek-R1 incentivizes reasoning in LLMs through reinforcement learning — Guo et al., Nature 645, 633–638, September 2025. The peer-reviewed version.
  3. DeepSeekMath: Pushing the Limits of Mathematical Reasoning in Open Language Models — Shao et al., DeepSeek, 2024. Introduces GRPO, the optimizer R1 uses.
  4. DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report — DeepSeek-AI, 2024. The 671B mixture-of-experts base model R1 starts from.
  5. deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-R1 model card — Hugging Face, 2025. Weights, license and the six distilled models.
  6. Learning to reason with LLMs — OpenAI, September 2024. The o1 announcement that R1 set out to match.
  7. Let's Verify Step by Step — Lightman et al., OpenAI, 2023. The process-reward-model approach that R1's appendix explains it abandoned.
2024 · Meta AI

The Llama 3 Herd of Models

The most detailed public recipe for training a frontier-class dense model: 405B parameters, 15.6 trillion tokens, 16,000 H100 GPUs, and a post-training loop of SFT, rejection sampling and DPO.

arXiv:2407.21783 · Meta AI, Grattafiori, Dubey, Jauhri and 556 others · arXiv technical report, July 2024

In one sentence

Meta scaled a deliberately plain dense Transformer with three levers, data quality, compute-optimal scale at 3.8×10²⁵ FLOPs, and managing complexity (no mixture of experts, DPO instead of PPO), and released weights that match GPT-4-class models on many benchmarks.

Explain it to a 10-year-old

Suppose you want to bake the biggest, best cake anyone has ever made, and then give the recipe away for free so anyone can bake it. This paper is that recipe book, written in enormous detail. The team made three big choices. First, ingredients: they gathered a mountain of text, about fifteen trillion word pieces, threw out the junk, and kept plenty of maths, code and other languages. Second, size: they used a rule learned from smaller test cakes to pick exactly how big a cake to bake for the oven time they had: sixteen thousand fast chips running for months. Third, keep it simple: no fancy tricks that might make the cake collapse, just a plain, well-tested design, because when the oven costs this much, a collapse is a disaster. Then they taught the finished cake to be polite and useful by showing it good examples and asking people which of two answers they liked better, six times over. The result was about as good as the best secret cakes, and anyone could download it.

The problem they wanted to solve

Through 2023 the best open-weights models (Llama 2 70B, then Mixtral) trailed GPT-4 by a wide margin, and frontier recipes were secret. Meta wanted a downloadable model competitive with GPT-4, and a report complete enough to explain the choices: data, scaling, infrastructure, post-training and safety, plus 128K context, eight languages, tool use and multimodality. Meta 2024

The key idea

The introduction names three levers. Data: about 15 trillion multilingual tokens, mixed roughly 50% general knowledge, 25% math and reasoning, 17% code and 8% multilingual. Scale: a scaling law fitted on smaller runs, N*(C) = A·C^α with α = 0.53 and A = 0.29, predicts about 402B parameters on 16.55T tokens at 3.8×10²⁵ FLOPs; the team chose 405B and 15.6T, "approximately compute-optimal" in the Chinchilla sense (Day 6). Managing complexity: a dense model rather than a mixture of experts to maximize training stability, and a post-training recipe built on supervised fine-tuning, rejection sampling and DPO rather than PPO, which was cheaper and worked better at scale.

Architecture (Table 3)8B70B405B
Layers3280126
Model dimension4,0968,19216,384
FFN dimension14,33628,67253,248
Attention heads / KV heads32 / 864 / 8128 / 8
SharedSwiGLU, 128K-token vocabulary, RoPE with base frequency 500,000, grouped-query attention

Grouped-query attention (8 key-value heads shared by all query heads) shrinks the KV cache that dominates inference memory (Day 7). Ainslie 2023

How it works, step by step

  1. Curate data. Crawl, strip boilerplate, deduplicate at URL, document and line level, filter with heuristics and quality classifiers, and tune the mix on small models.
  2. Choose the size from the scaling-law fit above, checking that loss is flat near the optimum.
  3. Pretrain at 8K context on most of the 15.6T tokens, continue at 128K, then anneal: learning rate to zero over the last 40 million tokens with high-quality data upsampled, and average the final checkpoints.
  4. Run the cluster. Up to 16,000 H100s with 4D parallelism (tensor, pipeline, context, data), 38 to 43% model-FLOPs utilization in BF16, and over 90% effective training time despite 466 job interruptions in one 54-day window, 78% of them hardware.
  5. Post-train in six rounds: train a reward model on human preference pairs, build SFT data partly by rejection sampling with that reward model, fine-tune, then run DPO (β = 0.1, plus a small likelihood term on the chosen answer).
  6. Add capabilities with targeted data (code, multilinguality, math, long context, tool use, factuality) and ship Llama Guard 3 for safety.
  7. Serve with FP8 quantization and pipeline parallelism; 405B weights in BF16 are about 810 GB (arithmetic estimate), so the model spans many GPUs.
  8. Go multimodal compositionally: pretrain image and speech encoders separately, then train adapters into the frozen language model.

What the results showed

Table 2 of the paper compares the post-trained models with the strongest closed models of mid-2024. Meta 2024

BenchmarkLlama 3 8BLlama 3 70BLlama 3 405BGPT-4 (0125)GPT-4oClaude 3.5 Sonnet
MMLU (5-shot)69.483.687.385.189.189.9
MMLU-Pro (5-shot, CoT)48.366.473.364.874.077.0
HumanEval (0-shot)72.680.589.086.690.292.0
GSM8K (8-shot, CoT)84.595.196.894.296.196.4
MATH (0-shot, CoT)51.968.073.864.576.671.1
GPQA (0-shot, CoT)32.846.751.141.453.659.4

The 405B model beats early-2024 GPT-4 on every row and trails GPT-4o and Claude 3.5 Sonnet on several: that is what "on par with GPT-4" meant in July 2024. Training took 30.84 million H100 hours for 405B, 39.3 million across the three sizes. Meta model card One smaller finding: annealing on high-quality data raised the 8B model's GSM8K by 24% but did nothing measurable for 405B.

Why it mattered

Llama 3.1 405B was the first open-weights model at GPT-4 level, released on 23 July 2024 with a license that, for the first time, allowed using its outputs to train other models. Meta 2024b That clause mattered: DeepSeek-R1's distilled 70B model is a Llama 3 fine-tune. The paper is also the closest thing to a textbook on frontier training: failure statistics, the scaling-law fit, the post-training loop and the data mix were all new in public. And it validated DPO at scale, which pushed the open community away from PPO (Day 8).

Limitations, and what came after

A dense 405B model is expensive to serve, and most users ran the 8B and 70B. Data sources are described only as "publicly available", so the most important ingredient is the least reproducible. The multimodal models of Sections 7 and 8 were not released. Benchmarks may overlap with training data; the paper discusses contamination but cannot rule it out (Day 13). The biggest change came from Meta itself: Llama 4, in April 2025, dropped "dense for stability" for a mixture-of-experts design with native multimodality. Meta 2025 As of October 2026 the frontier open models are sparse and reasoning-trained; Llama 3 reads as the high-water mark of the plain dense recipe, documented well enough to learn from.

How to read the actual paper

It is 92 pages; do not read it in order. Section 1 (three pages) states the three levers. Section 3.1 on data and 3.2.1 on scaling laws are the parts you cannot get elsewhere; Table 3 is the architecture. Section 3.3 on infrastructure is worth it for the failure statistics alone. Section 4.1 is the post-training loop. Then Table 2 for results. Skim Section 4.3 (capabilities) by heading and skip Sections 7 and 8 (vision, speech) unless interested. Two to three hours for the parts above.

Check yourself

Q1. Why did the Llama 3 team choose a dense model over a mixture of experts?

Answer 2. "Managing complexity" is one of the three levers; a dense Transformer was the safer bet for a run that cost tens of millions of GPU hours. Meta switched to MoE for Llama 4.

Q2. Which preference-optimization method did Llama 3 use in post-training?

Answer 4. The team reports DPO needed less compute than PPO at this scale and did better on instruction following; GRPO is DeepSeek's method.

Q3. Roughly how much text was the 405B model pretrained on?

Answer 1. 15.6T tokens, close to the scaling-law prediction of 16.55T; Llama 2 had used 2T and GPT-3 about 300B.

References

  1. The Llama 3 Herd of Models — Meta AI, 2024. The 92-page report; see the reading guide above.
  2. Introducing Llama 3.1: Our most capable models to date — Meta, July 2024. The release post with the licensing change.
  3. Llama 3.1 model card — Meta, GitHub, 2024. GPU hours, training data size and intended use.
  4. Llama 2: Open Foundation and Fine-Tuned Chat Models — Touvron et al., Meta, 2023. The predecessor; compare its 2T tokens and PPO-based RLHF.
  5. Training Compute-Optimal Large Language Models — Hoffmann et al., DeepMind, 2022. The Chinchilla scaling analysis Llama 3's size choice extends.
  6. GQA: Training Generalized Multi-Query Transformer Models from Multi-Head Checkpoints — Ainslie et al., Google, 2023. The grouped-query attention Llama 3 uses at every size.
  7. Direct Preference Optimization: Your Language Model is Secretly a Reward Model — Rafailov et al., NeurIPS 2023. The preference method Llama 3 chose over PPO.
  8. The Llama 4 herd — Meta, April 2025. The successor, and Meta's switch to mixture of experts.
